From Grassroots Contract to Grassroots App
(Full Version)
JAMES GOLIKE, London School of Economics and Political Science, United Kingdom
ANDY LEWIS-PYE, London School of Economics and Political Science, United Kingdom
EHUD SHAPIRO, London School of Economics and Political Science, United Kingdom and Weizmann
Institute of Science, Israel
NIMROD TALMON, Ben-Gurion University, Israel
Almost two centuries ago Pierre-Joseph Proudhon envisioned a social contract that is an agreement of man
with man; reciprocal; imposing no obligation upon the parties except that which results from their personal
promise; subject to no external authority; freely accepted and signed by all the participants; and of the nature
of a contract of exchange. He also envisioned that groups formed by a like contract could, by a similar contract,
agree with every and all other groups; digitally, that independent platform instances may coalesce by mutual
consent. Here we define grassroots contracts as social contracts meeting Proudhon’s six conditions and two
more: people are free to deal with each other, and there is no external register of people. We show that a
grassroots contract can be transformed from its natural language presentation into a working smartphone
grassroots app through an abstraction cascade: the contract’s text is rendered as speech-act schemas, first-order
universally-quantified implications guarded by the persons who must will the act, each denoting volition-
guarded transactions among agents, a person with their will and a machine with its state, verified syntactically
to be grassroots by three decidable conditions, Introduction, Provenance and Volition. Speech-act schemas are
a restricted surface syntax for volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions, the cascade from which to
a working app has already been described. We prove that the protocol realising a syntactically grassroots
contract is grassroots, and volitionally so: two instances coalesce only when a member of each wills it. We
distinguish three legal modalities of clauses, enforced, attested and undertaken, by whether an agent can breach
a clause and what non-repudiable evidence of the breach the contract leaves, and illustrate them with a social
network and with grassroots coins. Contracts are amended, extended, restricted, instantiated and implemented,
and the check of an amended contract is confined to what it adds. A checker of the three conditions, written
in Grassroots Logic Programs, accompanies the paper.
Additional Key Words and Phrases: grassroots contract, speech-act schema, compilation, legal modalities,
provenance, contract specification language, norm-governed systems, contract formation

1   INTRODUCTION
Almost two centuries ago Pierre-Joseph Proudhon envisioned a social contract [65, Fourth Study]
that is (1) an agreement of man with man; (2) reciprocal; (3) imposing no obligation upon the parties
except that which results from their personal promise; (4) subject to no external authority; (5) freely
accepted and signed by all the participants; (6) of the nature of a contract of exchange; and a few
other conditions, among them that it include all citizens with their interests and that it increase
the well-being and liberty of every citizen. In his words, the social contract is “an agreement of
man with man”; “essentially reciprocal: it imposes no obligation upon the parties, except that
which results from their personal promise of reciprocal delivery: it is not subject to any external
authority: it alone forms the law between the parties: it awaits their initiative for its execution”;
“freely discussed, individually accepted, signed with their own hands, by all the participants”; and
“of the nature of a contract of exchange: not only does it leave the party free, it adds to his liberty;
not only does it leave him all his goods, it adds to his property; it prescribes no labor; it bears only
Authors’ addresses: James Golike, London School of Economics and Political Science, London, United Kingdom; Andy
Lewis-Pye, London School of Economics and Political Science, London, United Kingdom; Ehud Shapiro, London School of
Economics and Political Science, London, United Kingdom and Weizmann Institute of Science, Rehovot, Israel; Nimrod
Talmon, Ben-Gurion University, Beer Sheba, Israel.
2                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


upon exchange”. He also envisioned a property of social contracts [65, Sixth Study], analogous to a
property of digital platforms we term grassroots [74, 76, 77]: that (i) one could “make a contract
with all, as . . . with some”; digitally, that a grassroots platform can have multiple instances, and (ii)
“each group of citizens . . . formed by a like contract . . . could thereafter, and always by a similar
contract, agree with every and all other groups”; digitally, that independent platform instances may
coalesce by mutual consent, possibly into a single global platform instance. In full: “if I could make
a contract with all, as I can with some; if all could renew it among themselves, if each group of
citizens, as a town, county, province, corporation, company, &c., formed by a like contract, and
considered as a moral person, could thereafter, and always by a similar contract, agree with every
and all other groups, it would be the same as if my own will were multiplied to infinity. I should be
sure that the law thus made on all questions in the Republic, from millions of different initiatives,
would never be anything but my law; and if this new order of things were called government, it
would be my government.” We proposed grassroots platforms owned, operated and governed by
their participants as a foundation for an equitable and democratic digital realm [76].
   A digital social contract is one realised by software the agents run [12]. We call the mathematical
object a protocol and the software implementing it a platform. A protocol or a platform realises a
contract, a contract being an informal text; one abstraction of the cascade implements another, and
implementations compose, so each implements every abstraction above it [47, 72]. A protocol is
grassroots when its instances are independent of one another and of every global resource but the
net, and can always coalesce, possibly into a single global instance [74, 76, 77], and a grassroots
platform is the software implementing a grassroots protocol. The grassroots notion is a formal
property of a protocol, recalled in Section 4.
   Here we define grassroots contracts as social contracts meeting Proudhon’s six conditions above
and that (7) people are free to deal with each other; and (8) there is no external register of people,
so people can come to know each other either directly or through people they already know. Our
main theorem is that the protocol realising such a contract, written as speech-act schemas and
meeting three decidable conditions, is grassroots: any two agents may come into relation without
anyone’s permission, and two instances formed independently coexist and may later coalesce.
   A social contract is a contract among those who adopt it, and a grassroots contract is a social
contract that runs: it is realised by software its agents run on their own phones. Participating in a
grassroots platform requires adopting the grassroots contract that defines it, as participating in a
global platform requires adopting an End-User Licence Agreement (EULA). A grassroots contract is
mutual and symmetric among all participants; an EULA is between each person and the platform
operator. A friendship or a mutual credit line is an act under the contract between particular
agents; a friendship under GSG is mutual trust, so its social graph is a web of trust. We show that
GSN, a contract for a social network [75], is grassroots, and GC, a contract for personal coins [73].
Not every social contract is. Examples that are not include a contract without coalescences, not
allowing two strangers to come into relation; a contract with named third-party control, e.g. where
introduction requires a token only a third party can make; and a contract with an act that changes
a stranger’s state without their will and requires no prior relation between the two; each fails one
of the conditions.
   We show that a grassroots contract can be transformed into a working smartphone-based
grassroots platform through an abstraction cascade [47], starting from converting the social contract
text to formal speech-act schemas, verified syntactically to be grassroots, and then to volition-
guarded multiagent atomic transactions [51], hitherto the most abstract formalism used to specify
grassroots platforms. The abstraction cascade from there to a working app has been described
elsewhere [47]. A grassroots contract has two artefacts: a legal text, and a protocol realising it.
A clause of the text describes an act, in the sense of a juridical act: a declaration by one or more
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                  3


agents intended to create a legal effect. Each agent takes it as a digital speech act [32]: a speech
act [4, 70, 71] performed digitally, by signing a text with their private key, as GKK:1 says. An
agent is a person and a machine; the person has a will, the machine a state. We wish to specify the
transactions among agents, which change the machines’ states and are guarded by the persons’
wills. Speech-act schemas specify them: each is a first-order universally-quantified implication, its
body what the act requires of the machines, its head what it changes, guarded by the persons
who must will it, and denotes a set of volition-guarded transactions, with the agents determined
upon signature. They are a restricted surface syntax for volition-guarded transactions [51], whose
semantics is a transition system per set of agents, with correct runs, safe and live. The key property
is grassroots, which a contract of speech-act schemas satisfies when it passes a syntactic check: it
meets the eight conditions above, provided it is syntactically grassroots, satisfying: (i) Introduction,
that some act can introduce any two willing agents, following which the state of each stores an
identifier of the other, as of a person they know, a friend, or the issuer of a coin they hold, and
we say that it records the other; (ii) Provenance, that the state of an agent stores an identifier of
an agent only by an act with that agent, or with an agent whose state stores that identifier; and
(iii) Volition, that an act requires the consent of an agent in each connected part of the undirected
graph formed by identifiers stored by its agents, unless that graph is connected. Conditions (1),
(2) and (4) hold of every contract written as speech-act schemas, since every participant in an
act takes a role in it and the schemas single out no one, and so does (5), since nobody is bound
who has not adopted the contract (Section 3.7); (3) is Volition, (6) and (7) are Introduction, and
(8) is Provenance (Section 3.8). Introduction is of one clause relative to all, Provenance is over all
clauses, and Volition is per clause, given Provenance. The three are decidable, and a checker for
them accompanies this paper, at https://github.com/EShapiro2/GLP/tree/main/programs/gsc; it
certifies both of the contracts we carry through. A schema denotes volition-guarded transactions,
and a contract the protocol over them, defined for contracts meeting the conditions.
    We prove that the protocol realising a syntactically grassroots contract is grassroots, and vo-
litionally so (Theorem 15). To do so, we first prove that politeness — any two agents can always
come to interact, and no act joins two instances unless each of them holds a guard of it — is
sufficient for the protocol over a contained set of volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions
to be grassroots, and volitionally so: two instances coalesce only when a member of each wills it
(Theorem 26). We then prove that a syntactically grassroots contract denotes a contained, polite set
of volition-guarded transactions (Section 7).
    We distinguish three legal modalities of the clauses of a grassroots contract (Section 10), by
whether an agent can breach a clause and what record of it the contract leaves in another agent’s
state: a clause is enforced, if performing it is an act of the contract and every correct run of it
keeps the clause; attested, if an agent may breach it, with the contract recording the breach; or
undertaken, if the contract records who gave the undertaking, and performing it is the person’s,
not the machine’s. A breach of an enforced clause is impossible with a correct realisation of the
contract; a breach of an attested or undertaken clause can be taken to court, with non-repudiable
evidence attributable to the obligor of the act alleged to constitute the breach, in the case of an
attested clause. We illustrate the three modalities with the grassroots social network [75], and with
grassroots coins [73]. The semantics and the conditions are algorithmic, so the only informal step
in this paper is the first: rendering a legal text as schemas.
    Contracts are written from other contracts, as GSN is from GSG and GSG from GKK. We define
five relations between contracts (Section 9): amendment, which deletes and adds clauses, extension,
also at a new argument of some predicates, and instantiation of a parametric contract, on their
schemas, and implementation and restriction, on their runs. Restriction is undecidable, and we give
syntactic conditions that imply it, a set we expect to grow. We prove what the check preserves: an
4                                               James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


amendment of a certified contract is certified by checking the clauses it adds and the introductory
act, one that strengthens the bodies of clauses and deletes clauses by checking the introductory act,
and an instance of a parametric contract is certified with it. An undertaken clause may say what its
obligor undertakes as a formula over runs, that a condition eventually leads to a response, kept or
breached by the person and not by the machines (Section 10); and the language is Turing-complete,
a counter being the multiplicity of an atom (Section 3).
   We carry two contracts through, a social network and personal coins, and certify both from
their schemas. A coin can be handed on, so a holding may record an agent its holder has never
transacted with; the certification then rests on traceable provenance, and conservation of money is
its special case.
Three languages. The paper employs three languages: natural language for the social contract;
speech-act schemas; and volition-guarded atomic transactions. Rendering the first as the second
is by judgement; the second denotes the third; and the conditions of the main theorem, that a
syntactically grassroots contract is grassroots, are verified in the second. GSG:1, of the social graph
(Section 2.2), reads “Alice and Bob, who trust each other and are not friends, may jointly decide to
become friends”. Its schema (Section 3.4) is a clause: its head is what the act adds and deletes, its
body what it requires and forbids, the holder of each atom its first argument, and its guard names
who must will it:
        befriend : friend (Alice, Bob), friend (Bob, Alice) ←
              identity(Alice), knows(Alice, Bob), ¬friend (Alice, Bob),
              identity(Bob), knows(Bob, Alice), ¬friend (Bob, Alice) guarded by Alice, Bob.
It denotes (Section 3.3) the volition-guarded transaction (𝑑 → 𝑑 ′, {Alice, Bob}), for every 𝑑 with
identity, knows(Bob) ∈ 𝑑 Alice , identity, knows(Alice) ∈ 𝑑 Bob , friend (Bob) ∉ 𝑑 Alice and friend (Alice) ∉
                ′
𝑑 Bob , where 𝑑 Alice                                  ′
                      = 𝑑 Alice ⊎ {friend (Bob)} and 𝑑 Bob = 𝑑 Bob ⊎ {friend (Alice)}. The rest of the paper
concerns the middle line. The abstraction cascade. The passage from a legal text to a running
platform goes through the abstraction cascade [47] set out in Figure 1; this paper provides (1)→(3).
A compiler carries the grassroots social graph and grassroots currencies [73] from their volition-
guarded transactions down the cascade to Grassroots Logic Programs that run with scripted
persons, and earlier programs of the social graph, the grassroots social network [75] and the
currencies run as mini-apps of one app, deployed on a physical smartphone [47]. The screens of
Figures 2 to 5 are screenshots of the GKK, GSG, GSN and GC mini-apps, each compiled from its
social contract down the abstraction cascade; one instance of the grassroots app on an iPhone
simulator runs the mini-app of every agent, the agents communicate over channels within it, and
a screenshot shows one person’s screen. All source code and all artefacts, including the social
contracts and the artefacts the abstraction cascade produces from them, are in the public repository
https://github.com/EShapiro2/GLP. GAS, the GLP app store in the same repository, makes an app of
every contract the repository records down to its platform, and runs it with each person’s agent on
their own device; GKK has run between a laptop and a phone near each other, each running its own
agent, with no server between them. A child-safe social network [79] is specified the same way. The
Human–Science–AI methodology. The formal objects of the cascade are its abstractions and the
implementations among them, each constructed, maintained, evolved and proved correct with AI, in
tandem with the paper describing it. The methodology has two kinds of artefact: papers written for
competitive peer review, and code. The papers serve as an evolving and cooperatively-constructed
interface between us humans and AI, recording our shared understanding of the subject matter,
and are the source of authority for coding. Papers are written by people and reviewed, revised and
extended with the help of AI; the code, with few exceptions, is written by AI; and both evolve in the
process of harmonising them. AI codes down the cascade, from paper to tested code, and every gap
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                   5


coding finds is repaired in the paper before it is repaired in the code. It is at the antipode of “vibe
coding”, where a person describes the intended behaviour informally and AI guesses the rest [67].
Spec-driven development makes the specification the primary artefact, maintained by people while
the code is regenerated from it [26, 30]; here that artefact is a scientific paper, and the feedback from
coding revises it. The AI is Claude, Anthropic’s models, among them Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude
Fable 5.1. Through Claude Code it wrote, each from the paper that specifies it, the checker and the
compiler of speech-act schemas, the compilers of the cascade below them, the apps of Figures 2 to 5,
GAS, and the tests that run the apps and draw their screens; and it revised and extended the text
of this paper, its definitions and proofs included, at the authors’ direction. Relation to contract
specification languages. Formalising legal text has a large literature, on contracts as automata, as
defeasible deontic rules and as domain-specific languages, and on statute as code, and Section 12
reviews it. Two languages formalise legal contracts in a way close enough to ours to say plainly
how they differ. Symboleo [63, 85] specifies a contract as obligations and powers with statechart
lifetimes, for verification and for monitoring; Stipula [16] is a domain-specific language whose
contracts are state machines over parties, fields and linear assets, with an agreement operator and
a formal semantics. Both bind their parties at contract formation and both keep one global contract
state. Our question is about the family of systems a contract induces over every finite set of people,
and about what happens when two instances that formed independently meet; it cannot be posed
in either language, and each requires a global resource that closure forbids. Section 12 compares
the two languages, together with the literature on norms and on contract languages more broadly.
Structure. Section 2 presents the contracts and Section 3 the syntax of speech-act schemas, with
listed roles, by which a leader closes a group with all its members, and cleared atoms, by which
ending a friendship ends every membership between the two, and the contracts’ schemas. Section 4
recalls what is needed of [51] and Section 5 states the protocol a contract denotes. Section 6 defines
politeness and proves it sufficient for a protocol to be grassroots and volitionally grassroots, and
Section 7 states the conditions on the schemas and certifies the contract from them. Section 9
defines amendment, extension, instantiation, implementation and restriction of contracts and what
the check preserves. Section 10 explains the legal modalities of contract clauses, Section 11 how
an agent enters the contract and what the code they run warrants, and Section 12 reviews related
work. Appendix A states the framework of [51] formally, citing its proofs; Appendix B holds the
proofs; Appendix C defines the values speech-act schemas allow; and Appendix D expands the
related work.

2   GRASSROOTS CONTRACTS
A grassroots contract is a finite list of clauses its agents undertake towards one another. A clause
either describes an act, which the protocol realising the contract carries out, or binds the agents
without describing one. Each contract is in a box of its own, which holds, just before its clauses, a
declaration of the contract’s names, saying what each names, as “Alice, Bob and Carol are persons.”;
the declaration is part of the contract, which the schemas and the pragmas take their names from.
A contract is self-contained: a definition that is part of it is in the box, before the declaration, as
GSG defines trust; a definition outside the box is a formal definition, which the box cites explicitly.
Two names are two persons; a clause that admits one person in both writes “Alice and Bob, possibly
one person”. Formally, contract variables are universally quantified per clause. A clause is cited by
the contract’s name and its number, as GSG:1; a contract that extends another cites its clauses and
does not restate them. A contract written from others says so in its first line, after its title, by the
relations of Section 9: each relation is followed by the contract it is to, or by ‘it’ for the contract
named last, a composition written ‘composes A with B’ or ‘composes it with B’, and named ‘the
6                                                         James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon



    (1) The legal text. The clauses a grassroots contract’s agents undertake towards one another,
    each describing an act or binding them without one.
                                                                  [informal]

    (2) Speech-act schemas. A formal language for those acts: a schema is a clause, its body an
    act’s precondition at each agent, its head its effect, guarded by the agents who must will it, the
    agents being filled in on signature.
                                                                  [this paper]

    (3) Volition-guarded multiagent atomic transactions [51]. The most abstract formalism used
    to specify grassroots platforms.
                                                                  [47]

    (4) Communicating volitional agents [47]. An implementation-ready restriction of the former in which the only non-
    unary transactions are agent discovery, agent introduction and message delivery.
                                                                  [47]

    (5) Volition-guarded Grassroots Logic Programs [47], GLP (6) extended with volitional procedures, whose interactive
    types determine the platform’s user interface; its compiler also takes the contract’s pragmas, which name the app’s tabs,
    buttons and questions.
                                                                  [47]

    (6) Grassroots Logic Programs [78, 83, 84], a multiagent, concurrent, polymorphically typed logic programming language,
    designed for the implementation of grassroots platforms by AI.
                                                                  [83]

    (7) Dart and Flutter [33], a programming language for smartphone applications, deployable on iPhone and Android, with
    Flutter for the user interface of both.
                                                                  [33]

    (8) The grassroots app. One app on each person’s own phone, each platform it runs a mini-app of it.


Fig. 1. The abstraction cascade, from a legal text to a running platform. The framed (1)–(3) are this paper.
The first arrow, the realisation of the legal text, is left to judgement, and the second is the semantics of this
paper. Each arrow from (3) to (6) is an implementation proved correct in the reference it carries, and they
compose [47]; the GLP engine in Dart and the app are built and tested, and not proved. A condition on the
transactions of (3), politeness, is sufficient for the platform they specify to be a grassroots platform, and
conditions on the schemas of (2) are sufficient in turn for politeness.


composition’ after it, the relations joined by ‘and’, and an instantiation may say after the contract’s
name, between commas, what it writes for what, as “CSSN instantiates GSNW, a child for a ward
and a parent for a guardian, and restricts and extends it” [79]. A contract that extends another has
the other’s acts and its own, and an act of the other that its clauses change it replaces, keeping no
act beside its replacement: GSN’s unfriend, which also ends every membership between the two,
replaces GSG’s. Each clause opens with its legal modality, as the box below sets out (Section 10). A
box ends with the contract’s pragmas, which the app built from it follows, of two kinds: interface
pragmas, the tabs, buttons and questions of the app, and implementation pragmas, what the app
does beyond its screens, as the checks it makes before installing a program; each kind under its
own title, and the title Pragmas alone means interface pragmas. An implementation pragma may
declare that a person’s act stands for every act of a clause that the person guards: “Encounter: Alice
stands on [Open place] until [Close place]” says that Alice’s act Open place is her standing request
of every act of Encounter she guards, so that she is asked nothing, and that her act Close place
withdraws it [47]. The interface pragmas may open with a line naming the app’s look, its icon, an
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                    7


emoji, and its family, as “Look: emoji, network”, the colours of each family being one file of the
repository beside the contracts, so that what an app shows comes from its contract; then a line for
each tab of the app, naming the tab and what it lists. Each clause then has a line naming it, then
the person who proposes an act of it, a button for each act they propose, in brackets, bearing its
act’s name, and any field they fill, in angle brackets; a line for each other person who proposes
an act of the clause, written the same way; and a line for each other person whose will the act
needs, their name, a colon, and the question they are asked. A button is offered for each way the
proposer’s own state satisfies the atoms of the act at the proposer, and a field is for a variable of
the act those atoms do not bind, a text, a number, a new name, a person not yet known; so GKK’s
Acquaintance has a field, the person, whom Alice does not yet know, and its Introduction none,
Alice knowing both Bob and Carol. A question line belongs to the nearest button line above it, so
where a proposer’s acts ask questions each act takes a line, and buttons share a line only when no
question follows. A name in a pragma is the schema’s role of that name, the schemas naming their
roles as the clauses do, a listed role by the variable its quantifier binds. The contracts of this section
are examples: the syntax of Section 3, the compilation of Section 5 and the conditions of Section 7
apply to any contract written the same way.
   The clauses are stated here in prose, as a contract states them. The sections that follow give the
acts a formal syntax and semantics, and certify the contracts from the schemas.

      Legal Modalities of Contract Clauses

      Enforced: no act of the contract can breach it.
      Attested: an agent may breach it, and the contract records the breach.
      Undertaken: its performance is the person’s, and the contract records the undertaking.

  An agent running correct code, code that correctly implements the contract, cannot breach an
enforced clause.

2.1    Identity and Keys
A person is a natural person of age. A person takes the pronoun ‘they’; for readability we may write
‘she’ for Alice and ‘he’ for Bob etc. Alice, Bob and Carol are variables naming distinct persons. A
person knows another when they hold the other’s public key. Note that knowing is not a symmetric
relation; a directed edge in the agent’s knowledge graph.

      GKK: Grassroots Keys and Knowledge
  Alice, Bob and Carol are persons.
  1. Identity. Alice may establish her identity once by choosing a unique key pair. She makes
     herself known by the public key, signs her acts with the private key, and is accountable for
     every act signed with it.
  2. Acquaintance. Alice and Bob may come to know each other by an act of both.
  3. Introduction. If Alice and Bob know each other and Alice knows Carol, then Alice can
     make Carol known to Bob, with Bob’s consent.
      Pragmas
  Contacts: those Alice knows.
  Identity: Alice [Establish]
  Acquaintance: Alice [Acquaint] <person>
8                                                James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon




Fig. 2. The GKK app: Alice, before establishing her identity, with the button Establish (GKK:1); Bob’s contacts
after Alice makes Carol known to him, each with the button Introduce (GKK:3), and Acquaint with its field
(GKK:2).



        Bob: Alice wishes that you know each other.
    Introduction: Alice [Introduce]
        Bob: Alice asks to introduce Carol to you.

   GKK is the base contract, which every contract below extends: a person comes to know another
by an act of both or through someone they know, which is condition (8) of Section 1. A key pair
chosen at random is unique with high probability, and [18] replaces one that is lost or stolen. The
GKK app is a mini-app of the grassroots app, compiled down the cascade from GKK; Figure 2 shows
it realising GKK:1 and GKK:3.

2.2    Social Graph
Note that friendship is a symmetric relation; an undirected edge in the agent’s social graph.

      GSG: Grassroots Social Graph
    GSG extends GKK.
    Alice trusts Bob when she knows Bob personally and believes him to keep the undertakings he
    signs under grassroots contracts.
    Alice and Bob are persons.
    1. Friendship. Alice and Bob, who trust each other and are not friends, may jointly decide to
       become friends.
    2. Ending a friendship. Alice may end her friendship with Bob.
    3. Loss of Trust. Alice ends her friendship with Bob when she ceases to trust him.
      Pragmas
    Friends: the friends of Alice.
    Friendship: Alice [Befriend]
        Bob: Alice asks to be your friend.
    Ending a friendship: Alice [Unfriend]
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                      9




Fig. 3. The GSG app: Alice asks to be Bob’s friend, and Bob answers yes or no (GSG:1); Alice’s friends after
Bob says yes, Bob’s row with the buttons Introduce (GKK:3) and Unfriend (GSG:2).




   GSG extends GKK and is the grassroots social graph [75]. Trust is a belief, so once Alice knows
that Bob breached an undertaking, in particular a breach of an attested clause, of which the contract
holds the evidence, she can no longer trust him, and by GSG:3 she must end the friendship. A
friendship is mutual trust on record, so the social graph is a web of trust [94, 95]. The GSG app is a
mini-app of the grassroots app, compiled down the cascade from GSG; Figure 3 shows it realising
GSG:1.

2.3    Forwarding with Provenance
An item is a speech act a person holds, created by signing it or received signed over by the friend
who forwarded it; the signatures an item carries are its provenance, and its author is its first signer.


      GFP: Grassroots Forwarding with Provenance
  GFP extends GSG.
  The author of an item is its first signer.
  Alice and Bob are persons.
  1. Signing. Alice may create an item by signing it.
  2. Forwarding. Alice may forward an item she holds to her friend Bob, adding her signature
     to the signatures the item carries; Bob sees them.
      Pragmas
  Items: the items Alice holds, each with its signatures.
  Signing: Alice [Sign] <text>
  Forwarding: Alice [Forward]


   GFP extends GSG with items that carry their provenance: an item forwarded along a chain of
friends arrives with the signature of each, the author’s inside. The dissemination of programs by
the grassroots app, in preparation, extends it.
10                                             James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


2.4     Social Network
       GSN: Grassroots Social Network
     GSN amends GSG.
     Alice and Bob are persons.
     1. Messaging. Alice may send a message to her friend Bob.
     2. Group. Alice may create a group, of which she is the leader and first member; Alice may
        close the group by leaving it.
     3. Joining and Leaving. Alice, the leader of a group, may add her friend Bob to it, with Bob’s
        consent; either Alice or Bob may then end Bob’s membership in the group, which also ends
        when Alice and Bob cease to be friends.
     4. Group messaging. A member of a group may send a message to the group, and its leader
        delivers it to every current member.
      Pragmas
     Groups: the groups Alice is a member of.
     Messages: the messages Alice received.
     Posts: the posts Alice received in her groups.
     Messaging: Alice [Message] <text>
     Group: Alice [Create] <name> [Close]
     Joining and Leaving: Alice [Join]
         Bob: Alice asks you to join her group.
         Alice [Remove]
         Bob [Leave]
     Group messaging: Bob [Post] <text>
         Alice [Post own] <text>

  GSN extends GSG with messages and groups [75], and the paper carries it through. The GSN
app is a mini-app of the grassroots app, compiled down the cascade from GSN; Figure 4 shows it
realising GSN:2, GSN:3 and GSN:4. The accountability in GKK:1, the trust GSG:1 requires and GSG:3
bind the persons and describe no act of the contract; the other clauses describe acts and determine
the contract’s transactions. Section 10 says what a realisation does with a clause that describes no
act. In the repository GSN says ‘agent’ for ‘person’, so that children [79] and AI agents [80] can be
added to it.

2.5     Guardians and Wards
A ward is an agent who acts with a guardian: a child with their parents, an AI agent with the person
operating it, an organisation with its officers.

       GnW: Guardians 'n' Wards
     GnW amends GSG.
     A relation is a friendship or the membership of a group. Bob trusts Carol, a ward of Alice, when
     he trusts Alice.
     Alice, Bob and Erin are persons, Carol and Dave wards.
     1. Creation. Alice may create a ward, Carol, and establish Carol’s identity by choosing Carol’s
        key pair. Alice is then the creator, a guardian and a friend of Carol, a friendship Carol may
        not end.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                       11




Fig. 4. The GSN app: on Bob’s phone, Alice asks Bob to join her group friends, and Bob answers yes or no
(GSN:3); Bob’s Groups tab once he has joined, the group friends, led by Alice, with the buttons Leave and
Post (GSN:3, GSN:4), the posts delivered to him being in his Posts tab; on Alice’s phone, the group she leads,
with the buttons of the leader’s acts, Close, Join and Post own (GSN:2 to GSN:4).



  2.    Joint guardianship. Bob, a friend of a guardian of Carol, may become a guardian and a
     friend of Carol by an act of Bob and the guardians of Carol; Bob’s guardianship ends by an
     act of Bob, by an act of the other guardians of Carol, or when Bob ends his friendship with
     Carol. Carol may not end a friendship with her guardian.
  3. Ward and person. Carol, a ward of Alice, and Bob may come to know each other by an act
     of both. Carol enters a relation with Bob provided they know each other and Alice and Bob
     are friends, by an act of Carol, Alice and Bob. Carol, and the agent in a relation with Carol,
     may end it, as may Alice.
  4. Ward and ward. Carol, a ward of Alice, and Dave, a ward of Bob, may come to know each
     other by an act of both. Carol enters a relation with Dave provided they know each other,
     Alice knows Dave and is a friend of Bob, by an act of Carol, Alice, Dave and Bob, Alice and
     Bob possibly one person.
  5. Retiring. The guardians of Carol may retire Carol; every guardianship and relation of Carol
     then ends and Carol takes no act.
    Pragmas
  Wards: the wards Alice is a guardian of.
  Creation: Alice [Enter] <name>
      Carol: Alice asks to be your guardian.
  Joint guardianship: Alice [Make guardian]
      Bob: Alice asks you to be a guardian of her ward Carol.
      Erin: Alice asks that Bob be a guardian of your ward Carol.
      Alice [End guardian] <person>
      Erin: Alice asks to end Bob’s guardianship of your ward Carol.
      Bob [Resign] [Guardian unfriend]
  Ward and person: Bob [Ward acquaint] <person>
      Carol: Bob wishes that you know each other.
      Alice [Ward befriend]
      Carol: Alice asks that you and Bob befriend.
      Bob: Alice asks that you and her ward Carol befriend.
12                                            James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon



         Alice [Ward unfriend] <person>
         Carol [Ward end]
     Ward and ward: Carol [Wards acquaint] <person>
         Dave: Carol wishes that you know each other.
         Alice [Wards befriend]
         Carol: Alice asks that you and Dave befriend.
         Dave: Alice asks that you and Carol befriend.
         Bob: Alice asks that Carol and your ward Dave befriend.
     Retiring: Alice [Retire]
         Erin: Alice asks to retire your ward Carol.

   GnW amends GSG: the acts of GKK and GSG by which two agents come to know each other
and befriend are for persons, and a ward comes to know an agent, and befriends one, by the acts
of GnW:3 and GnW:4. A ward is created with a guardian, takes no act without one, and may be
retired by her guardians, after which she takes no act. GnW is parametric in its predicates of wards
(Section 9), and three contracts instantiate it, each with wards of its own kind: the child-safe social
network [79], whose wards are children and their guardians parents; accountable AI agents [80],
whose wards are AI agents and their guardians the persons operating them; and a contract for
grassroots organisations, in preparation, whose wards are organisations and their guardians officers.

2.6     Coins
A coin is a unit of debt naming its issuer, and an Alice-coin is a coin issued by Alice.

       GC: Grassroots Coins [73]
     GC extends GKK.
     Alice and Bob are persons.
     1. Minting. Alice may decide to issue Alice-coins.
     2. Swap. Alice and Bob, who know each other, may jointly decide to swap coins they hold.
     3. Payment. Alice may decide to pay Bob-coins she holds to Bob, whom she knows.
     4. Redemption. Alice may decide to redeem a Bob-coin she holds against any coin held by
        Bob, whom she knows.
     5. Pricing. Alice prices her offerings in Alice-coins and accepts them in payment at those
        prices.
      Pragmas
     Coins: the coins Alice holds.
     Minting: Alice [Mint] <amount>
     Swap: Alice [Swap] <amount given> <issuer wanted> <amount wanted>
        Bob: Alice asks to swap coins with you.
     Payment: Alice [Pay] <amount>
     Redemption: Alice [Redeem] <issuer wanted>

   GC is the grassroots coins contract [73], quoted from its owner’s copy; it extends GKK and not
GSG, no act of it requiring a friend, and the paper carries it through beside GSN. The first four
clauses describe acts and determine the contract’s transactions; a swap in which Alice gives an
Alice-coin and Bob gives a Bob-coin opens a mutual credit line between them, each thereafter
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                     13




Fig. 5. The GC app: Alice’s holdings after minting an Alice-coin (GC:1); Bob’s after swapping a Bob-coin for
Alice’s Alice-coin (GC:2), the coin with the button Pay (GC:3).



holding a coin of the other. The fifth describes no act: it gives a coin its worth. The GC app is a
mini-app of the grassroots app, compiled down the cascade from GC; Figure 5 shows it realising
GC:1 and GC:2.

3     SPEECH-ACT SCHEMAS
A clause of a contract describes an act, in the sense of a juridical act: a declaration by one or more
agents intended to create a legal effect. Each agent takes it as a digital speech act [32]: a speech
act [4, 70, 71] performed digitally, by signing a text with their private key, as GKK:1 says. This
section defines their formal syntax and semantics. An agent is a person and a machine; the person
has a will, the machine a state, here a multiset of atoms. Speech-act schemas specify the transactions
among agents: each is a first-order universally-quantified implication, Head ← Body guarded by
the persons who must will the act, its body what the act requires of the machines, its head what it
changes. A contract 𝐶 is a finite set of speech-act schemas over a set Σ of predicates, and 𝑆𝐶 is its
set of states.

3.1    Atoms and States
Write Π for a countable, potentially infinite set of at least two agents, of which every contract
and every run concerns a finite subset, and Δ for a countable set of utterances disjoint from it:
a message, a post or an item, taken as an identity and carrying no signature. A speech act is an
utterance or a speech act, signed by an agent: 𝐷 is the least set with Δ × Π ⊆ 𝐷 and 𝐷 × Π ⊆ 𝐷,
and the signer of (𝑥, 𝑝) ∈ 𝐷 is signer(𝑥, 𝑝) := 𝑝 [12, 32]; a speech act signed again carries its earlier
signers inside it, and 𝐷 𝑃 := {𝑑 ∈ 𝐷 : signer(𝑑) ∈ 𝑃 } for 𝑃 ⊆ Π. A community name is formed from
an agent and speech acts: the set N of community names is disjoint from Π ∪ 𝐷 and generated
freely by an injection 𝑝 ↦→ ⟨𝑝⟩ of Π into it and an injection (𝜈, 𝑦) ↦→ 𝜈 · 𝑦 of N × 𝐷 into it with
disjoint images; the agents of a name are ppl(⟨𝑝⟩) := {𝑝} and ppl(𝜈 · 𝑦) := ppl(𝜈) ∪ {signer(𝑦)},
and N𝑃 := {𝜈 ∈ N : ppl(𝜈) ⊆ 𝑃 }. Write Σ for a set of predicate symbols, predicates for short, and
𝛼 : Σ → N for their arities; a predicate of arity 𝑛 applied to 𝑛 arguments is an atom, a syntactic object
with no truth value. The arguments of an atom of a state are agents, speech acts and community
names; those of an atom of a schema are agent variables, speech-act variables and name terms, and
its first argument is its holder. An atom names what is among its arguments, the signer of a speech
14                                                James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


act among them and the agents of a community name among them: in a state an agent, and in a
schema an agent variable, a name term naming its base and the speech-act variables in it.
     Definition 1 (Atoms and states). For any set 𝑊 of arguments put
                             Σ[𝑊 ] = { 𝑒 (𝑢 1, . . . , 𝑢𝛼 (𝑒 ) ) : 𝑒 ∈ Σ, 𝑢𝑖 ∈ 𝑊 },
call its members the atoms over 𝑊 , and call 𝑢 1, . . . , 𝑢𝛼 (𝑒 ) the arguments and 𝑒 the predicate of
𝑒 (𝑢 1, . . . , 𝑢𝛼 (𝑒 ) ). An atom over Π ∪ 𝐷 ∪ N is ground , a local state is a finite multiset of ground
atoms, the initial state is 𝑠 0 = ∅, and
                𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) = { 𝑠 : 𝑠 a finite multiset over Σ[𝑃 ∪ 𝐷 𝑃 ∪ N𝑃 ] }            (𝑃 ⊂ Π).

     Three predicates carry the example through this section: identity, of arity zero, and knows and
     friend, of arity one. After Alice establishes an identity, comes to know Bob and befriends him,
     the state of Alice is the multiset {identity, knows(Bob), friend (Bob)}, which lies in 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) for
     every 𝑃 holding Bob and in none omitting him.

   Multiset operations are written ⊎, \, ∩ and ⊆: multiplicities are added, subtracted, minimised
and compared. A set is a multiset in which every multiplicity is one. The domain of the semantics
is multisets: a machine configuration is read as a structure in which each predicate, its holder first,
denotes a multiset of tuples, the multiplicity of a tuple being the number of copies of the atom that
its holder holds. An atom of a schema with multiplicity 𝜅 holds at a binding where its image is
held 𝜅 times or more, and its negation where fewer; a quantifier holds as it does over the tuples of
positive multiplicity; and a count or a sum over the instances of a quantifier takes each instance
as many times as its multiplicity (Definition 6, Appendix C), as the query languages for multisets
evaluate aggregates, each copy counting [53].
   A state is local, so its holder is no argument of its atoms: friend (Bob) in the state of Alice says
that Bob is a friend of Alice, and ¢(𝑢) in the state of Alice is a coin issued by 𝑢 and held by Alice; a
friend set is a set of the former and a holding a multiset of the latter. A schema writes the holder
first: its atom friend (Alice, Bob) stands for friend (Bob) in the state of the agent bound to Alice, and
a predicate of arity 𝑛 in a state has arity 𝑛 + 1 in a schema.

3.2     The Syntax of a Schema
Fix a set 𝑋 of agent variables disjoint from Π and a set 𝑌 of speech-act variables disjoint from 𝐷.
A schema is a clause over atoms whose arguments are agent variables, speech-act variables and
name terms, the first argument of each its holder; a literal is an atom or a negated atom ¬𝜑.
  A schema is a contract template: its agent variables are its parameters, and the identities of the
agents assuming them are filled in on signature. The guard names the agents the machine asks [12].
  For example, take the predicates identity, of arity zero in a state, and knows and friend, of arity
one. GSG:1, that Alice and Bob, who trust each other and are not friends, may jointly decide to
become friends, is the schema
          befriend : friend (Alice, Bob), friend (Bob, Alice) ←
               identity(Alice), knows(Alice, Bob), ¬friend (Alice, Bob),
               identity(Bob), knows(Bob, Alice), ¬friend (Bob, Alice) guarded by Alice, Bob.
Read it holder by holder. Alice must hold identity, an identity established under GKK:1, and
knows(Bob), held after an acquaintance under GKK:2 or an introduction under GKK:3, and must
not hold friend (Bob); the head adds friend (Bob) to her state, which is the effect of befriending; and
the same holds of Bob with the two exchanged. Both are guards, so the act takes the will of both.
Nothing is deleted, and both agents change state.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                         15


   Closing a group is one act of its leader and of every member. The body and the head of a schema
may have a quantified conjunct, ∀¯    𝑥 (𝐴 ∧ 𝜑 → 𝜓 ): for every value of the variables 𝑥¯ under which an
agent holds the atom 𝐴 and 𝜑 holds, 𝜓 holds, in the body, or is made to hold, in the head, where 𝜓 is
a conjunction of literals. In the body of close, “every Bob that Alice lists in the group is a member”,
∀Bob (listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛) → member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ ·𝑛)), and in its head, “every Bob that Alice lists is
no longer a member”, ∀Bob (listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛) → ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ ·𝑛)); Bob, quantified and
holding the literals, is a listed role of the schema. A quantified conjunct may also be a condition on
one agent’s own state, as a ride-hailing contract in preparation awards a ride to Bob only if every bid
Alice holds on the ride scores no more than Bob’s, ∀𝑥, 𝑝 ′, 𝑑 ′ (bid (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑟, 𝑝 ′, 𝑑 ′ ) → 𝑊 (𝑑 ′ ) − 𝑝 ′ ≤
𝑊 (𝑑) − 𝑝), which makes no listed role. Any first-order formula may serve as a condition in the
body, provided it is safe: every variable a quantifier binds ranges over the arguments of an atom
an agent holds, as in the safe queries of the relational calculus [1, 15], so the formula is evaluated
over the agents’ finite states. A name term names a community, such as a group. So the full power
of universally quantified first-order logic is used: a schema is an implication, not a clause, close
quantifying in its body and in its head:
          close : closed (Alice, 𝑛), ¬listed (Alice, Alice, 𝑛), ¬member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛),
                  ∀Bob (listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛) → ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛)) ←
               member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), listed (Alice, Alice, 𝑛),
               ∀Bob (listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛) → member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛)) guarded by Alice.
  Definition 2 (Safe formula, name term, role). A name term of a schema is ⟨𝜋⟩ for an agent variable
𝜋 of it, or 𝜎 · 𝑦 for a name term 𝜎 of it and a speech-act variable 𝑦 of it or the wildcard _, and its
base is 𝜋 and the base of 𝜎 respectively. A safe formula of a schema is built from atoms, the first
argument of each its holder, and constraints (Appendix C) by ∧, ∨ and ¬ and by the anchored
quantifiers ∃𝑥¯ (𝐴 ∧ 𝜑) and ∀¯ 𝑥 (𝐴 ∧ 𝜑 → 𝜓 ), 𝜑 and 𝜓 safe formulas and 𝐴 an atom, the quantifier’s
anchor, among whose arguments, or in name terms among them, every variable of 𝑥¯ occurs. A
quantified conjunct is a safe formula ∀¯  𝑥 (𝐴 ∧ 𝜑 → 𝐿1 ∧ · · · ∧ 𝐿𝑚 ), 𝑚 ≥ 0 and each 𝐿 𝑗 a literal. An
agent role of a schema is an agent variable of it that no quantifier binds and that holds an atom of it
or is a guard. An agent variable bound by a quantifier whose anchor is held by an agent role, and
holding an atom of the schema, is a listed role, every quantifier binding it having the same anchor,
whose holder is the listed role’s base; it is written 𝜋 [𝐴], 𝜋 its base and the bound variables replaced
by _ in 𝐴. A role is an agent role or a listed role, and the base of an agent role is itself.

  A group of GSN (Section 2.4) is named ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛, by its leader Alice and a speech act 𝑛 Alice
  signs, and Alice is its one agent. Closing it has two roles: the agent role Alice, its leader, and
  the listed role Alice[listed (_, 𝑛)], every agent Alice lists as a member of the group 𝑛, the Bob of
  the two quantified conjuncts above.

   Definition 3 (Speech-act schema). A speech-act schema is written
                                       𝜏:    𝐻 ←𝐵       guarded by 𝐺,
with 𝜏 its name, 𝐵, its body, a finite multiset of literals and safe formulas, and 𝐻 , its head, a finite
multiset of literals and quantified conjuncts, over atoms whose arguments are agent variables,
speech-act variables, name terms, the wildcard _ and, in 𝐻 , signed terms sgn(𝑦, 𝜋), 𝑦 a speech-act
variable that occurs in an atom an agent role requires and 𝜋 an agent role, the speech act 𝑦 signed
by the agent in 𝜋, every agent variable a quantifier binds that holds an atom being a listed role,
and 𝐺 a set of agent variables of it, its guards. A wildcard in an unnegated atom is a variable of
its own, one per occurrence; in a negated atom it is universally quantified, the atom forbidding,
in 𝐵, and deleting, in 𝐻 , every atom that matches it, and a negated atom of 𝐻 with a wildcard
16                                               James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


is cleared. Its roles 𝜋1, . . . , 𝜋𝑘 , 𝑘 ≥ 1 its arity, are its agent roles and its listed roles, a role being
a guarding role if it is a guard, and its variables are its agent variables that are not roles and its
speech-act variables that no conjunct quantifies. An agent role requires the atoms of 𝐵 it holds
and forbids those negated in 𝐵 there; it adds the atoms of 𝐻 it holds, deletes those negated in 𝐻
there with no wildcard, and clears those with one; a listed role does the same by the literals it holds
in the conclusions of the quantified conjuncts of 𝐵 and 𝐻 that bind it, their other variables read
as variables of the schema. Each such atom is written without its holder, and for the role 𝜋𝑖 the
five multisets are written =(𝑖), ¬(𝑖), +(𝑖), −(𝑖), _(𝑖). The literals an agent role holds in a
quantified conjunct of 𝐻 that binds no role it is are conditional: the role adds, deletes or clears
them at each instance of the conjunct (Definition 7), and they are in none of its five multisets;
the safe formulas of 𝐵 are conditions on the agents’ states, which the denotation reads and the
three conditions read only as stated; where Definitions 11 and 12 and the guarded predicates of
Section 10 speak of an atom a schema adds or deletes, a conditional one is included, and a listed
role’s at its conjunct; for both, the anchor of the conjunct counts as an atom its holder requires,
and the conjunct’s other variables as variables of the schema. It is required that −(𝑖) ⊆ =(𝑖), as a
deleted atom is held, and that +(𝑖) ⊎ −(𝑖) ≠ ∅ at some role, as a transaction changes a state [51]; a
role that is not a guarding role and has +(𝑖) ⊎ −(𝑖) = ∅ is a reading role.
   The sentences of GSG:1 and its schema correspond: “may jointly decide” is the guard, “who
trust each other” the two knows atoms of the body, trust presupposing that each knows the other
and the rest of it describing no act, “are not friends” its two negated atoms, and “become friends”
the head. The syntax is universally quantified first-order implications over multisets of atoms,
with a guard, the body a safe formula, which with the cardinality of Appendix C is a formula of
first-order logic with counting [52], and with its sum one that also sums, and nothing else: the
wildcard is an anonymous variable, universally quantified in a negated atom, ¬listed (Alice, Bob, _)
being ∀𝑦 ¬listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑦); a listed role, and a condition on one agent’s state, is a quantified
conjunct; and a multiplicity 𝑘 · 𝜑 (Appendix C) is 𝑘 copies of 𝜑. The definitions restrict this syntax
only where a proof below uses the restriction, and say which. What is not first-order logic is the
arrow, which is read as a state change (Section 3.3).

     At the role Alice of befriend the literals with holder Alice give +(1) = {friend (Bob)} and
     ¬(1) = {friend (Bob)}, =(1) = {identity, knows(Bob)} and −(1) empty, and at Bob the same
     with the two roles exchanged. Both roles guard, and +(𝑖) ⊎ −(𝑖) ≠ ∅ at each: befriending
     changes the state of both agents.

    A guarding role may add and delete nothing; the agent in that role then wills the act, and their
state is a precondition of it and does not change. A reading role neither wills the act nor changes
state; the agent’s state is a condition of the act, read by the others, so Definition 13 admits one in a
schema with a guarding role only where a guarding role of the schema requires an atom naming
it, as Alice, who points to Bob, who is connected to Carol, may decide to point to Carol, and in
a schema with no guarding role only where the atoms it adds and deletes at one role, each the
only one of its predicate, together name every other role, as Alice, who follows a feed through
Bob, receives from Bob every item of it that Bob holds (Section 5.1). A cleared atom deletes every
atom of the agent’s state that it matches, and requires none: GSN’s unfriend (Section 3.4) has
¬listed (Alice, Bob, _) in its head, every membership of Bob in a group Alice leads, so that ending a
friendship ends every membership between the two at once.
    Speech-act schemas allow values of any type: an argument of an atom may be a value or a value
variable, a schema may carry a constraint on its value variables, which every binding satisfies, and
an atom of the head may carry a multiplicity, a count of copies and so a positive whole number, as
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                   17


the bonds of [81] need for maturity dates and amounts (Appendix C). A value names no agent, so the
conditions below do not concern it. A contract may also name environment predicates, such as the
date, whose atoms are over values, which the machine holds from startup and the platform keeps
current, and which no schema adds or deletes; a schema may require or forbid one (Appendix C).
  Multiplicities make the language Turing-complete, with one agent and no guard.
  Proposition 4 (Turing completeness). For every two-counter machine 𝑀 there is a syntactically
grassroots contract 𝐶𝑀 such that 𝑀 halts from empty counters if and only if some correct run of the
protocol realising 𝐶𝑀 reaches a configuration in which an agent holds halt.
   The proof, in Appendix B, writes a counter as the multiplicity of an atom, incremented by
adding a copy, decremented by deleting one and tested for zero by a negated body atom, and each
instruction of 𝑀 as a schema with no guard, which liveness takes.
   A contract also carries pragmas, which the app built from it follows: its tabs, and for each act
who proposes it, the button proposing it and the question the machine puts to each other agent in
a guarding role.
   Definition 5 (Binding). A binding 𝛽 for a schema 𝜏 maps its agent variables and its speech-act
variables that no conjunct quantifies to Π and 𝐷, distinct agent roles to distinct agents, but for
two the schema marks as possibly one, which it may send to one agent if neither of them adds
or deletes an atom naming the other, as Proposition 51 needs, and is extended to name terms by
𝛽 (⟨𝜋⟩) := ⟨𝛽 (𝜋)⟩ and 𝛽 (𝜎 · 𝑦) := 𝛽 (𝜎) · 𝛽 (𝑦), to signed terms by 𝛽 (sgn(𝑦, 𝜋)) := (𝛽 (𝑦), 𝛽 (𝜋)),
to the wildcard by 𝛽 (_) := _, and to atoms and to multisets of them componentwise. A ground
atom matches 𝛽 (𝜑), for an atom 𝜑 with a wildcard, if it is obtained from 𝛽 (𝜑) by replacing each
wildcard by an argument, a speech act where the wildcard is in a name term. It is admissible if
every speech-act variable of 𝜏 that occurs in an atom added at a role, as an argument or in a name
term, and in no atom required at a role is mapped to a speech act signed by the agent in a guarding
role of 𝜏.

  A binding of befriend sends Alice to 𝑝 and Bob to 𝑞, distinct because each role adds an atom
  naming the other, and carries +(1) to {friend (𝑞)} and +(2) to {friend (𝑝)}. Befriending has no
  speech-act variable, so every binding of it is admissible.

   Alice and Bob are two persons, and Alice is one person, in a clause and in its schema: distinct
agent roles are bound to distinct agents, but for two a clause names as possibly one person, which
its schema marks so, and a listed role and the role whose agent lists it, as close’s two Alices, are
carried out by that agent together, as Definition 7 states.
   A speech-act schema is a STRIPS operator [23] in multiagent form: what a role requires and
forbids is a precondition, what it adds and deletes are an add list and a delete list, and a schema
carries one of each per role, where STRIPS has one precondition and one pair of lists over a single
global state. The literals are also the four kinds of arc of a contextual net transition [61]: an atom
required and deleted is consumed, one required and kept is a positive context condition, one
forbidden is a negative context condition, and one added is produced. We take no result from either
theory.

3.3 Semantics
A schema denotes a set of volition-guarded transactions [51], and speech-act schemas are a restricted
surface syntax for them; the denotation is defined from the schema over the states of [51]. An agent
is a person and a machine. A machine state is a local state, the atoms the machine holds; a volitional
state is the person’s, the set of classes of acts they are willing their machine to take part in, a class
18                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


being an act with its participants (Definition 16); an agent configuration over 𝑃 gives each agent of
𝑃 its two states, and the initial configuration has every machine empty and every person willing
nothing. A schema denotes a relation on agent configurations, read off its literals and quantified
conjuncts: at a binding 𝛽 of its variables, it relates 𝑐 to 𝑐 ′ when, at 𝑐, every atom of its body holds at
the machine of the agent in its holder’s role and no negated atom of its body does, the person of
each guard holds the class of the act, and 𝑐 ′ is 𝑐 with the head applied, the atoms of the head added
at the machines of their holders and the negated ones deleted, and the class removed from every
person, the will being fulfilled. A person changes their volitional state by a volition change, an act
of theirs alone that no schema describes. In the terms of [51] the relation a schema denotes at a
binding is the set of volition-guarded transactions it determines there, a machine transaction over
the agents in its roles with the agents in its guarding roles as guards, which the two definitions
below state.                          Ã
   For multisets 𝑠 1, . . . , 𝑠𝑛 write 𝑗 𝑠 𝑗 for their join, in which the multiplicity of each member is the
greatest of its multiplicities in the 𝑠 𝑗 .

   Definition 6 (Extent). Let 𝛽 be a binding for a schema 𝜏 and 𝑑 a machine configuration over a
set of agents holding 𝛽 (𝜋) for every agent role 𝜋 of 𝜏. An instance in 𝑑 of a quantifier of 𝜏 with
anchor 𝐴, within the instances of the quantifiers enclosing it, is an assignment 𝛼 of the variables
it binds under which the agent of the holder of 𝐴 holds (𝛽 ∪ 𝛼) (𝐴) in 𝑑; a state being finite, a
quantifier has finitely many, and the multiplicity of 𝛼 is the number of copies of (𝛽 ∪ 𝛼) (𝐴) that
agent holds. The extent in 𝑑 of an agent role 𝜋 of 𝜏 is ext𝑑 (𝜋) := {𝛽 (𝜋)}, and of a listed role 𝑥 it
is ext𝑑 (𝑥) := {𝛼 (𝑥) : 𝛼 an instance in 𝑑 of a quantifier binding 𝑥 at which the condition 𝜑 of its
conjunct holds}, so a condition leaves out of an act those it excludes, as the guardians of a ward
other than one of them end his guardianship (Section 3.5).

     At a binding sending Alice to 𝑎 and 𝑛 to 𝜈, the extent of the listed role of closing a group in a
     configuration 𝑐 is {𝑞 : listed (𝑞, 𝜈) ∈ 𝑐 𝑎 }, every member 𝑎 lists, 𝑎 among them.

   Definition 7 (Denotation). Let 𝜏 be a schema with roles Ð 𝜋1, . . . , 𝜋𝑘 , 𝛽 a binding for it and 𝑑 a

a listed role’s taken under 𝛽 ∪ 𝛼 at each instance 𝛼 of its conjunct with 𝛼 (𝜋𝑖 ) = 𝑝 at which the
conjunct’s 𝜑 holds, together with the conditional literals held by 𝑝 under 𝛽 ∪𝛼 at each such instance
of their conjuncts, sorted the same way. A machine transaction 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ over 𝑄 is determined by 𝜏
at 𝛽, and the volition-guarded transaction it determines, below, is denoted by 𝜏 at 𝛽, if 𝛽 (𝜎 · 𝑦) is an
