which at a binding 𝛽 denotes the volition-guarded transactions determined by 𝜏 at 𝛽.
   The holder of an atom in a schema is dropped in the state: 𝛽 (+(𝑖)) is added at the machine of
𝑝 ∈ 𝑄𝑖 , and so on. The denotation of a contract is the protocol over the transactions its schemas
denote (Definition 16): a transition system per set of agents, whose transitions are the volition
changes and the transactions, and whose runs are correct when safe and live, every class that stays
enabled being eventually taken. So a contract has the runs, the liveness and the grassroots notions
of [51] by definition, and the theorems of Sections 6 and 7 are about its denotation. A schema is
also a program of first-order dynamic logic, and the run properties are formulas of temporal logic
(Section 12).

3.4    The Schemas of the Social Network
The schemas of GSN are those of GKK and GSG, GSG’s unfriend replaced by GSN’s, a message and
the schemas of a group; those of GFP are GKK’s, GSG’s and two over item, of arity one: item(𝑥) in
the state of Alice is a speech act Alice holds, which she signed or a friend signed over to her. Ten
predicates, each shown with its arity in a state. identity, of arity zero: in the state of Alice it says
that Alice has established an identity; every role of every schema but establish’s requires it, and the
clauses below show it only in establish. knows, of arity one: knows(Bob) in the state of Alice says
that Alice holds Bob’s public key; nothing deletes or forbids it, a second copy meaning the same.
friend, of arity one: friend (Bob) in the state of Alice says that Bob is a friend of Alice. received, of
arity two: received (𝑥, Alice) in the state of Bob is a speech act 𝑥 Bob received from Alice, signed by
Alice, what Alice received from others signed inside it. Alice and Bob are the roles, and Carol an
agent variable.

      GKK: Speech-Act Schemas

  1. identity(Alice) ←
          ¬identity(Alice) guarded by Alice.
  2. knows(Alice, Bob), knows(Bob, Alice) ←
          guarded by Alice, Bob.
  3. knows(Bob, Carol) ←
          knows(Alice, Bob), knows(Alice, Carol), knows(Bob, Alice) guarded by Alice, Bob.

      GSG: Speech-Act Schemas

  1. friend (Alice, Bob), friend (Bob, Alice) ←
          knows(Alice, Bob), ¬friend (Alice, Bob), knows(Bob, Alice), ¬friend (Bob, Alice)
          guarded by Alice, Bob.
  2. ¬friend (Alice, Bob), ¬friend (Bob, Alice) ←
          friend (Alice, Bob), friend (Bob, Alice) guarded by Alice.
20                                                 James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


       GFP: Speech-Act Schemas

     1. item(Alice, 𝑥) ←
            ¬item(Alice, 𝑥) guarded by Alice.
     2. item(Bob, sgn(𝑥, Alice)) ←
            item(Alice, 𝑥), friend (Alice, Bob) guarded by Alice.

       GSN: Speech-Act Schemas

     1. received (Bob, 𝑥, Alice) ←
              friend (Alice, Bob) guarded by Alice.
     2. member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), listed (Alice, Alice, 𝑛) ←
              ¬member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), ¬closed (Alice, 𝑛) guarded by Alice.
        closed (Alice, 𝑛), ¬listed (Alice, Alice, 𝑛), ¬member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛),
        ∀Bob (listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛) → ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛)) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), listed (Alice, Alice, 𝑛),
              ∀Bob (listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛) → member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛)) guarded by Alice.
     3. listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), friend (Alice, Bob), ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛)
              guarded by Alice, Bob.
        ¬listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) ←
              listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) guarded by Bob.
        ¬listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) ←
              listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) guarded by Alice.
        ¬friend (Alice, Bob), ¬listed (Alice, Bob, _), ¬member (Alice, ⟨Bob⟩ · _), ¬friend (Bob, Alice),
        ¬listed (Bob, Alice, _), ¬member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · _) ←
              friend (Alice, Bob), friend (Bob, Alice) guarded by Alice.
     4. posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), ¬posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛) guarded by Alice.
        posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), friend (Alice, Bob), ¬posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛),
              member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) guarded by Bob.
        delivered (Alice, 𝑥, Bob, 𝑛), received_in(Bob, 𝑥, Alice, 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛), listed (Alice, Bob, 𝑛), friend (Alice, Bob),
              ¬delivered (Alice, 𝑥, Bob, 𝑛), member (Bob, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) unguarded.
        received_in(Alice, 𝑥, Alice, 𝑛) ←
              member (Alice, ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), posted (Alice, 𝑥, 𝑛), ¬received_in(Alice, 𝑥, Alice, 𝑛)
              unguarded.

   A group is named by the community name ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛 (Section 3.1), Alice its leader and 𝑛 a
speech act Alice signs: member (⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛) in the state of Bob says that Bob is a member of the
group 𝑛 of Alice, and in the state of Alice listed (Bob, 𝑛) says the same, closed (𝑛) that the group
has ended, posted (𝑥, 𝑛) that 𝑥 was posted to it, and delivered (𝑥, Bob, 𝑛) that Alice delivered 𝑥 to
Bob in it; received_in(𝑥, Alice, 𝑛) in the state of Bob is a speech act 𝑥 Alice delivered to Bob in the
group 𝑛, signed by the member who posted it. In the schemas of a group Alice is the leader. A
member’s post adds the posted item at the leader, as the member signed it, and delivery adds it
at each member. Remove, guarded by Alice, ends a member’s membership at Alice’s will. GSN’s
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                 21


unfriend, which replaces GSG’s, also ends every membership between the two: it clears at each of
them the other’s listing in a group they lead and their own membership in a group the other leads.
Close has a quantified conjunct in its body and in its head, over every Bob that Alice lists, the listed
role Alice[listed (_, 𝑛)] (Definition 2), so closing a group ends every membership in one act.
 “Alice may establish her identity once” is establish, guarded by Alice and forbidding the identity
it adds, and every other role requires it. “Alice and Bob may come to know each other by an act
of both” is acquaint, guarded by both, each role adding knows of the other; “Alice can make Carol
known to Bob, with Bob’s consent” is introduce, guarded by both, Carol its agent variable, whom
Alice must know and Bob comes to know, and “if Alice and Bob know each other” is the knows
each role requires of the other. “Alice and Bob, who know each other . . . and are not friends, may
jointly decide” is the two guarding roles of befriend, the knows each requires and its forbidden
atom, and that they trust each other describes no act; “Alice may end her friendship” is the single
guarding role of unfriend, which deletes in both roles. “Alice may send a message to her friend
Bob” is message: only the sender guards, since only the sender decides; “her friend Bob” is the
friend (Bob) the sender requires; and the message is a speech act signed by the sender (Definition 5),
what they received from others signed inside it. Create and close are GSN:2, join, guarded by both,
leave, remove and GSN’s unfriend GSN:3, and post_own, post, deliver and deliver_leader GSN:4.
“Alice may create an item by signing it” is GFP’s sign, 𝑥 a speech act Alice signs (Definition 5);
“may forward an item she holds to her friend Bob, adding her signature” is forward, Alice requiring
item(𝑥) and friend (Bob) and Bob receiving item(sgn(𝑥, Alice)), the item signed over by Alice, with
its earlier signatures inside. Delivery is not guarded: the leader undertook it on creating the group.
The records. The schemas meet the clauses differently. Befriend is the only schema adding a
friend atom, that atom’s argument is a role, and befriend guards in both, so an agent’s friends are
only agents who have willed the friendship, and no act of the contract can put one there against
that agent’s will. Unfriend is the only schema deleting a friend atom and it deletes in both roles,
so a friendship never ends for one agent without the other. A speech act a person signs records
nothing against them until it is sent; the record is then the recipient’s received (𝑥, Alice), signed
by the sender, what the sender received from others signed inside it. Acquaint and introduce are
the schemas adding a knows atom, so a person knows another only by an act of both or through
someone who knows them, and befriend requires knows of the other at each role, so two persons
can befriend only if they know each other. Knowing a person is not trusting them, so the schemas
cannot stop the trust GSG:1 requires being absent; instead befriend leaves each of the two on record
in the other’s state.

3.5   The Schemas of Guardians and Wards
GnW amends GSG. The declaration of a contract says of each of its names what it is, a person
or a ward, and GnW records it: an agent holds person or ward, of arity zero, from the act that
establishes its identity, establish, which now adds person with identity, or the schema of GnW:1. A
role named as a person requires person, and one named as a ward ward; so the acts of GKK and
GSG are for persons, the person introduced being a role of introduce that reads, and no act of a
person says anything of wards. Unfriend is a person’s too, and not for a guardian and her ward,
forbidding at Alice guardian_of of Bob. Five more predicates join them, each shown with its arity
in a state. creator and created, of arity one: creator (Alice) in the state of Carol and created (Carol)
in the state of Alice record that Alice created Carol, and no schema deletes them. guardian_of
and guarded_by, of arity one: every guardian Bob of Carol, her creator at first among them, holds
guardian_of (Carol), and Carol holds guarded_by(Bob). retired, of arity zero, records that a ward
was retired. Every role requires identity, but establish’s and Carol’s in the schema of GnW:1, which
22                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


forbid it and add it, and every role of Alice, Bob and Erin requires person; the clauses below show
neither.


       GnW: Speech-Act Schemas

     1. created (Alice, Carol), guardian_of (Alice, Carol), friend (Alice, Carol), knows(Alice, Carol),
        identity(Carol), ward (Carol), creator (Carol, Alice), guarded_by(Carol, Alice),
        friend (Carol, Alice), knows(Carol, Alice) ←
             ¬identity(Carol) guarded by Alice, Carol.
     2. guardian_of (Bob, Carol), friend (Bob, Carol), knows(Bob, Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Bob),
        friend (Carol, Bob), knows(Carol, Bob) ←
             ¬guardian_of (Bob, Carol), guardian_of (Alice, Carol), friend (Alice, Bob), ward (Carol),
             guarded_by(Carol, Alice), ¬guarded_by(Carol, Bob), ¬friend (Carol, Bob),
             ∀Erin (guarded_by(Carol, Erin) → guardian_of (Erin, Carol)) guarded by Bob, Alice,
             Erin.
        ¬guardian_of (Bob, Carol), ¬guarded_by(Carol, Bob) ←
             guardian_of (Bob, Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Bob), guardian_of (Alice, Carol),
             guarded_by(Carol, Alice),
             ∀Erin (guarded_by(Carol, Erin) ∧ Erin ≠ Bob → guardian_of (Erin, Carol))
             guarded by Alice, Erin.
        ¬guardian_of (Bob, Carol), ¬guarded_by(Carol, Bob) ←
             guardian_of (Bob, Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Bob) guarded by Bob.
        ¬friend (Bob, Carol), ¬guardian_of (Bob, Carol), ¬friend (Carol, Bob),
        ¬guarded_by(Carol, Bob) ←
             friend (Bob, Carol), guardian_of (Bob, Carol), friend (Carol, Bob),
             guarded_by(Carol, Bob) guarded by Bob.
     3. knows(Carol, Bob), knows(Bob, Carol) ←
             ward (Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Alice) guarded by Carol, Bob.
        friend (Carol, Bob), friend (Bob, Carol) ←
             ward (Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Alice), knows(Carol, Bob), ¬friend (Carol, Bob),
             knows(Bob, Carol), ¬friend (Bob, Carol), guardian_of (Alice, Carol), friend (Alice, Bob)
             guarded by Carol, Bob, Alice.
        ¬friend (Carol, Bob), ¬friend (Bob, Carol) ←
             friend (Carol, Bob), guarded_by(Carol, Alice), ¬guarded_by(Carol, Bob),
             friend (Bob, Carol), guardian_of (Alice, Carol) guarded by Alice.
        ¬friend (Carol, Bob), ¬friend (Bob, Carol) ←
             ward (Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Alice), friend (Carol, Bob), ¬guarded_by(Carol, Bob),
             friend (Bob, Carol) guarded by Carol.
     4. knows(Carol, Dave), knows(Dave, Carol) ←
             ward (Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Alice), ward (Dave), guarded_by(Dave, Bob)
             guarded by Carol, Dave.
        friend (Carol, Dave), friend (Dave, Carol) ←
             ward (Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Alice), knows(Carol, Dave), ¬friend (Carol, Dave),
             ward (Dave), guarded_by(Dave, Bob), knows(Dave, Carol), ¬friend (Dave, Carol),
             guardian_of (Alice, Carol), knows(Alice, Dave), friend (Alice, Bob),
Grassroots Contracts                                                                               23



           guardian_of (Bob, Dave) guarded by Carol, Dave, Alice, Bob; Alice and Bob possibly
           one person.
  5. retired (Carol), ¬friend (Carol, _), ¬guarded_by(Carol, _),
     ∀Bob (friend (Carol, Bob) → ¬friend (Bob, Carol)),
     ∀Erin (guarded_by(Carol, Erin) → ¬guardian_of (Erin, Carol)) ←
           ward (Carol), guarded_by(Carol, Alice), guardian_of (Alice, Carol),
           ∀Bob (friend (Carol, Bob) → friend (Bob, Carol)),
           ∀Erin (guarded_by(Carol, Erin) → guardian_of (Erin, Carol)) guarded by Alice, Erin.

   The schema of GnW:1 is enter: a ward is founded by its first guardian, who makes the ward’s
founding will on behalf of the ward, so both guard it. Those of GnW:2 are make_guardian, guarded
by the new guardian Bob, by Alice, a guardian whose friend he is, and by every guardian Erin
of Carol, a listed role; end_guardian, guarded by a guardian Alice and every other guardian Erin
but Bob, whom the condition of its conjunct leaves out; resign; and guardian_unfriend. Those
of GnW:3 are ward_acquaint, ward_befriend, ward_unfriend, by a guardian, and ward_end, by
the ward, a person ending the friendship by unfriend. Those of GnW:4 are wards_acquaint and
wards_befriend, and that of GnW:5 retire, guarded by the guardians of the ward, which takes every
friend Bob and every guardian Erin of hers by its quantified conjuncts and clears her friendships
and guardianships.
   A ward takes no act without a guardian: every role a ward guards requires guarded_by of a
guardian, Alice being an agent variable of ward_acquaint, ward_end and wards_acquaint and
holding nothing in them. A guardian, the creator among them, may end their guardianship, so a
ward may be left with none; she then goes on existing, with her records and her friends, who may
each end their friendship with her, and takes no act, and nobody can become her guardian, there
being no guardian for him to be a friend of. A retired ward is one with no guardian and no friend,
and no guard need ask whether an agent is retired. GnW is parametric in six of its predicates,
ward, creator, created, guardian_of , guarded_by and retired (Definition 38): a contract with wards
of some kind instantiates them at predicates of its own, and has these schemas for its wards without
restating one. A parametric contract has no realisation, so no platform runs GnW; each runs an
instance of it. The relation these schemas write is the friendship; a contract with groups writes
the membership of a group the same way, as the child-safe social network does [79]. A contract
built on GnW may add to a ward’s acts what its wards carry, as an AI agent carries its principal’s
attestation of the code it runs [80]; may restrict them, as a child comes to know only an agent a
parent knows [79]; and may have wards of a kind that takes no act, their guardians taking every
act for them, as the officers of an organisation do: no role of its schemas that guards requires the
mark of that kind, and no schema says of an agent that it is not of it.
   GnW is not syntactically grassroots, in the sense of Definition 14 below. Acquaint requires person,
which a ward does not hold, and befriend requires knows of the other, which no schema of arity
one adds; so the contract has no one act that any two agents can take. A ward enters the contract
with a guardian recorded, who is a person, and while she has one comes to know any agent by an
act of the two, and Section 8 certifies GnW by that.

3.6   The Schemas of the Coins
The schemas of GC are GKK’s three and four over one predicate, ¢, of arity one: ¢(𝑢) in the state
of Alice is a coin issued by 𝑢 and held by Alice. The roles are Alice and Bob; every role requires
identity, shown only in establish. A negated atom of a head with a multiplicity 𝑘 deletes 𝑘 copies,
so a deleted coin is also required, 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, 𝑢) standing in the body and negated in the head.
24                                                 James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


       GC: Speech-Act Schemas

     1. 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, Alice) ←
               guarded by Alice.
     2. 𝑘 1 · ¢(Alice, 𝑣), knows(Alice, 𝑣), ¬𝑘 · ¢(Alice, 𝑢), 𝑘 · ¢(Bob, 𝑢), knows(Bob, 𝑢), ¬𝑘 1 · ¢(Bob, 𝑣)
        ←
               knows(Alice, Bob), 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, 𝑢), knows(Bob, Alice), 𝑘 1 · ¢(Bob, 𝑣) guarded by Alice,
               Bob.
     3. ¬𝑘 · ¢(Alice, Bob), 𝑘 · ¢(Bob, Bob) ←
               knows(Alice, Bob), 𝑘 · ¢(Alice, Bob) guarded by Alice.
     4. ¬¢(Alice, Bob), ¢(Alice, 𝑟 ), knows(Alice, 𝑟 ), ¬¢(Bob, 𝑟 ), ¢(Bob, Bob) ←
               knows(Alice, Bob), ¢(Alice, Bob), ¢(Bob, 𝑟 ) guarded by Alice.


“Alice may decide” is the single guarding role of mint, of pay and of redeem; “Alice and Bob may
jointly decide” is the two guarding roles of the swap; “Bob-coins she holds” is that pay and redeem
require of Alice coins whose argument is Bob, pay 𝑘 of them and redeem one; “whom she knows”
and “who know each other” are the knows the roles require; “any coin held by Bob” is redeem’s
agent variable 𝑟 ; and whoever receives a coin comes to know its issuer, the swap and redeem adding
knows of the issuer at the receiving role, so a holder can always redeem. The amounts 𝑘 and 𝑘 1 are
multiplicities (Section 3.2), positive whole numbers. The mutual credit line is the swap at a binding
sending 𝑢 to Alice and 𝑣 to Bob, where each gives coins of its own issue.

The records. Selling at an advertised price is no act of the contract, so no act can breach GC:5 and
no run shows whether the issuer honours it; but a mutual credit line leaves a coin of the issuer in
another agent’s holding, and that coin, naming the issuer, is the record of the undertaking. Minting
records nothing against the issuer, putting the coin in its own holding; the undertaking becomes
evidence only when the coin first passes to someone else, as a promissory note does when handed
over. A clause that a holding contains a coin only if its issuer willed the act that put it there is
breached by pay, which adds ¢(Bob) at Bob and guards only at Alice, and by the swap, which moves
a coin between two agents by the will of the two. An issuer is not asked when its coin moves. A
coin is therefore money rather than a promise between two people.


3.7     The Conditions Every Contract of Speech-Act Schemas Meets
Of the eight conditions of Section 1, (1), (2) and (4) are met by every contract written as speech-act
schemas, and so is (5), in that nobody is bound who has not adopted the contract, by two properties
of the language. Both are about the protocol realising a contract, so both are stated and proved in
Section 5, where that protocol is defined, and used here. Conditions (3), (6), (7) and (8) are those of
Section 3.8.
   The first property is that every participant in an act takes a role in it: an agent’s state changes
only under a schema of the contract and only in a role of it, which is Proposition 19.
   The second is that the language cannot name an agent. The agent roles 𝑅, the agent variables
𝑋 and the speech-act variables 𝑌 are disjoint from Π, and a schema is written over atoms whose
arguments are these and name terms built from them, so no schema mentions an agent; and by
Definition 1 every agent begins in the same state 𝑠 0 = ∅. Permuting the agents therefore carries
the system to itself, which is Proposition 20, and no agent can reach a position another could not,
which is Corollary 21.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                                         25


(1) (1) An agreement of man with man. Only agents hold states, and by Proposition 19 an agent
    bears an effect of the contract only in a role; by Corollary 21 no agent can reach a position
    another could not. No schema can require that an agent be a natural person.
(2) (2) Reciprocal, (4) subject to no external authority. An agent consents twice and both consents
    are their own: adopting the contract is consent to its schemas, and willing an act in a guarding
    role is consent to that act. By Proposition 19 nothing binds an agent outside a role of a schema,
    so nothing binds them that their adoption did not, and every participant in an act takes a role
    in it, so no registry, no log and no timing authority takes part in one; by Corollary 21 no agent
    is privileged by the text. There is nothing else in the model to be subject to. That an agent is
    bound only by their own promise, condition (3), is Volition, Definition 13; Proudhon’s reciprocal
    delivery, that each agent has value for value, is a condition on the contract’s content and is not
    carried over.
(3) (5) Freely accepted and signed by all the participants. This is how a contract is entered into
    rather than what it says. Section 11 says how: each participant signs a one-sided undertaking
    to keep the contract and presents it to every participant it interacts with, so every participant
    has signed it and, in an instance of two or more, another participant holds it (Proposition 54);
    and by Proposition 19 nobody else is bound.
The remaining conditions do not hold for every contract. A contract with no act by which two
strangers can come into relation is written as speech-act schemas as readily as one that has such
an act, and fails Introduction. Section 3.8 states them as conditions on the schemas.

 condition                                   formal counterpart                                    established by
 (1) an agreement of man with man            every participant takes a role; no privileged agent   Proposition 19, Corollary 21
 (2) reciprocal                              every participant takes a role                        Proposition 19
 (3) no obligation but by personal promise volition, Definition 13                                 Proposition 29
 (4) subject to no external authority        every participant takes a role                        Proposition 19
 (5) freely accepted and signed by all       the undertaking, Section 11                           Proposition 54
 (6) of the nature of a contract of exchange an unobstructed introductory act, Definitions 9, 10   Proposition 28
 (7) people free to deal with each other     an unobstructed introductory act                      Proposition 28
 (8) no external register of people          traceable provenance, Definition 11                   Lemma 83

The counterpart of a condition in the table is structural. Proudhon’s reciprocal delivery, that each
agent has value for value, and exchange in its substantive sense are conditions on a contract’s
content, met by its clauses rather than by the form of its schemas.

3.8    What We Prove
Conditions (3), (6), (7) and (8) of Section 1 become three conditions on a contract’s schemas, and
the protocol realising a contract meeting them is grassroots. All three are properties of the text
and are decidable, so whether a contract meets them is decided by a checker rather than left to its
drafter. This section states them and the theorem; the sections that follow provide what is needed
to prove it, and Section 7 proves it.
   An agent’s state names agents: those they know, their friends, the issuers of the coins they hold,
the senders of the messages they hold. A state that names an agent stores an identifier of them, in
the words of Section 1, and every condition below is about which agents a state names.

   Definition 8 (Records). A local state records an agent 𝑞 if one of its atoms names 𝑞.

  The state {friend (𝑞)} of 𝑝 records 𝑞. A state holding received (𝑥, 𝑝) records 𝑝, who signed 𝑥; an
  agent signed inside 𝑥 is no argument of the atom and is not recorded.
26                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


   A speech act names its signer and nobody else. A state holding one records the signer; an agent
inside it, its earlier signers among them, is no argument of the atom carrying it and is not recorded:
the chain of signatures inside a speech act is carried rather than required. A contract records, and
its schemas can require, only the arguments of atoms and their signers, and an atom has finitely
many.
Introduction. Conditions (6) and (7): any two agents must be able to come into relation by an act
of the contract willed by both that leaves each recording the other, e.g. acquaintance.
   Definition 9 (Introductory act). A schema 𝜏 of 𝐶 of arity two, guarded in both roles, with no name
term and with literals for its body and head, is an introductory act if any two agents 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞 have an
introduction: an admissible binding 𝛽 of 𝜏 into {𝑝, 𝑞} that sends 𝜋1 to 𝑝 and 𝜋 2 to 𝑞, under which
some atom added at 𝜋1 names 𝑞 and some atom added at 𝜋2 names 𝑝.

     Acquaintance has arity two and guards in both roles, and at the binding sending Alice to 𝑝 and
     Bob to 𝑞 it adds knows(𝑞) at 𝑝 and knows(𝑝) at 𝑞, each naming the other agent. Befriending is
     an introductory act too, but not an unobstructed one: it requires knows of the other, which no
     schema of arity one adds.

  Having such an act is not enough, because it may be out of reach: it may require an atom an
agent cannot obtain, or forbid one that somebody else can put there and leave. The condition is
that neither happens.
   Definition 10 (Unobstructed). An introductory act 𝜏 of 𝐶 is unobstructed if any two agents 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞
have an introduction 𝛽 such that, at each role 𝜋𝑖 of 𝜏:
(1) every atom 𝜏 requires at 𝜋𝑖 is, under 𝛽, added by some schema of 𝐶 of arity one that is guarded
    in its role, has no name term, has literals for its body and head, requires and clears nothing,
    and forbids nothing or only that atom, which 𝜏 then requires once at 𝜋𝑖 , at some admissible
    binding that sends its role to 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 );
(2) no listed role of a schema of 𝐶 adds an atom whose predicate is that of an atom 𝜏 forbids at 𝜋𝑖 ,
    and every schema of 𝐶 that, at some binding, adds at 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) an atom 𝜏 forbids at 𝜋𝑖 has, at that
    binding, the other of 𝑝 and 𝑞 in a role that is not a reading role and adds at 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) an atom of its
    + naming them.
   The proof of Proposition 28 takes an introduction and supplies every atom it requires by schemas
of arity one, which is what the restrictions of Definitions 9 and 10 are for: a name term would need
a fresh name, a formula other than a literal a condition on states those schemas do not set, and a
constraint a binding satisfying it, whose existence is undecidable in general (Appendix C).

     Acquaintance requires identity at each role, once, which establish adds, establish having arity
     one, being guarded in its role, requiring nothing and forbidding only identity, so clause 1 holds;
     and it forbids nothing, so clause 2 asks nothing.

   The second clause concerns an atom somebody else can put in an agent’s state and leave there.
It excepts an act that puts it there while bringing the two agents together: its binding assigns
the other agent a role, and the atom it adds names that agent, so every transaction it determines
changes the state of both and leaves the first recording the second, and is an interaction between
them. Befriending at the binding that swaps its two roles is one, and so is any more guarded act
that specialises the introduction, e.g. a befriending that also takes the parents’ will. The clause
requires only that the act leave one of the two recording the other, which is enough for the act to
be an interaction between them, and is all the proof of Proposition 28 uses of it.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                 27


Provenance. Condition (8): a record may name an agent its holder has never dealt with: a coin
that changed hands, a message delivered to a group by its leader. Such a record must be traceable
to someone who was there: either the act that made it had that agent as a participant, or another
participant of the act already held a record naming them.
   Definition 11 (Traceable provenance). The base of an argument of a schema is the argument itself
where it is an agent role or an agent variable, and its base where it is a name term. Write Φ𝐸 for the
atoms of a schema whose predicate is in 𝐸. A set 𝐸 ⊆ Σ has traceable provenance in a contract 𝐶 if
for every schema 𝜏 of 𝐶 with roles 𝜋1, . . . , 𝜋𝑘 , every 𝑖, every 𝜑 ∈ +(𝑖) ∩ Φ𝐸 and every argument 𝑢
of 𝜑 that is an agent role, an agent variable or a name term, whose base is not that of 𝜋𝑖 ,
the base of 𝑢 is the base of some 𝜋 𝑗   or of an argument of some 𝜓 ∈ =( 𝑗)∩Φ𝐸 , 𝜋 𝑗 an agent role,
or a safe formula of 𝐵 names it through 𝐸, and every speech-act variable that is an argument of 𝜑
or occurs in a name term among its arguments is an argument of such a 𝜓 , occurs in a name term
among its arguments, is named by a safe formula of 𝐵 through 𝐸, or occurs in no atom required at
a role of 𝜏. A safe formula names a base, or a speech-act variable, through 𝐸 if it is an atom of Φ𝐸
held by an agent role an argument of which has that base, or has the variable among its arguments
or in a name term among them; a conjunction one conjunct of which names it, or one conjunct of
which is the constraint 𝑢 = 𝑣, of two agent variables, two speech-act variables or two name terms,
and another names 𝑣, or its base, 𝑢, or its base, being then named; a disjunction both disjuncts of
which name it; or ∃𝑥¯ (𝐴 ∧ 𝜑) with 𝐴 ∧ 𝜑 naming it and 𝑥¯ not binding it; for this the body is read as
one conjunction, of its literals, its safe formulas and its constraint. A signed term sgn(𝑦, 𝜋) among
the arguments of 𝜑 counts as the two arguments 𝑦 and 𝜋, here and in Definition 13. A predicate
other than an environment predicate (Appendix C) has traceable provenance in 𝐶 if it belongs to
such a set, and an atom is said to have it when its predicate does. A conditional atom added at an
agent role is read the same way (Definition 3), the anchor of its conjunct counting as an atom of =
at its holder.

  Delivery (Section 3.4) shows what the condition asks. It adds received_in(𝑥, Alice, 𝑛) at Bob:
  Alice is a role of it, and 𝑥 is an argument of posted (𝑥, 𝑛), which Alice requires, so the speech
  act reaches Bob through an agent that held it already.

  A predicate whose atoms are added only with roles among their arguments has traceable
provenance. The condition is weaker: an act may give an agent a record of someone they do not
yet record, provided another agent to that act records them already.
Volition. Condition (3): the agents to an act fall into parts connected by the records they hold, and
the act either has one such part, as unfriending and messaging between friends and paying by a
holder of the payee’s coins do, or is willed by an agent in each part.
   Definition 12 (Signature atoms). Let 𝜏 be a schema of 𝐶 with no guarding role. Atoms 𝜑 1, . . . , 𝜑𝑚
of +(𝑖) ⊎ −(𝑖), 𝑚 ≥ 1 and 𝜋𝑖 an agent role of 𝜏, are signature atoms of 𝜏 if every agent role of 𝜏
but 𝜋𝑖 is the base of an argument of one of them, and each 𝜑 𝑗 is either in +(𝑖), the only atom 𝜏
adds with its predicate, with no other schema of 𝐶 with no guarding role adding an atom with that
predicate, or in −(𝑖), the only atom 𝜏 deletes or clears with its predicate, with no other schema of 𝐶
with no guarding role deleting or clearing an atom with that predicate.
   Definition 13 (Volition). The role graph of a schema is the undirected graph on its roles with an
edge between 𝜋𝑖 and 𝜋 𝑗 when they have the same base or when one of the two requires an atom of
traceable provenance an argument of which has the base of the other. A contract 𝐶 satisfies volition
if every schema of it has a connected role graph or has a guarding agent role in every connected
28                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


component of its role graph, every reading role of a schema of it with a guarding role has an edge
to a guarding role from an atom that guarding role requires, or is a listed role whose base is a
guarding role, which its anchor names, and every schema of it with no guarding role and a reading
role has signature atoms and has only agent roles as reading roles.
   A guarding role in each component is an agent role, a listed role’s extent being possibly empty;
the proof of Proposition 29 uses this, and Lemma 17 the signature atoms and the agent reading
roles of a schema with no guarding role.

     The role graph of unfriend joins its two roles: Alice requires friend (Bob), an atom of traceable
     provenance with Bob among its arguments. Only Alice guards unfriending, so that edge is what
     its role graph rests on.

   The role graph is the undirected graph formed by the identifiers the agents store, of Section 1,
taken on the roles of one schema. For a schema of two agent roles it is connected exactly when one
of them requires such an atom naming the other. Above that arity connectedness is weaker than
requiring it of every pair, and Proposition 29 uses it: lying in one instance is an equivalence, so a
path through the roles puts all the participants there.
   Definition 14 (Syntactically grassroots). A contract is syntactically grassroots if it has an unob-
structed introductory act, the set of predicates occurring in it other than its environment predicates
has traceable provenance, and it satisfies volition.
   The denotation of a contract (Section 3.3) is defined for speech-act schemas, provided they have
traceable provenance and satisfy volition; it is a grassroots protocol where they are syntactically
grassroots, by the theorem below, and where they meet the conditions of Section 8.
   Theorem 15 (Grassroots realisation). The protocol realising a syntactically grassroots contract
is grassroots and volitionally grassroots.
   Sections 4 to 7 prove it.
   Whether a contract is syntactically grassroots is decidable, and a checker deciding it accompanies
this paper, at https://github.com/EShapiro2/GLP/tree/main/programs/gsc. It certifies GSN and GC,
with the sets of predicates of traceable provenance that Section 7 states. Everything the three
conditions quantify over is finite once the bindings are taken up to renaming: an introductory
act is an assignment of a schema’s agent variables to its two roles; unobstructed is the matching
of one atom against those the finitely many schemas add; volition is a connectivity check on the
roles, together with a comparison of the atoms the schemas with no guarding role add and delete.
Traceable provenance is not decided in a single pass: the largest set of predicates having it is reached
by dropping any predicate that fails the condition and repeating, which ends because there are
finitely many.
   Section 4 recalls what grassroots means and Section 5 says what the protocol realising a contract
is. Section 6 proves the theorem the proof rests on, and Section 7 proves this one.

4     VOLITION-GUARDED TRANSACTIONS AND GRASSROOTS PROTOCOLS
This section recalls from [51] what the statements below use, in brief. Nothing in it is ours. Appen-
dix A states the same notions formally, together with the results used in the proofs of Appendix B,
so that the paper is self-contained; the proofs of those results are in [51] and are not repeated.
Volition-guarded transactions follow multiagent transition systems [72] and multiagent atomic
transactions [82]. Throughout, Π is a countable, potentially infinite set of agents, an agent being a
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                   29


person operating a machine, and 𝑃 ⊂ Π ranges over its finite nonempty subsets. For a set S, S 𝑃 is
the set of total functions from 𝑃 to S, and 𝑐 𝑝 is the value of 𝑐 at 𝑝.
   A local-states function 𝑆 maps every 𝑃 ⊂ Π to a set 𝑆 (𝑃) of local machine states containing the
initial state 𝑠 0 , with 𝑃 ⊂ 𝑃 ′ implying 𝑆 (𝑃) ⊂ 𝑆 (𝑃 ′ ). A machine transaction over participants 𝑄 is
a pair 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ ∈ (S𝑄 ) 2 with 𝑑 ≠ 𝑑 ′ ; it is unary if |𝑄 | = 1. A volition-guarded transaction is a pair
(𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) of a machine transaction 𝑡 over 𝑄 with its guards 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄: the agents in 𝑄 ′ must be willing
for it to be taken. A transaction equivalence ∼ relates transactions with the same participants and
has countably many classes; an agent wills a class, and taking one member of it fulfils that will. For
a set 𝑅 of volition-guarded transactions over 𝑆 and 𝑃 ⊂ Π, we write 𝑅(𝑃) for those of them whose
transactions are over 𝑆 (𝑃 ′ ) for some 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑃, and call 𝑅 contained if every transaction of it with
participants in 𝑃 and source over 𝑆 (𝑃) is in 𝑅(𝑃).
   An agent state is a pair of a volitional state, a set of classes the agent is willing their machine to
take part in, and a machine state. A set of volition-guarded transactions over a local-states function
induces, for each 𝑃, a transition system whose configurations are agent configurations over 𝑃 and
whose transitions are volition changes, by which an agent by themselves alters their volitional state,
and volitional machine transactions, each induced by a volition-guarded transaction whose guards
all hold its class. A transaction is machine-enabled in a configuration whose machine states restrict
on the participants to its source, and enabled if in addition every guard holds its class. The family
of these systems, one per 𝑃, is the protocol F over the set, F (𝑃) being its member at 𝑃; runs are
safe when consecutive states form transitions, live when no class stays enabled forever without a
member being taken, and correct when both.
   A transition of F (𝑄) is an interaction between two agents if it changes the state of each and its
restriction to one of them is no transition of that agent’s own system; it is an interaction between
two disjoint sets of agents if it is one between a member of each. In the interaction graph of a run,
two agents are joined whenever some transition of the run is an interaction between them; an
instance in the run is a connected component of that graph, and two instances coalesce when they
come to lie in one component.
   A protocol is oblivious if any interleaving of correct runs of two disjoint sets of agents is a correct
run of the two together, interactive if two sets that have not yet interacted can always still do so, and
grassroots if it is both. An agent has left when no transaction they guard can be machine-enabled
again. A protocol is volitionally grassroots if it is oblivious, two sets each holding an agent that has
not left can always still interact, and the first interaction of two sets is induced by a transaction
guarded by a member of each, so instances coalesce only by mutual consent; a grassroots protocol
meeting the last condition is volitionally grassroots. In a grassroots protocol two instances can
always coalesce.

5   THE REALISATION OF A CONTRACT
A schema denotes volition-guarded transactions (Definition 7), and a contract the protocol over
them, which has the runs, the liveness and the grassroots notions of [51]. This section states that
protocol and what is proved of it before the three conditions. The protocol is defined for contracts
with traceable provenance that satisfy volition, and an app is compiled from a contract that is
certified besides, as syntactically grassroots or with wards (Section 8), so a contract is checked
before it is compiled. 𝑆𝐶 is a local-states function in the sense of Appendix A: 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) for every
𝑃, and 𝑃 ⊂ 𝑃 ′ implies 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) ⊂ 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃 ′ ), strictly, provided some predicate has positive arity, as one
does in every syntactically grassroots contract, its introductory act adding at 𝜋 1 an atom naming
𝜋2 : for 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 ′ \ 𝑃 and such a predicate, an atom with 𝑞 among its arguments gives a state in 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃 ′ )
and not in 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃).
30                                                      James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


5.1      The Compilation
Compilation prints the transactions a schema denotes in the notation of [51], the form inside
Definition 7, as the examples below show; the compiler of the repository prints it.

     With Alice and Bob standing for the agents in those roles, befriend (Section 3.4) denotes, in
     that notation,
                   ′                                           ′
                 𝑐 Alice := 𝑐 Alice ⊎ {friend (Bob)},        𝑐 Bob := 𝑐 Bob ⊎ {friend (Alice)},
                 provided identity, knows(Bob) ∈ 𝑐 Alice and identity, knows(Alice) ∈ 𝑐 Bob ,
                 provided friend (Bob) ∉ 𝑐 Alice and friend (Alice) ∉ 𝑐 Bob ,
                 guarded by {Alice, Bob},
     and denotes, for every 𝑑 with identity, knows(Bob) ∈ 𝑑 Alice , identity, knows(Alice) ∈ 𝑑 Bob ,
     friend (Bob) ∉ 𝑑 Alice and friend (Alice) ∉ 𝑑 Bob , the volition-guarded transaction (𝑑 →

     one for every admissible 𝑑, since the two may befriend whatever other friends each has, and ∼
     relates them all: they are one act of befriending, available at different points of a run.

     Write 𝐿 for ext𝑐 (Alice[listed (_, 𝑛)]). Close (Section 3.4) denotes

                  guarded by {Alice}.
     Alice lists herself, so she is in the extent of the listed role, and the act deletes her membership
     with her listing. Its participants are Alice and every member she lists, read from her own state,
     so no member is left out of the act.

     The swap (Section 3.6) denotes
                          ′
                        𝑐 Alice := (𝑐 Alice \ 𝑘 · ¢(𝑢)) ⊎ 𝑘 1 · ¢(𝑣) ⊎ {knows(𝑣)},
                          ′

                        provided identity, knows(Bob) ∈ 𝑐 Alice and 𝑘 · ¢(𝑢) ⊆ 𝑐 Alice ,
                        provided identity, knows(Alice) ∈ 𝑐 Bob and 𝑘 1 · ¢(𝑣) ⊆ 𝑐 Bob ,
                        guarded by {Alice, Bob}.
     The bindings with 𝑢 = 𝑣 determine nothing, by Definition 7: under them each role adds coins
     it deletes. The swap at 𝑢 = Alice and 𝑣 = Bob is the mutual credit line.

   The equivalence is on machine transactions, so two acts that no configuration distinguishes are
one act, and a contract whose act names carry a distinction of their own writes that distinction
into the effects. A contract compiles schema by schema. A schema with no guarding role compiles
to transactions with an empty guard, which are enabled whenever they are machine-enabled, and
one that stays enabled is eventually taken: no agent wills such an act. A clause saying that an act
is taken whenever it can be is rendered by such a schema, as a contract for feeds and followers,
in preparation, renders “Alice, who follows Carol’s feed through Bob, receives from Bob every
item of Carol’s feed that Bob holds”: Bob’s role is a reading role requiring that Bob holds the item,
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                  31


and Alice’s role adds the item and the record that it was received from Bob, a signature atom
(Definition 12).
  Definition 16 (Realisation of a contract). Let 𝐶 be a contract with traceable provenance that
satisfies volition. Its realisation 𝑅𝐶 is the set of volition-guarded transactions determined by the
schemas of 𝐶 at all admissible bindings for them, and ∼ is the equivalence on their underlying
machine transactions generated by relating two of the same participants when one schema at one
admissible binding determines both. The protocol realising 𝐶 is the volitional transactions-based
protocol over 𝑅𝐶 , 𝑆𝐶 and ∼.
   A set of volition-guarded transactions over 𝑆𝐶 with an equivalence ∼ is well formed if two
machine transactions of different classes, each underlying an unguarded transaction of it, have
disjoint 𝑃-closures for every 𝑃 ⊂ Π, and ∼ is a transaction equivalence with countably many classes
over any 𝑃, which is what Definitions 56 and 63 require.
  Lemma 17 (The compilation is well defined). 𝑅𝐶 with ∼ is well formed.

  Lemma 18 (Containment). 𝑅𝐶 is contained.

5.2   Every Participant Takes a Role
Throughout, 𝐶 is a syntactically grassroots contract, F the protocol realising it and 𝑃 ⊂ Π. No act
reaches anyone without a role in it.
   Proposition 19 (Every participant takes a role). Every agent whose machine state a transition
of F (𝑃) changes is in the extent of a role of a schema of 𝐶 at some binding.
   An agent therefore bears an effect of the contract only under a schema of it, and only in a role.
The second property of Section 3.7 is that the schemas single out no agent.
   A permutation 𝜎 of Π extends to speech acts by 𝜎 (𝑥, 𝑝) := (𝜎 (𝑥), 𝜎 (𝑝)), the identity on utterances,
to community names by 𝜎 (⟨𝑝⟩) := ⟨𝜎 (𝑝)⟩ and 𝜎 (𝜈 · 𝑦) := 𝜎 (𝜈) · 𝜎 (𝑦), to atoms by permuting their
arguments, and to states, configurations, transactions and runs componentwise.
  Proposition 20 (Anonymity). For every permutation 𝜎 of Π, 𝜎 (𝑅𝐶 ) = 𝑅𝐶 , 𝜎 (𝑐 0 (𝑃)) = 𝑐 0 (𝜎 (𝑃)),
and 𝜎 carries the runs of F (𝑃) to the runs of F (𝜎 (𝑃)).

  Corollary 21 (No privileged agent). If a configuration is reachable in a run over 𝑃, so is its
image under every permutation of 𝑃.
  Conditions (1), (2), (4) and (5) of Section 1 follow from these two properties, as Section 3.7 sets
out. That the protocol realising the contract is grassroots does not follow from them.

6 POLITE SETS OF VOLITION-GUARDED TRANSACTIONS
Here we prove that the following condition on a contained set of volition-guarded transactions
makes the protocol over it grassroots and volitionally grassroots: (1) any two agents can always
come to interact, and (2) no act joins two instances unless each of them holds a guard of it. Section 7
states conditions on the acts of a contract sufficient for its compiled volition-guarded transactions
to satisfy it, and GSN and GC (Section 2) satisfy it.
   The proofs use two graphs: the interaction graph of Section 4, undirected, whose connected
components are the instances; and the directed graph of who holds a record of whom, defined next.
   Throughout this section, 𝑆 is a local-states function, 𝑅 a set of volition-guarded transactions
over 𝑆 with equivalence ∼, and F the protocol over 𝑅, 𝑆 and ∼. The proofs, in Appendix B, use
four results of [51] recalled in Appendix A: obliviousness rests on Lemmas 75 and 74 through
32                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


Proposition 76, and interactivity on Lemma 60; openness yields interactivity and closure yields
consent.
  Definition 22 (Records). A machine state 𝑠 records an agent 𝑞 ∈ Π if 𝑠 ∉ 𝑆 (𝑃) for every 𝑃 ⊂ Π
with 𝑞 ∉ 𝑃.
   Definition 23 (Recording graph). The recording graph of a machine configuration 𝑑 is the directed
graph on Π with an edge 𝑝 → 𝑞 whenever 𝑑𝑝 records 𝑞. The recording graph of an agent configu-
ration is that of its machine states, and the recording graph of a run is the union of the recording
graphs of its configurations.
  Definition 24 (Mutual recording). A volition-guarded transaction (𝑑 → 𝑑 ′, {𝑝, 𝑞}) over partic-
ipants {𝑝, 𝑞}, 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞 ∈ Π, is a mutual recording of 𝑝 and 𝑞 if it changes the state of each and the
recording graph of 𝑑 ′ has the edges 𝑝 → 𝑞 and 𝑞 → 𝑝.
   A state records 𝑞 when it could not have arisen among agents that leave 𝑞 out: every set of
agents whose states include it contains 𝑞. The recording graph of the initial configuration is empty,
since 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆 (𝑃) for every 𝑃. In GKK (Section 2.1) the edges out of an agent are those they know,
in GSG their friends too, and in GC (Section 3.6) the issuers of the coins they hold. A mutual
recording is an act of two agents, willed by both, after which there is an edge each way between
them: acquaintance is one, and so is befriending. An edge may point at an agent its source has
never transacted with, as when a message is delivered to a group by its leader or a coin changes
hands, and nothing below forbids it. The recording graph of a configuration is not monotone along
a run, as an act may delete the atoms that put an edge there; the recording graph of a run and the
interaction graph are monotone by construction.
     Definition 25 (Open, Closed, Polite). The set 𝑅 is:
(1) open if for any two agents 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞 in any 𝑄 ⊂ Π, every finite safe run of F (𝑄) with no interaction
    between 𝑝 and 𝑞 has a finite safe extension, by volition changes and transitions that are not
    interactions between 𝑝 and 𝑞, at whose end some transaction of 𝑅(𝑄) whose induced transitions
    are interactions between 𝑝 and 𝑞 is machine-enabled;
(2) closed if for every 𝑄 ⊂ Π, every transaction of 𝑅 enabled at the end of a finite safe run of F (𝑄)
    has all its participants in one instance in that run or a guard in every instance in that run
    holding one of them;
(3) polite if it is open and closed.
   Openness says that any two agents can always be brought, by zero or more acts that are not
between the two, to a configuration at which an act coupling the two can be taken if all its guards
are willing. It does not require that no one else take part in the act: a mutual recording is one, and
so is any act that leaves one of them holding a record of the other, whoever else takes part. Of
two agents that have already taken part in one act nothing is required: they have interacted, and
being interactive requires no more. Closure says that an act whose participants lie in two or more
instances is enabled only where each of those instances holds a guard of it, and an act no one wills
only where they already lie in one instance.
  Theorem 26 (Polite). The protocol over a contained, polite set of volition-guarded transactions is
grassroots and volitionally grassroots.
    Politeness is a condition on the transactions of a protocol. Being grassroots is a property of its
runs. The theorem states that the first is sufficient for the second. Section 7 proves the step before
it, from the acts of a contract to the transactions realising them.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                 33


7     CONDITIONS SUFFICIENT FOR OPENNESS AND CLOSURE
We are now ready to prove Theorem 15, restated. Openness and closure are conditions on runs;
the three conditions are on the schemas, and the proof has two halves: openness follows from the
unobstructed introductory act, and closure from volition and traceable provenance. The proofs are
in Appendix B. Throughout this section 𝐶 is a syntactically grassroots contract and F the protocol
realising it.
   Theorem 15 (Grassroots realisation). The protocol realising a syntactically grassroots contract
is grassroots and volitionally grassroots.

    Lemma 27 (Records). On local states, Definitions 22 and 8 agree.

    Proposition 28 (Openness). 𝑅𝐶 is open.

    Proposition 29 (Closure). 𝑅𝐶 is closed.
  Proof of Theorem 15. Let 𝐶 be syntactically grassroots. By Proposition 28 𝑅𝐶 is open and by
Proposition 29 it is closed, so it is polite; by Lemma 18 it is contained; and by Theorem 26 the
protocol over it is grassroots and volitionally grassroots.                                    □

Openness and closure. Openness lets any agent start an instance, and without permission. A
contract that gives one agent of each instance a standing of their own is not open: two instances
with no such agent can never coalesce. Federated architectures fail this condition, a server being
such an agent [51].
   Closure leaves an instance requiring no global resource and no central authority: a registry
that issues identifiers, a log that orders acts, or an authority that timestamps them takes part in a
transaction without a role in it. Every act of a grassroots contract is between its agents, so no third
party takes part in one. A smart contract that depends on a fact outside the blockchain obtains it
from an oracle, a third party whose report the contract cannot check [10]; here a fact about the
world enters as an agent’s signed assertion. A smart contract requires a consensus protocol; a
contract realised as volition-guarded transactions requires none where the agents transact between
themselves [39, 50], and where a collective decision is called for it constitutes its own consensus
among the agents [46].

7.1    The Social Network Certified
Acquaint, which has no name term, is an introductory act: for any two agents, at the binding
sending Alice and Bob to them, every transaction it determines adds knows(Bob) at Alice and
knows(Alice) at Bob, changing both states, and it is guarded in both roles, so each is a mutual
recording of the two. It is unobstructed: it requires identity at each role, once, which establish adds,
establish having arity one and no name term, being guarded in its role, requiring nothing and
forbidding only identity; and it forbids nothing. Acquaint and introduce are the schemas adding a
knows atom: acquaint adds at each role an atom naming the other role, and introduce adds knows(𝑐)
at Bob, 𝑐 an argument of knows(𝑐), which Alice requires; so knows has traceable provenance, and
so has friend, befriend being the only schema adding a friend atom and that atom’s argument
a role. So has the set of all ten predicates of the contract: establish adds identity, which has no
arguments; message adds received (𝑥, Alice) at Bob, whose arguments are the role Alice and the
speech-act variable 𝑥, which occurs in no atom required at a role; and the schemas of a group meet
the condition, as the paragraph below shows.
34                                            James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


  Volition holds, by Definition 13: acquaint and befriend are guarded in both their roles; establish
has arity one; introduce’s role Alice requires knows(Bob), and the role Alice of unfriend and of
message requires friend (Bob), each an atom of traceable provenance naming the other role; and
the schemas of a group meet it, as the paragraph below shows.
The schemas of a group. Create adds atoms whose arguments are its role, the name ⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛
based on it, and a speech-act variable it requires nowhere; join and close add atoms whose ar-
guments are roles and that name; post_own and post add posted (𝑥, 𝑛) at the leader, 𝑥 required
nowhere; deliver adds delivered (𝑥, Bob, 𝑛) at the leader and received_in(𝑥, Alice, 𝑛) at the mem-
ber, whose arguments are roles, 𝑛 and 𝑥, which the posted (𝑥, 𝑛) required at the leader carries;
deliver_leader adds received_in(𝑥, Alice, 𝑛) at the leader, whose arguments are its role, 𝑛 and 𝑥,
which the posted (𝑥, 𝑛) it requires carries; and leave, remove and unfriend add nothing, so the six
predicates of a group have traceable provenance, each traced through an agent role. Join is guarded
in both its roles; create, post_own and deliver_leader have arity one; the two roles of close have the
base Alice; and the role Alice of leave, remove and deliver requires listed (Bob, 𝑛) and the role Bob
of post requires member (⟨Alice⟩ · 𝑛), each an atom of traceable provenance an argument of which
has the base of the other role, so volition holds of them. Acquaint forbids nothing, so clause 2 of
Definition 10 asks nothing of the listed role of close.
   The contract is therefore syntactically grassroots, and by Theorem 15 the protocol realising it is
grassroots and volitionally grassroots, so the platform realising the contract is a grassroots platform.
So is GFP: sign adds item(𝑥) at Alice, 𝑥 required nowhere; forward adds item(sgn(𝑥, Alice)) at Bob,
whose arguments are the role Alice and 𝑥, which the item(𝑥) Alice requires carries, so item has
traceable provenance; sign has arity one and forward’s role Alice requires friend (Bob), so volition
holds; and acquaint is its unobstructed introductory act as in GKK.
The instances. The introductory act is acquaintance, and a knows atom was added by an acquaint,
a mutual recording and so an interaction by Lemma 81, or by an introduce, an interaction of the two
it joins, the one introduced being known to the introducer already; every friend atom was added
by a befriend, a mutual recording. Every interaction of this contract between two agents requires
or adds an atom in the state of one of them naming the other, so it puts an edge of the recording
graph between them; and by Lemma 83, every edge of the recording graph joins two agents in one
instance. The instances of a run are therefore the connected components of its recording graph,
taken undirected; under GSG every friend edge was willed by both its ends, so the social graph
is a web of trust and its instances are its connected components. A message adds an edge from
the recipient to the sender, a participant; a delivery adds one from the member to the leader, a
participant, and one to the member who posted the item, who signed it, reached through the leader
(Lemma 83). A coin is different: it names its issuer, who may never have transacted with its holder
(Section 7.2).

7.2   The Coins Certified
Acquaint is GC’s introductory act, unobstructed, as in GKK (Section 7.1); the swap is an introductory
act too, at the binding sending 𝑢 to Alice and 𝑣 to Bob leaving each holding a coin the other issued,
the mutual credit line, but not an unobstructed one, requiring at each role knows of the other and a
coin, which no schema of arity one that requires nothing adds, mint requiring identity.
   ¢ has traceable provenance, as identity and knows have in GKK; the swap and redeem add knows
of the issuer of the coin a role receives, the coin an atom the other role requires. Mint adds ¢(Alice)
at Alice and pay adds ¢(Bob) at Bob, each naming the role that holds it; the swap adds in each role a
coin the other role requires; and redeem adds ¢(𝑟 ) at Alice, which role Bob requires, and ¢(Bob) at
Bob, naming its own role. Volition holds, by Definition 13: mint has arity one, the swap is guarded
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                 35


in both its roles, and pay and redeem are guarded at Alice only and require there knows(Bob)
and ¢(Bob), atoms of traceable provenance having Bob among their arguments. The contract is
therefore syntactically grassroots, and by Theorem 15 the protocol realising it is grassroots and
volitionally grassroots, so the platform realising the contract is a grassroots platform.
The origin of a record. A coin names its issuer, who is not a participant of the payment that
moves it, so a ¢ edge may point at an agent its source has never transacted with, where a friend
edge can only have been added by the two agents it joins. Traceable provenance covers it: an edge
is added either to a participant of the act that adds it or to an agent another participant of that act
records already, so no act ever leaves an edge whose ends lie in different instances, which Lemma 83
proves by induction on the run. For coins the chain is the passage of the coin from its issuer, and
conservation of money [51] is the special case, here an invariant of the schemas rather than one
proved of the runs.

8   CONTRACTS WITH WARDS
A ward comes to know an agent by an act of her own, acquaintance being for persons, so a
contract with wards has no one act that any two agents can take and is not syntactically grassroots
(Section 3.5). The protocol realising it is volitionally grassroots nonetheless: a ward enters the
contract with a guardian recorded, who is a person, and while she has a guardian comes to know
any agent by an act of the two; and a ward with no guardian has left (Definition 77). This section
states conditions on the schemas under which a contract with wards is certified, decidable as those
of Section 3.8 are and decided by the same checker, states that they are sufficient (Theorem 36)
and certifies GnW by them. The conditions say what an agent is and holds, and none says what
an agent is not: an act of a person requires that its agents be persons, and mentions no ward. A
contract that confines a ward to the agents her guardian knows, as the child-safe social network
does [79], writes its acts of knowing with the guardian among their roles, and the definitions admit
both. The proofs are in Appendix B.
Reflexive predicates. Two agent roles a schema marks as possibly one are bound to one agent
only if neither adds or deletes an atom naming the other (Definition 5), so no relation a contract
records is reflexive. A contract may declare a predicate of arity one reflexive: every state is then
taken to hold the atom of that predicate naming its holder, and no schema adds, deletes or clears, at
a role, the atom of it naming that role. For a configuration 𝑑 and an agent 𝑝, 𝑑ˆ𝑝 is 𝑑𝑝 together with
the atom naming 𝑝 of every reflexive predicate, and Definition 7 reads 𝑑ˆ𝑝 for 𝑑𝑝 in what it asks of a
state: what is required, what is forbidden and the safe formulas of the body. In a contract declaring
none, 𝑑ˆ𝑝 = 𝑑𝑝 . Definition 10 then excepts, in clause 1, an atom of a reflexive predicate naming 𝜋𝑖 ,
and asks besides that 𝜏 forbid no such atom at 𝜋𝑖 . GnW declares friend and knows reflexive, so that
two wards with one guardian may befriend by an act in which that guardian takes both guardian
roles (GnW:4).
  Lemma 30 (Reflexive predicates). Lemmas 18 and 83 and Propositions 28 and 29 hold for a
contract declaring reflexive predicates, with Definition 10 read as above.

Kinds. A mark is a predicate of arity zero in a state, as identity, and its atom is written as the mark.
The declaration of a contract says what each of its names is, a person or a ward, and a contract
with wards has a mark for each: an agent holds the mark of its kind from the act that establishes its
identity.
  Definition 31 (Kinds). 𝐶 has kinds (𝜄, 𝑓 , 𝑀), for marks 𝜄 and 𝑓 and a nonempty set 𝑀 of marks
not holding them, if:
36                                                James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


(1) no schema of 𝐶 deletes 𝜄 or 𝑓 ;
(2) a schema that adds 𝜄 at a role forbids it there and adds there 𝑓 or one mark in 𝑀, and adds no
    other of them there; a schema adds a mark in 𝑀 only so, and at one role; and it adds 𝑓 only so,
    or at a role at which it deletes a mark in 𝑀;
(3) a schema that deletes a mark in 𝑀 does so at one role, and adds 𝑓 there;
(4) 𝐶 has an establishing act, a schema of arity one, guarded in its role, with no name term and with
    literals for its body and head, that adds 𝜄 and 𝑓 , requires and clears nothing and forbids only 𝜄.
An agent holding 𝑓 is free, and one holding 𝑚 ∈ 𝑀 a ward of the kind 𝑚.
  In GnW 𝜄 is identity, 𝑓 is person and 𝑀 = {ward}: establish adds identity and person, and enter
adds identity and ward at the ward. An agent with an identity is of one kind (Lemma 84), and a
ward becomes a person only by an act that deletes her mark, as a child comes of age [79].
Guardianship and relay. A ward holds a record of each of her guardians, and each guardian a
record of her; a guardian is a person.
  Definition 32 (Guardianship). Let 𝐶 have kinds (𝜄, 𝑓 , 𝑀). A guardianship of 𝑚 ∈ 𝑀 in 𝐶 is (𝑚, 𝑣, 𝑢),
with 𝑣 and 𝑢 predicates whose first argument is an agent and whose other arguments, if any, are
values, their atoms written 𝑣 (𝜌, 𝑧)
                                  ¯ and 𝑢 (𝜎, 𝑧),
                                              ¯ such that:
(1) no schema of 𝐶 clears an atom of 𝑣, and a schema clears an atom of 𝑢 only with a wildcard for
    each of its arguments;
(2) a schema that deletes 𝑚 at a role requires an atom of 𝑢 there;
(3) a schema adds 𝑢 (𝜎, 𝑧)¯ at a role 𝜌 if and only if it adds 𝑣 (𝜌, 𝑧)
                                                                     ¯ at 𝜎, a role of it at which it requires
    𝑓 ; and it then adds 𝑚 at 𝜌, or requires 𝑚 at 𝜌 and forbids 𝑢 (𝜎, 𝑧)  ¯ there, and, unless 𝑚 is passive,
    requires an atom of 𝑢 there;
(4) a schema deletes 𝑢 (𝜎, 𝑧)¯ at a role 𝜌 if and only if it deletes 𝑣 (𝜌, 𝑧)
