A     THE FRAMEWORK OF VOLITION-GUARDED MULTIAGENT ATOMIC
      TRANSACTIONS
This appendix states formally what Section 4 recalls in brief, so that the statements and proofs
of Section 6 can be read without recourse to another paper. Everything in it is from [51], and the
proofs of its results are there and are not repeated. It leaves out the volition-guarded if-then-else
transactions of [51], which compiled schemas do not produce.
   Earlier work introduced multiagent transition systems [72], grassroots protocols and plat-
forms [74], and their definition via multiagent atomic transactions [82], which describe the be-
haviour of machines but not of the people operating them. A volition-guarded transaction is a
machine transaction guarded by the volitions of some, all, or none of the agents whose machines
participate; an agent’s volitional state is a set of equivalence classes of machine transactions they
are willing their machine to take part in. The social graph illustrates the two extremes: befriending
is guarded by both agents, while unfriending is guarded by either. An agent may freely change
their volitional state; an equivalence class is removed from every agent’s volitional state when a
machine transaction in that class is taken, the will having been fulfilled.

A.1    Agents, Machines and Volition-Guarded Transactions
We assume a countable, potentially infinite set of agents Π, an agent being a person operating a
machine, but consider only finite subsets of it, so when referring to a particular set of agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π
we assume 𝑃 to be nonempty and finite. We use ⊂ to denote the strict subset relation and ⊆ when
54                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


equality is also possible, and use 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 as a shorthand for 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 ∧ 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 ∧ 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞. As standard,
we use 𝑆 𝑃 to denote the set of all total functions from 𝑃 to 𝑆, and if 𝑐 ∈ 𝑆 𝑃 we use 𝑐 𝑝 to denote the
value of 𝑐 at 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃.
   Definition 55 (Machine State, Configuration, Transaction, Volition-Guarded Transaction [51]). Given
an arbitrary set 𝑆 of machine states, with a designated initial state 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆, and agents 𝑄 ⊂ Π, a
machine configuration over 𝑄 is a member of 𝑆 𝑄 , and a machine transaction over participants 𝑄 is a
pair 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ ∈ (𝑆 𝑄 ) 2 such that 𝑐 ≠ 𝑐 ′ ; it is unary if |𝑄 | = 1. Given such a machine transaction 𝑡, a
volition-guarded multiagent atomic transaction over 𝑡—henceforth, volition-guarded transaction—is
a pair (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) where 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 are its guards.
    Machine transactions are atomic and asynchronous [72]: they can be carried out by their par-
ticipants at any time, regardless of the states of non-participants. Participants include both active
agents, whose state changes, and stationary agents, whose state is a precondition but does not
change. Volition-guarded transactions can be carried out only if their guards 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 are willing,
and do not distinguish between agents that initiate a transaction and those willing to take part in
it. When we say a transaction is guarded by {𝑝, 𝑞}, both must be willing; when we say it is guarded
by either 𝑝 or 𝑞, we mean there are two volition-guarded transactions over the same machine
transaction, (𝑡, {𝑝}) and (𝑡, {𝑞}), so that either agent’s volition suffices.
    Distinct machine transactions can represent the same act in different configurations; a transaction
equivalence relates them.
  Definition 56 (Transaction Equivalence [51]). Given a set of machine transactions 𝑅, a transaction
equivalence is an equivalence relation ∼ on 𝑅 such that 𝑡 ∼ 𝑡 ′ implies 𝑡 and 𝑡 ′ have the same
participants, and 𝑅/∼ is countable. We write [𝑡] for the equivalence class of 𝑡 under ∼.
   All befriend transactions of 𝑝 and 𝑞, differing only in the configurations in which they occur,
form one equivalence class. An agent wills a class, and taking one member of it fulfils that will.
   Definition 57 (Agent State and Configuration [51]). Given agents 𝑃, states 𝑆 with initial state 𝑠 0 , a
set of machine transactions 𝑇 each over its own participants 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 and 𝑆, and equivalence ∼ on 𝑇 ,
an agent state is a pair (𝑉 , 𝑚) ∈ A = (2𝑇 /∼ × 𝑆) where 𝑉 is its volitional state and 𝑚 ∈ 𝑆 its machine
state. The initial agent state is (∅, 𝑠 0 ). An agent configuration 𝑐 over 𝑃, 𝑆, 𝑇 , and ∼ is a member
𝑐 ∈ A 𝑃 in which 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 ⊆ (𝑇 /∼)𝑝 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, where (𝑇 /∼)𝑝 denotes the classes in 𝑇 /∼ in which
𝑝 is a participant; we write 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 for the volitional state and 𝑐𝑚
                                                                𝑝 for the machine state of agent 𝑝 in 𝑐.

  Definition 58 (Volitional Transaction [51]). Given agents 𝑃, states 𝑆, machine transactions 𝑇 over
𝑃 and 𝑆, and equivalence ∼ on 𝑇 :
(1) A volition change of agent 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 is a pair 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ of agent configurations over {𝑝}, 𝑆, 𝑇 , and ∼
    such that 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 , 𝑐 ′𝑣𝑝 ⊆ (𝑇 /∼)𝑝 and 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 ≠ 𝑐 ′𝑣𝑝 , and 𝑐𝑚    ′𝑚
                                                             𝑝 =𝑐 𝑝.
(2) A volitional machine transaction induced by a volition-guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ), for some
    𝑡 = (𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ ) ∈ 𝑇 over 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 with guards 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄, is a pair 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ where 𝑐 ≠ 𝑐 ′ are agent
    configurations over 𝑃, 𝑆, 𝑇 , and ∼ such that [𝑡] ∈ 𝑐𝑞𝑣 for every 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄 ′ ; 𝑐𝑚               ′𝑚    ′
                                                                                     𝑝 = 𝑑𝑝 and 𝑐 𝑝 = 𝑑𝑝 for
    every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄; 𝑐𝑚        = 𝑐 ′𝑚 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 \ 𝑄; and 𝑐 ′𝑣 = 𝑐 𝑣 \ {[𝑡]} for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃.
                       𝑝        𝑝                               𝑝    𝑝
(3) A volitional transaction is a volition change or a volitional machine transaction.
  When a volitional machine transaction induced by (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) is taken, the class [𝑡] is removed from
every agent’s volitional state: the will is fulfilled by any equivalent transaction. An agent may
independently change their volitional state via volition changes, which may add or remove classes;
beyond these, the framework removes a class from 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 only upon fulfilment.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                          55


A.2    Transition Systems and the Induced Volitional System
   Definition 59 (Transition System, Computation, Run, Safe, Live, Correct [51]). A transition system
is a tuple 𝑇 𝑆 = (𝑆, 𝑠 0,𝑇 , ∼), where:
(1) 𝑆 is an arbitrary non-empty set, referred to as the set of states.
(2) Some 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆 is the designated initial state.
(3) 𝑇 ⊆ 𝑆 2 is a set of correct transitions over 𝑆, where each transition 𝑡 ∈ 𝑇 is a pair (𝑠, 𝑠 ′ ) of
     non-identical states 𝑠 ≠ 𝑠 ′ ∈ 𝑆, also written as 𝑡 = 𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ .
(4) ∼ is a partial equivalence relation on 𝑇 : a symmetric and transitive relation on 𝑇 , not necessarily
     reflexive. Its domain {𝑡 ∈ 𝑇 : 𝑡 ∼ 𝑡 } is partitioned into countably many liveness classes 𝑇 /∼; a
     transition outside the domain belongs to no class.
A computation of 𝑇 𝑆 is a nonempty, potentially infinite sequence of states 𝑟 = 𝑠 1, 𝑠 2, · · · ; it is a run of
𝑇 𝑆 if 𝑠 1 = 𝑠 0 . A prefix of a computation is a finite initial segment of it. A computation 𝑟 = 𝑠 1, 𝑠 2, . . .
is safe, also written 𝑟 ⊆ 𝑇 , if 𝑠𝑖 → 𝑠𝑖+1 ∈ 𝑇 for every two consecutive states. A class [𝑡] ∈ 𝑇 /∼ is
enabled in a state 𝑠 if 𝑠 → 𝑠 ′ ∈ [𝑡] for some 𝑠 ′ ∈ 𝑆. A run 𝑟 is live if no class [𝑡] ∈ 𝑇 /∼ is enabled in
every state of some suffix of 𝑟 with no member of [𝑡] occurring in the suffix. A run is correct if it is
safe and live.
   A partial equivalence, rather than a total one, is used so that some transitions may carry no
liveness requirement: only transitions in the domain of ∼ form classes and thereby incur a liveness
obligation, while transitions outside the domain, belonging to no class, may occur in a correct run
but are never required to.
   Lemma 60 (Extension [51]). Every finite safe run of a transition system is a prefix of a correct run
of it.
   Definition 61 (Multiagent Transition System [51]). Given agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π and an arbitrary set 𝑆 of
states with a designated initial state 𝑠 0 ∈ 𝑆, a multiagent transition system over 𝑃 and 𝑆 is a transition
system 𝑇 𝑆 = (𝐶, 𝑐 0,𝑇 , ∼) with configurations 𝐶 := 𝑆 𝑃 , initial configuration 𝑐 0 := {𝑠 0 }𝑃 , transitions
𝑇 ⊆ 𝐶 2 a set of transactions over 𝑃 and 𝑆, and ∼ a partial equivalence on 𝑇 .
   Rather than specifying a multiagent transition system over a set of agents 𝑃 directly, it is specified
via machine transactions. A machine transaction over 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 defines a set of multiagent transitions
over 𝑃 in which all members of 𝑃 \ 𝑄 are stationary.
    Definition 62 (Transaction Closure [51]). Let 𝑃 ⊂ Π, 𝑆 a set of machine states, and 𝐶 := 𝑆 𝑃 . For
any transition or transaction 𝑡 = 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ , we write 𝑡𝑞 := 𝑐𝑞 → 𝑐𝑞′ and say 𝑝 is stationary in 𝑡 if
𝑐 𝑝 = 𝑐 𝑝′ . For a machine transaction 𝑡 = (𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ ) over 𝑆 with participants 𝑄, the 𝑃-closure of 𝑡, 𝑡↑𝑃,
is the set of transitions over 𝑃 and 𝑆 defined by:
                   (
                     {𝑡 ′ ∈ 𝐶 2 : ∀𝑞 ∈ 𝑄.(𝑡𝑞 = 𝑡𝑞′ ) ∧ ∀𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 \ 𝑄.(𝑝 is stationary in 𝑡 ′ )} if 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃
          𝑡↑𝑃 :=
                     ∅                                                                       otherwise
If 𝑅 is a set of machine transactions, each 𝑡 ∈ 𝑅 over some 𝑄 and 𝑆, then the 𝑃-closure of 𝑅, 𝑅↑𝑃, is
                                                       Ð
the set of transitions over 𝑃 and 𝑆 defined by 𝑅↑𝑃 := 𝑡 ∈𝑅 𝑡↑𝑃. Given a relation ∼ on 𝑅, its 𝑃-closure
∼↑𝑃 is the relation on 𝑅↑𝑃 with 𝑡ˆ (∼↑𝑃) 𝑡ˆ′ iff 𝑡ˆ ∈ 𝑡↑𝑃 and 𝑡ˆ′ ∈ 𝑡 ′ ↑𝑃 for some 𝑡 ∼ 𝑡 ′ . A transition
over 𝑃 is unary if it lies in the 𝑃-closure of a unary transaction.
   If distinct transactions in 𝑅 have disjoint 𝑃-closures, as when every participant of every trans-
action in 𝑅 changes state, then each transition in 𝑅↑𝑃 has a unique inducing transaction, ∼↑𝑃
relates two transitions exactly when their inducing transactions are ∼-related, and ∼↑𝑃 is a partial
equivalence whenever ∼ is. For volition-guarded transactions, disjointness is required only of
unguarded ones of different classes (Definition 63).
56                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


   Definition 63 (Volitional Multiagent Transition System [51]). Given agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π, machine states
𝑆 with initial state 𝑠 0 , a set 𝑅 of volition-guarded transactions such that every (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅 has the
participants of 𝑡 contained in 𝑃, and an equivalence ∼ on the set 𝑇𝑅 := {𝑡 : (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅 for some 𝑄 ′ }
of underlying machine transactions such that two of them of different classes, each underlying an
unguarded transaction of 𝑅, have disjoint 𝑃-closures, the volitional multiagent transition system
induced by (𝑆, 𝑅, ∼) over 𝑃 is the multiagent transition system (A 𝑃 , 𝑐 0,𝑇𝑉 , ∼𝑉 ) where:
(1) A := 2𝑇𝑅 /∼ × 𝑆 is the agent state space;
(2) 𝑐 0 ∈ A 𝑃 is the initial agent configuration, with 𝑐 0 𝑝𝑣 = ∅ and 𝑐 0𝑚𝑝 = 𝑠 0 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃;
(3) 𝑇𝑉 consists of all transitions 𝑒 → 𝑒 ′ ∈ (A 𝑃 ) 2 of one of two forms: (i) a volition change of some
    𝑝 ∈ 𝑃—𝑒𝑝𝑣 , 𝑒 ′𝑣𝑝 ⊆ (𝑇𝑅 /∼)𝑝 and 𝑒𝑝𝑣 ≠ 𝑒 ′𝑣𝑝 , 𝑒𝑝𝑚 = 𝑒 ′𝑚             ′
                                                            𝑝 , and 𝑒𝑟 = 𝑒𝑟 for every 𝑟 ∈ 𝑃 \ {𝑝}; or (ii) a
    volitional machine transaction induced by some volition-guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅 per
    Definition 58(2);
(4) ∼𝑉 is the restriction of ∼↑𝑃 (Definition 62) to the volitional machine transactions, relating
    two of them whenever their inducing machine transactions are ∼-equivalent, and leaves every
    volition-change transition outside its domain, so volition changes belong to no class and carry
    no liveness obligation.
   Every volitional machine transaction has ∼-equivalent inducing machine transactions, so ∼𝑉
is a partial equivalence. A step induced by a guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ), 𝑄 ′ ≠ ∅, removes [𝑡]
from the volitional state of each guard, which held it, and a step of another class removes another
class, so two guarded transactions of different classes may share their 𝑃-closures; two unguarded
transactions of different classes have no transition in common, by the condition on 𝑃-closures.
    Definition 64 (Enabled [51]). Given a set of volition-guarded transactions, each (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) with
𝑡 = 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ a machine transaction over some 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 and 𝑆 with guards 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄, and an equivalence
∼ on machine transactions: the volition-guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) is machine-enabled in agent
configuration 𝑐 over 𝑃 if 𝑐𝑚𝑝 = 𝑑𝑝 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄, and enabled if in addition [𝑡] ∈ 𝑐 𝑞 for every
                                                                                       𝑣

𝑞 ∈ 𝑄 . An equivalence class [𝑡] is enabled in 𝑐 if some volition-guarded transaction (𝑡 ′, 𝑄 ′ ) with
       ′

𝑡 ′ ∈ [𝑡] is enabled in 𝑐.
  A volition-guarded transaction with an empty guard requires no volitions and is enabled when-
ever its machine precondition is met. A class of volitional machine transactions is enabled at a
configuration in the sense of Definition 59 exactly when some volition-guarded transaction induc-
ing it is enabled in the above sense, and this determines which runs are live and correct. Volition
changes belong to no class and so impose no liveness obligation; personal choices remain free.

A.3   Protocols, Grassroots Protocols and Coalescence
A protocol is a family of multiagent transition systems, one for each set of agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π, which
share an underlying set of local machine states S with a designated initial state 𝑠 0 . A local-states
function maps every set of agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π to a set of local machine states 𝑆 (𝑃) ⊂ S that includes 𝑠 0
and satisfies 𝑃 ⊂ 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π =⇒ 𝑆 (𝑃) ⊂ 𝑆 (𝑃 ′ ).
   Definition 65 (Protocol [51]). A protocol F over a local-states function 𝑆 is a family of multiagent
transition systems that has exactly one transition system F (𝑃) = (𝐶 (𝑃), 𝑐 0 (𝑃),𝑇 (𝑃), ∼ (𝑃)) for
every 𝑃 ⊂ Π, with agent states A (𝑃), configurations 𝐶 (𝑃) := A (𝑃) 𝑃 , initial configuration 𝑐 0 (𝑃) ∈
𝐶 (𝑃), and partial equivalence ∼ (𝑃) on 𝑇 (𝑃) determined by the protocol, such that 𝑃 ⊆ 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π
implies A (𝑃) ⊆ A (𝑃 ′ ) and 𝑐 0 (𝑃)𝑝 = 𝑐 0 (𝑃 ′ )𝑝 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃.
   In a grassroots protocol two disjoint sets of agents can each operate independently — their
interleaved correct runs are correct runs of the combined system — yet the combined system offers
behaviours that neither set could produce by itself.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                                57


   Definition 66 (Interleaving [51]). Let 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π be disjoint nonempty sets of agents, 𝑟 = 𝑐 0, 𝑐 1, . . .
a run of F (𝑃), and 𝑟 ′ = 𝑑 0, 𝑑 1, . . . a run of F (𝑃 ′ ). An interleaving of 𝑟 and 𝑟 ′ is a sequence 𝑒 0, 𝑒 1, . . .
of configurations in 𝐶 (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ) for which there exist non-decreasing sequences of indices (𝑖𝑘 )𝑘 ≥0
and ( 𝑗𝑘 )𝑘 ≥0 with 𝑖 0 = 𝑗0 = 0 such that for every 𝑘 ≥ 0:
(1) (𝑒𝑘 )𝑝 = (𝑐𝑖𝑘 )𝑝 for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃,
(2) (𝑒𝑘 )𝑞 = (𝑑 𝑗𝑘 )𝑞 for every 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 ′ ,
(3) if 𝑒𝑘+1 exists, then exactly one of: (i) 𝑖𝑘+1 = 𝑖𝑘 + 1 and 𝑗𝑘+1 = 𝑗𝑘 , a 𝑃-step, or (ii) 𝑖𝑘+1 = 𝑖𝑘 and
    𝑗𝑘+1 = 𝑗𝑘 + 1, a 𝑃 ′ -step.
Moreover, if 𝑟 is finite of length 𝑛 then 𝑖𝑘 = 𝑛 for some 𝑘, and if 𝑟 is infinite then for every 𝑚 ≥ 0
there is a 𝑘 with 𝑖𝑘 = 𝑚; likewise for 𝑟 ′ and ( 𝑗𝑘 ).
  An interleaving is well defined: by Definition 65, A (𝑃) ⊆ A (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ) and A (𝑃 ′ ) ⊆ A (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ), so
each 𝑒𝑘 , with 𝑝-components in A (𝑃) and 𝑞-components in A (𝑃 ′ ), is a configuration in 𝐶 (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ).
Also 𝑒 0 = 𝑐 0 (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ), by the agreement of initial configurations across F (𝑃), F (𝑃 ′ ) and F (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ).
   Definition 67 (Interaction, Interactive Run, First Interaction [51]). Let 𝑄 ⊂ Π. For a configuration
𝑐 over 𝑄 and 𝑄 ′ ⊆ 𝑄, write 𝑐 |𝑄 ′ for the restriction of 𝑐 to 𝑄 ′ . A transition 𝑒 → 𝑒 ′ of F (𝑄) is an
interaction between 𝑥 ≠ 𝑦 ∈ 𝑄 if 𝑒 | {𝑥 } ≠ 𝑒 ′ | {𝑥 } and 𝑒 | {𝑦 } ≠ 𝑒 ′ | {𝑦 } , and 𝑒 | {𝑥 } → 𝑒 ′ | {𝑥 } is not a
transition of F ({𝑥 }) or 𝑒 | {𝑦 } → 𝑒 ′ | {𝑦 } is not a transition of F ({𝑦}). It is an interaction between
disjoint nonempty 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 if it is an interaction between some 𝑥 ∈ 𝑃 and some 𝑦 ∈ 𝑃 ′ . A run 𝑟ˆ of
F (𝑄) is interactive between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ if some transition of 𝑟ˆ is an interaction between them; the
first interaction of 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ in 𝑟ˆ is the first such transition.
   Definition 68 (Oblivious, Interactive, Grassroots [51]). A protocol F is:
(1) oblivious if for every disjoint nonempty 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π, every interleaving of a correct run of F (𝑃)
    and a correct run of F (𝑃 ′ ) is a correct run of F (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ).
(2) interactive if for every 𝑄 ⊂ Π and disjoint nonempty 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑄, every prefix of a correct run
    of F (𝑄) containing no interaction between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ is a prefix of a correct run of F (𝑄) that
    contains one.
(3) grassroots if it is oblivious and interactive.
   Being oblivious means two disjoint sets of agents coexist without interference. Being interactive
means two sets that have not yet interacted can still do so, and the condition is required at every
such point and not at the initial configuration only, so a protocol whose sets of agents can reach a
state from which they can never couple is not interactive.
   Definition 69 (Interaction Graph, Instance, Coalescence [51]). Let F be a protocol, 𝑄 ⊂ Π, and 𝑟 a
prefix of a run of F (𝑄). The interaction graph of 𝑟 has vertices 𝑄 and an edge between 𝑎 ≠ 𝑏 ∈ 𝑄
whenever some transition of 𝑟 is an interaction between 𝑎 and 𝑏. A nonempty 𝑃 ⊆ 𝑄 is an instance
in 𝑟 if it is a connected component of the interaction graph of 𝑟 . Disjoint nonempty 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑄
coalesce in a run 𝑟ˆ of F (𝑄) if they are instances in some prefix of 𝑟ˆ and lie in one component of the
interaction graph of a longer prefix of 𝑟ˆ.
   Proposition 70 (Coalescence [51]). Let F be a grassroots protocol, 𝑄 ⊂ Π, and 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 disjoint
instances in a prefix 𝑟 of a correct run of F (𝑄). Then 𝑟 is a prefix of a correct run of F (𝑄) in which 𝑃
and 𝑃 ′ coalesce.

A.4    Volitional Transactions-Based Protocols
A volitional transactions-based protocol assigns to each set of agents the volitional multiagent
transition system induced by its volition-guarded transactions.
58                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


   Definition 71 (Transactions Over a Local-States Function [51]). Let 𝑆 be a local-states function. A
set of transactions 𝑅 is over 𝑆 if every transaction 𝑡 ∈ 𝑅 is a multiagent transition over 𝑄 and 𝑆 (𝑃 ′ )
for some 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π. Given such a set 𝑅 and 𝑃 ⊂ Π, 𝑅(𝑃) := {𝑡 ∈ 𝑅 : 𝑡 is over 𝑄 and 𝑆 (𝑃 ′ ), 𝑄 ⊆
𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑃 }.
  Definition 72 (Volitional Transactions-Based Protocol [51]). Let 𝑆 be a local-states function and
𝑅 a set of volition-guarded transactions over 𝑆 with equivalence ∼. The protocol F over 𝑅, 𝑆, and
∼ assigns to each set of agents 𝑃 ⊂ Π the volitional multiagent transition system F (𝑃) induced
by (𝑆 (𝑃), 𝑅(𝑃), ∼) over 𝑃 (Definition 63). In particular, 𝐶 (𝑃) = A (𝑃) 𝑃 with agent state space
A (𝑃) := 2𝑇𝑅 (𝑃 ) /∼ × 𝑆 (𝑃), monotone in 𝑃 as Definition 65 requires, and 𝑐 0 (𝑃) has 𝑝-component
(∅, 𝑠 0 ) for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃.
   Throughout, ∼ is an equivalence on 𝑇𝑅 , the machine transactions underlying the whole set 𝑅; for
𝑇 ′ ⊆ 𝑇𝑅 we write 𝑇 ′ /∼ for the set of ∼-classes with a representative in 𝑇 ′ , and [𝑡] for the class of 𝑡
in 𝑇𝑅 /∼. Hence 𝑃 ⊆ 𝑃 ′ implies 𝑇𝑅 (𝑃 ) /∼ ⊆ 𝑇𝑅 (𝑃 ′ ) /∼.
   Definition 73 (Contained [51]). A set 𝑅 of volition-guarded transactions over a local-states function
𝑆 is contained if for every 𝑃 ⊂ Π, every (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅 with 𝑡 = 𝑐 → 𝑐 ′ over participants 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 and
𝑐 ∈ 𝑆 (𝑃)𝑄 is in 𝑅(𝑃).
  The three results below are used in the proofs of Section 6. The first is the invariant that in F (𝑃)
an agent wills only transactions over agents of 𝑃, so the guard of a transaction that reaches outside
𝑃 cannot will it while 𝑃 runs by itself.
   Lemma 74 (Volitional Containment [51]). Let F be a volitional transactions-based protocol
over a set of volition-guarded transactions 𝑅 with equivalence ∼. For every 𝑃 ⊂ Π, every configuration
𝑐 of F (𝑃), and every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃: 𝑐 𝑝𝑣 ⊆ 𝑇𝑅 (𝑃 ) /∼, where 𝑇𝑅 (𝑃 ) := {𝑡 : (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅(𝑃) for some 𝑄 ′ }.
   Lemma 75 (Interleaving Safety [51]). Let F be a volitional transactions-based protocol and
𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π disjoint and nonempty. Every finite prefix of an interleaving of a correct run of F (𝑃) and a
correct run of F (𝑃 ′ ) is a finite safe run of F (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ).
   Proposition 76 ([51]). A volitional transactions-based protocol over a contained set of volition-
guarded transactions is oblivious provided that for every disjoint nonempty 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π, no equivalence
class whose transactions have participants spanning both 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ is ever enabled in any interleaving
of correct runs of F (𝑃) and F (𝑃 ′ ).
  A person may die or become incapacitated, and an AI agent operating a machine may be retired;
their will then has no further effect. An agent has left when no act they guard can be enabled again.
  Definition 77 (Left [51]). Let F be a volitional transactions-based protocol over 𝑅, 𝑄 ⊂ Π, 𝑐
a configuration of F (𝑄), and 𝑥 ∈ 𝑄. Then 𝑥 has left in 𝑐 if no (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅(𝑄) with 𝑥 ∈ 𝑄 ′ is
                                                          ∗
machine-enabled in any configuration 𝑐 ′ of F (𝑄) with 𝑐 →
                                                         − 𝑐 ′ ⊆ 𝑇 (𝑄).
   Definition 78 (Interactive Among the Agents That Have Not Left [51]). A volitional transactions-
based protocol F is interactive among the agents that have not left if for every 𝑄 ⊂ Π and disjoint
nonempty 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑄, every prefix of a correct run of F (𝑄) containing no interaction between 𝑃
and 𝑃 ′ , in whose last configuration some agent of 𝑃 and some agent of 𝑃 ′ have not left, is a prefix
of a correct run of F (𝑄) that contains one.
   An interactive protocol is interactive among the agents that have not left, the latter requiring a
continuation of fewer prefixes.
   In a run of F (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ) an agent of 𝑃 may will a transaction reaching 𝑃 ′ , which a run of F (𝑃)
forbids; the volitional notion constrains the resulting coupling.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                         59


   Definition 79 (Volitionally Grassroots [51]). A volitional transactions-based protocol F is voli-
tionally grassroots if it is oblivious, interactive among the agents that have not left, and, for every
disjoint nonempty 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π and every safe run of F (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ) interactive between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , the
first interaction of 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ is induced by a volition-guarded transaction (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) with 𝑄 ′ ∩ 𝑃 ≠ ∅
and 𝑄 ′ ∩ 𝑃 ′ ≠ ∅.
    A grassroots protocol meeting the condition on first interactions is volitionally grassroots.

B     PROOFS
The statements of Sections 3, 5, 6, 7, 9, 10 and 11 are restated and proved here in order, with the
three lemmas and the one definition used only in these proofs. Throughout, 𝐶 is a syntactically
grassroots contract, F the protocol realising it and 𝑃 ⊂ Π. An atom a transition adds, deletes or
requires at an agent may come from a conditional literal or from a listed role’s literal at an instance
𝛼 of its conjunct (Definitions 3 and 6); every argument below about 𝛽 (𝜑) then applies to (𝛽 ∪ 𝛼) (𝜑),
with the anchor (𝛽 ∪ 𝛼) (𝐴), held by the agent of its holder, in the place of an atom required there;
the atoms of a condition of the body are held by the agents in roles.

B.1    Compiling Schemas into Transactions
    Lemma 17 (The compilation is well defined). 𝑅𝐶 with ∼ is well formed.
    Proof. Let 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ be determined by 𝜏 at 𝛽 over 𝑄, and let 𝑃 ′ be 𝑄 together with the agents 𝛽
assigns to the agent variables of 𝜏, the agents among the arguments of the atoms of 𝑑, the signers of
the speech acts among them and the agents of the community names among them, and the signers
of the speech acts 𝛽 assigns. Every atom of every 𝑑𝑝′ is an atom of 𝑑𝑝 or the image under 𝛽 of an
atom added at a role, and the arguments of either lie in 𝑃 ′ ∪ 𝐷 𝑃 ′ ∪ N𝑃 ′ , so 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ is a machine
transaction over 𝑄 and 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃 ′ ).
    Let machine transactions 𝑡 and 𝑡 ′ underlie unguarded transactions of 𝑅𝐶 , determined by schemas
𝜏 and 𝜏 ′ with no guarding role at admissible bindings 𝛽 and 𝛽 ′ , and let a transition lie in the
𝑃-closures of both. An agent whose state 𝑡 changes is not stationary in that transition, so it is a
participant of 𝑡 ′ , which changes its state in the same way, and conversely; by Definition 7 the atoms
a transaction adds at an agent are those of its state after less those before, and the atoms it deletes
those before less those after, so 𝑡 and 𝑡 ′ add and delete the same atoms at the same agents. If neither
𝜏 nor 𝜏 ′ has a reading role, every role of each adds or deletes an atom, so by Definitions 3 and 7 the
state of every participant of each changes, and 𝑡 and 𝑡 ′ have the same participants and the same
restriction to them, hence are equal. Otherwise let 𝜏 have one, and let 𝜑 1, . . . , 𝜑𝑚 , at the agent role
𝜋𝑖 , be signature atoms of 𝜏 (Definitions 12 and 13). Take 𝜑 𝑗 ∈ +(𝑖), the case 𝜑 𝑗 ∈ −(𝑖) being the
same with deleting or clearing in place of adding. Then 𝑡 adds 𝛽 (𝜑 𝑗 ) at 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ), so 𝑡 ′ does, and 𝜏 ′
adds an atom with the predicate of 𝜑 𝑗 at a role whose extent holds 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ); by Definition 12 𝜏 ′ = 𝜏
and that atom is 𝜑 𝑗 at 𝜋𝑖 , so 𝛽 ′ (𝜋𝑖 ) = 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) and 𝛽 ′ (𝜑 𝑗 ) = 𝛽 (𝜑 𝑗 ). This holds for every 𝑗, and every
agent role of 𝜏 but 𝜋𝑖 is the base of an argument of some 𝜑 𝑗 , so 𝛽 ′ and 𝛽 agree on every agent role.
The reading roles of 𝜏 are agent roles, and every other role of it adds or deletes an atom, so the
participants of each of 𝑡 and 𝑡 ′ are the agents whose state it changes and the agents 𝛽 sends the
reading roles to, the same for both; 𝑡 and 𝑡 ′ share a transition of their 𝑃-closures, so they have the
same restriction to each of these participants (Definition 62), and 𝑡 = 𝑡 ′ . Either way two of them of
different classes have disjoint closures, which is what Definition 63 requires of 𝑅𝐶 .
    Two machine transactions related by the relation generating ∼ have the same participants by
Definition 16, and so do two related by ∼. A class over 𝑃 is a union of sets of transactions each
determined by a schema at a binding sending its agent roles and agent variables into 𝑃 and its
speech-act variables into 𝐷; a contract has finitely many schemas, each has finitely many such
60                                             James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


bindings of its agent roles and agent variables, and 𝐷 is countable, Δ and Π being countable, so the
classes over 𝑃 are countably many, as Definition 56 requires.                                      □
     Lemma 18 (Containment). 𝑅𝐶 is contained.
    Proof. Let (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 , with 𝑡 = 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ determined by a schema 𝜏 at an admissible binding
𝛽 over participants 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑃 and 𝑑 ∈ 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃)𝑄 . An atom of 𝑑𝑝′ , 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄, is an atom of 𝑑𝑝 or 𝛽 (𝜑) for an
atom 𝜑 added at a role, and it suffices that every agent 𝛽 (𝜑) names is in 𝑃 (Definition 1). Such an
agent is 𝛽 (𝑢) for 𝑢 the base of an argument of 𝜑 that is an agent role, an agent variable or a name
term, or the signer of 𝛽 (𝑦) for a speech-act variable 𝑦 that is an argument of 𝜑 or occurs in a name
term among its arguments. By Definition 11, the set of predicates of 𝐶 having traceable provenance,
𝑢 is the base of a role of 𝜏, and then 𝛽 (𝑢) is the agent in an agent role of 𝜏, an agent of 𝑄; or 𝑢 is
the base of an argument of an atom 𝜓 required at an agent role 𝜋 𝑗 , and then 𝛽 (𝜓 ) is an atom of
𝑑 𝛽 (𝜋 𝑗 ) ∈ 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) naming 𝛽 (𝑢), so 𝛽 (𝑢) ∈ 𝑃; or a safe formula of 𝐵 names 𝑢 through 𝐸, and then, the
formula holding in 𝑑 at 𝛽 (Definition 7), by induction on it some atom 𝜓 of Φ𝐸 in it, held by an
agent role 𝜋 𝑗 , has 𝛽 (𝜓 ) ∈ 𝑑 𝛽 (𝜋 𝑗 ) naming 𝛽 (𝑢): for an atom, itself; for a conjunction, the conjunct
naming 𝑢, or, for 𝑢 = 𝑣, the conjunct naming 𝑣, as 𝛽 (𝑢) = 𝛽 (𝑣); for a disjunction, a disjunct that
holds; for ∃𝑥¯ (𝐴 ∧ 𝜑), an instance under which 𝐴 ∧ 𝜑 holds; so 𝛽 (𝑢) ∈ 𝑃. Likewise 𝑦 is an argument
of such a 𝜓 or occurs in a name term among its arguments, or a safe formula of 𝐵 names it, and
then the signer of 𝛽 (𝑦) is in 𝑃, or 𝑦 occurs in no atom required at a role, and then 𝛽 (𝑦) is signed
by the agent in a guarding role (Definition 5), an agent of 𝑄. So 𝑑𝑝′ ∈ 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) for every 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄, and
(𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 (𝑃).                                                                                      □
     The proof of Lemma 18 uses traceable provenance and no other condition of Definition 14.
   Lemma 80 (Every participant changes state). Every transition of F (𝑃) induced by a transaction
of 𝑅𝐶 changes the state of every participant of that transaction that is in no reading role.
  Proof. Let the transaction be (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ), with 𝑡 determined by a schema 𝜏 at a binding 𝛽, and let 𝑝
be a participant of 𝑡, so 𝑝 is in the extent of a role of 𝜏 (Definition 7). If one such role is neither
guarding nor reading, the machine state of 𝑝 changes by Definitions 3 and 7. Otherwise 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄 ′ ,
and the transition is taken where (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) is enabled, so [𝑡] is in the volitional state of 𝑝 there, and
the transition removes it (Definition 58).                                                              □
   Proposition 19 (Every participant takes a role). Every agent whose machine state a transition
of F (𝑃) changes is in the extent of a role of a schema of 𝐶 at some binding.
   Proof. A volition change leaves every machine state unchanged (Definition 58), so a transition
changing one is a volitional machine transaction induced by some (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 (𝑃), and by that
definition it leaves the machine state of every agent outside the participants of 𝑡 unchanged. By
Definition 16 𝑡 is determined by a schema 𝜏 of 𝐶 at a binding 𝛽, and by Definition 7 the participants
of 𝑡 are the agents in the extents of the roles of 𝜏.                                              □
  Proposition 20 (Anonymity). For every permutation 𝜎 of Π, 𝜎 (𝑅𝐶 ) = 𝑅𝐶 , 𝜎 (𝑐 0 (𝑃)) = 𝑐 0 (𝜎 (𝑃)),
and 𝜎 carries the runs of F (𝑃) to the runs of F (𝜎 (𝑃)).
   Proof. 𝜎 maps Σ[𝑃 ∪ 𝐷 𝑃 ∪ N𝑃 ] bijectively onto Σ[𝜎 (𝑃) ∪ 𝐷𝜎 (𝑃 ) ∪ N𝜎 (𝑃 ) ], carrying signers and
the agents of names with it, hence 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) onto 𝑆𝐶 (𝜎 (𝑃)), and fixes 𝑠 0 = ∅; so 𝜎 (𝑐 0 (𝑃)) = 𝑐 0 (𝜎 (𝑃)).
Let 𝑡 be determined by a schema 𝜏 at a binding 𝛽. Then 𝜎 ◦ 𝛽 is again a binding for 𝜏, sending
distinct agent roles to distinct agents, and two marked possibly one to one agent, as 𝛽 does and
leaving 𝑌 mapped into 𝐷, and it is admissible, 𝜎 carrying the signer of 𝛽 (𝑦) to the agent in the
same guarding role under 𝜎 ◦ 𝛽. Each clause of Definition 7 is an inclusion, a disjointness, a match
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                    61


or an identity between images under 𝛽 and the local states of 𝑑 and 𝑑 ′ , 𝜎 carries the extent of a role
at 𝛽 in 𝑑 to its extent at 𝜎 ◦ 𝛽 in 𝜎 (𝑑) and a name in no atom of 𝑑 to one in no atom of 𝜎 (𝑑), and 𝜎 is
a bijection on atoms, so applying it to both sides of each shows 𝜎 (𝑡) determined by 𝜏 at 𝜎 ◦ 𝛽. The
guarding roles of 𝜏 do not depend on the binding, so 𝜎 carries the volition-guarded transaction at 𝛽
to the one at 𝜎 ◦ 𝛽; by Definition 16 𝜎 (𝑅𝐶 ) ⊆ 𝑅𝐶 , and applying 𝜎 −1 proves the reverse inclusion.
Two members of 𝑅𝐶 are ∼-related exactly when one schema at one binding determines both, a
condition 𝜎 preserves, and 𝑅𝐶 (𝑃) is carried to 𝑅𝐶 (𝜎 (𝑃)) because 𝜎 carries 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃 ′ ) to 𝑆𝐶 (𝜎 (𝑃 ′ )) for
every 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑃. The volitional construction and the 𝑃-closure are defined from 𝑅𝐶 (𝑃), ∼ and 𝑐 0 (𝑃)
only, so 𝜎 carries F (𝑃) to F (𝜎 (𝑃)) and runs to runs.                                                  □

  Corollary 21 (No privileged agent). If a configuration is reachable in a run over 𝑃, so is its
image under every permutation of 𝑃.
  Proof. Extend the permutation of 𝑃 by the identity on Π \ 𝑃 and apply Proposition 20, which
carries the run to a run over 𝑃 ending in the image configuration.                          □

B.2   Turing Completeness
  Proposition 4 (Turing completeness). For every two-counter machine 𝑀 there is a syntactically
grassroots contract 𝐶𝑀 such that 𝑀 halts from empty counters if and only if some correct run of the
protocol realising 𝐶𝑀 reaches a configuration in which an agent holds halt.
    Proof. A two-counter machine [59] has states 𝑞 0, . . . , 𝑞ℎ , 𝑞 0 initial and 𝑞ℎ halting, two coun-
ters holding natural numbers, initially zero, and for each state 𝑞 ≠ 𝑞ℎ one instruction: inc(𝑖, 𝑞 ′ ),
add one to counter 𝑖 and go to 𝑞 ′ ; or dec(𝑖, 𝑞 ′, 𝑞 ′′ ), if counter 𝑖 is positive subtract one and
go to 𝑞 ′ , else go to 𝑞 ′′ . Let 𝐶𝑀 be GKK together with the following schemas over the predi-
cates at𝑞 , 𝑞 a state of 𝑀, ctr 1 , ctr 2 , started and halt, all of arity zero in a state, with the one
agent role Alice: the schema start, started (Alice), at𝑞0 (Alice) ← identity(Alice), ¬started (Alice)
guarded by Alice; for each inc(𝑖, 𝑞 ′ ) at 𝑞 the schema ¬at𝑞 (Alice), at𝑞 ′ (Alice), ctr 𝑖 (Alice) ←
at𝑞 (Alice); for each dec(𝑖, 𝑞 ′, 𝑞 ′′ ) at 𝑞 the two schemas ¬at𝑞 (Alice), at𝑞 ′ (Alice), ¬ctr 𝑖 (Alice) ←
at𝑞 (Alice), ctr 𝑖 (Alice) and ¬at𝑞 (Alice), at𝑞 ′′ (Alice) ← at𝑞 (Alice), ¬ctr 𝑖 (Alice); and the schema
halt (Alice) ← at𝑞ℎ (Alice), ¬halt (Alice); the schemas of 𝑀 have no guard. Deleting ctr 𝑖 removes
one copy, and the negated body atom ¬ctr 𝑖 (Alice) holds when the machine holds none, so the
multiplicity of ctr 𝑖 in the state of an agent is the value of counter 𝑖. 𝐶𝑀 is syntactically grassroots:
acquaint is its unobstructed introductory act as in GKK, the added schemas add atoms with no
arguments, so traceable provenance stands, and each has one role, so its role graph is connected and
it has no reading role. In a correct run, an agent 𝑝 that establishes an identity and wills start takes
it, by liveness, and thereafter holds at𝑞 for exactly one 𝑞; while 𝑞 ≠ 𝑞ℎ exactly one schema of 𝑀 is
enabled at 𝑝, since 𝑀 is deterministic and the two schemas of a dec have exclusive bodies, it has no
guard, so it stays enabled until taken and liveness takes it, and taking it carries the state and the
counters of 𝑝 as the instruction of 𝑀 does. So the states at𝑞 and the multiplicities of ctr 1 and ctr 2 at
𝑝 along the run are the computation of 𝑀 from empty counters, and 𝑝 comes to hold halt if and
only if that computation reaches 𝑞ℎ . Since two-counter machines compute every partial recursive
function [59], the halting problem for them is undecidable, and so is whether some correct run of
the protocol realising a contract reaches a configuration holding a given atom.                           □

B.3   Polite Sets of Volition-Guarded Transactions
Throughout this subsection, 𝑆 is a local-states function, 𝑅 a set of volition-guarded transactions
over 𝑆 with equivalence ∼, and F the protocol over 𝑅, 𝑆 and ∼, as in Section 6.
  Let 𝑄 ⊂ Π.
62                                              James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


 Lemma 81 (A Mutual Recording is an Interaction). Every transition of F (𝑄) induced by a
mutual recording of 𝑝 and 𝑞 in 𝑅(𝑄) is an interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞.

  By Definition 67 it is then an interaction between any two disjoint sets of agents one of which
holds 𝑝 and the other 𝑞.

  Proof. The machine states of 𝑝 and of 𝑞 both change, by Definition 24. Since 𝑑𝑝′ records 𝑞,
𝑑𝑝′∉ 𝑆 (𝑃) for every 𝑃 with 𝑞 ∉ 𝑃, and in particular 𝑑𝑝′ ∉ 𝑆 ({𝑝}); the machine states of every
configuration of F ({𝑝}) lie in 𝑆 ({𝑝}) by Definition 72, so the restriction of the transition to 𝑝 is
no transition of F ({𝑝}). By Definition 67 the transition is an interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞, hence
between any two disjoint sets of agents one of which holds 𝑝 and the other 𝑞.                       □

  Theorem 26 (Polite). The protocol over a contained, polite set of volition-guarded transactions is
grassroots and volitionally grassroots.

   Proof. Let 𝑅 be polite. One fact is used throughout: an agent whose state a volitional machine
transaction changes is a participant of the transaction inducing it, as only a participant changes
machine state and only a participant holds its class in their volitional state (Definitions 57 and 58).
Oblivious. Let 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π be disjoint and nonempty, 𝑟 a correct run of F (𝑃), 𝑟 ′ a correct run
of F (𝑃 ′ ), and 𝑒 = 𝑒 0, 𝑒 1, . . . an interleaving of 𝑟 and 𝑟 ′ , with index sequences (𝑖𝑘 ) and ( 𝑗𝑘 ). By
Lemma 75, every finite prefix of 𝑒 is a finite safe run of F (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ). Every transition of 𝑒 is a 𝑃-step
or a 𝑃 ′ -step and so alters the state of agents of one of the two sets only, so no transition of 𝑒 is an
interaction between an agent of 𝑃 and an agent of 𝑃 ′ , and no instance in a prefix of 𝑒 holds agents
of both.
    𝑅 is contained, and we verify the other hypothesis of Proposition 76. Let [𝑡] be a class of
𝑇𝑅 (𝑃 ∪𝑃 ′ ) /∼ whose transactions have participants 𝑄 meeting both 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ —the same 𝑄 for every
member of the class, by Definition 56—let (𝑡 ′′, 𝑄 ′′ ) ∈ 𝑅 with 𝑡 ′′ ∈ [𝑡], and take 𝑎 ∈ 𝑄 ∩ 𝑃. Suppose
(𝑡 ′′, 𝑄 ′′ ) is enabled at some 𝑒𝑘 . Its participants 𝑄 meet both sets and no instance in the prefix of 𝑒
ending at 𝑒𝑘 holds agents of both, so they do not all lie in one instance in that prefix; by closure the
instance holding 𝑎 there holds some 𝑏 ∈ 𝑄 ′′ , and 𝑏 ∈ 𝑃 as that instance holds no agent of 𝑃 ′ . Since
𝑄 meets 𝑃 ′ , 𝑡 ′′ ∉ 𝑅(𝑃), so [𝑡] ∉ 𝑇𝑅 (𝑃 ) /∼; and by Lemma 74 applied to 𝑟 , 𝑒𝑘 𝑏𝑣 = (𝑐𝑖𝑘 )𝑏𝑣 ⊆ 𝑇𝑅 (𝑃 ) /∼.
Hence [𝑡] ∉ 𝑒𝑘 𝑏𝑣 and the guard on 𝑏 fails, so (𝑡 ′′, 𝑄 ′′ ) is not enabled at 𝑒𝑘 —a contradiction.
    So no class whose transactions have participants in both sets is ever enabled in 𝑒, and by
Proposition 76, F is oblivious.
Interactive. Let 𝑄 ⊂ Π, let 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 be disjoint and nonempty, let 𝑟ˆ be a prefix of a correct run
of F (𝑄) containing no interaction between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , and take 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 and 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 ′ . An interaction
between 𝑝 and 𝑞 is one between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ by Definition 67, so 𝑟ˆ contains none, and by openness
some finite safe extension of 𝑟ˆ by volition changes and transitions that are not interactions between
𝑝 and 𝑞 ends where some (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅(𝑄) whose induced transitions are interactions between 𝑝
and 𝑞 is machine-enabled. If a transition of that extension is an interaction between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , the
extension is a finite safe run having 𝑟ˆ as a prefix and containing one, and by Lemma 60 a prefix of a
correct run of F (𝑄); so suppose none is.
   Extend further by a volition change of each agent of 𝑄 ′ , where needed, so that [𝑡] lies in the
volitional state of each; these leave every machine state unchanged, so (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) is now enabled.
Extend by the volitional machine transaction it induces, an interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞 and hence
between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ . The result is a finite safe run of F (𝑄) having 𝑟ˆ as a prefix and containing an
interaction between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , and by Lemma 60 it is a prefix of a correct run of F (𝑄). Hence F is
interactive, and with obliviousness, grassroots.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                    63


Volitionally grassroots. Let 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊂ Π be disjoint and nonempty, let 𝑟ˆ be a safe run of F (𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ )
interactive between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , and let 𝑒 → 𝑒 ′ be its first interaction. It changes the state of an
agent of 𝑃 and of an agent of 𝑃 ′ , and a volition change alters one agent only, so it is a volitional
machine transaction induced by some (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅(𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ ) whose participants 𝑄 meet both sets;
take 𝑎 ∈ 𝑄 ∩ 𝑃 and 𝑏 ∈ 𝑄 ∩ 𝑃 ′ .
  The participants of 𝑡 do not all lie in one instance in the prefix 𝜌 of 𝑟ˆ ending at 𝑒: were they, 𝑎
and 𝑏 would lie in one connected component of the interaction graph of 𝜌; every agent of 𝜌 is in
𝑃 ∪ 𝑃 ′ , so a path from 𝑎 to 𝑏 in that component has an edge between some 𝑥 ∈ 𝑃 and some 𝑦 ∈ 𝑃 ′ ,
and the transition of 𝜌 that puts that edge there is an interaction between 𝑥 and 𝑦, hence between
𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , and it precedes 𝑒 → 𝑒 ′ —contradicting that 𝑒 → 𝑒 ′ is the first. The same argument shows
that every instance in 𝜌 lies wholly in 𝑃 or wholly in 𝑃 ′ , so the instance of 𝑎 and the instance of 𝑏
are distinct. As (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) is enabled at 𝑒, by closure each of the two holds an agent of 𝑄 ′ , the first in
𝑃 and the second in 𝑃 ′ , so 𝑄 ′ meets both. F is oblivious and interactive, so it is grassroots, and
interactive among the agents that have not left, so it is volitionally grassroots (Definition 79). □

B.4   Conditions Sufficient for Openness and Closure
  Lemma 27 (Records). On local states, Definitions 22 and 8 agree.
   Proof. Suppose an atom of 𝑠 names 𝑞, as Definition 8 requires, and let 𝑃 ⊂ Π with 𝑞 ∉ 𝑃. That
atom has an argument in Π \ 𝑃, a speech act among its arguments whose signer is in Π \ 𝑃, or a
community name among its arguments outside N𝑃 , so 𝑠 ∉ 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) by Definition 1. As 𝑃 was arbitrary,
𝑠 records 𝑞. Suppose no atom of 𝑠 names 𝑞, and let 𝑃 be the agents among the arguments of the
atoms of 𝑠 together with the signers of the speech acts among them, the agents of the community
names among them and some agent other than 𝑞, which exists as Π has at least two agents. Then 𝑃
is nonempty, 𝑞 ∉ 𝑃 and 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃), so 𝑠 does not record 𝑞.                                      □
  The recording graph of a configuration of the protocol realising 𝐶 therefore has an edge 𝑝 → 𝑞
exactly when some atom of the state of 𝑝 names 𝑞. Its edges are classified by the predicate of the
atom.
  Definition 82 (𝐸-recording graph). Let 𝐸 be a set of predicates. The 𝐸-recording graph of a config-
uration is the spanning subgraph of its recording graph whose edges 𝑝 → 𝑞 are those for which
the state of 𝑝 contains an atom with predicate in 𝐸 that names 𝑞; the 𝐸-recording graph of a run is
the union of those of its configurations.
  Proposition 28 (Openness). 𝑅𝐶 is open.
   Proof. Let 𝜏 be an unobstructed introductory act of 𝐶, let 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞 in 𝑄, let 𝑟 be a finite safe run of
the protocol over 𝑄 containing no interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞, and let 𝛽 be an introduction of 𝑝
and 𝑞 meeting Definition 10. Extend 𝑟 , for each role 𝜋𝑖 of 𝜏 and each 𝜑 ∈ =(𝑖), by a volition change
of 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) and a unary transition adding 𝛽 (𝜑) there, induced by a schema of arity one of clause 1
at an admissible binding sending its role to 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) and its agent variables into 𝑄, which that agent
takes by itself; where that schema forbids 𝛽 (𝜑) and the state of 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) holds it already, 𝜏 requires it
once there, and the transition is skipped. Each transition taken is machine-enabled, the schema
requiring nothing, forbidding nothing or only 𝛽 (𝜑), which is then absent, and having no name
term, so no name need be fresh. Such a schema deletes nothing, so the extension is finite and safe
and every atom 𝜏 requires at 𝛽 is present at its last configuration. A schema of arity one has neither
of 𝑝 and 𝑞 in a role other than the one sent to 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ), so by clause 2 of Definition 10 no atom it adds
at 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) is one 𝜏 forbids at 𝜋𝑖 : the extension adds no forbidden atom, and no atom 𝜏 requires at 𝛽 is
one it forbids.
64                                             James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


   Let 𝜑 ∈ ¬(𝑖) and suppose the state of 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) there contains a copy of 𝛽 (𝜑). The initial state is
empty and the extension added no forbidden atom, so some transition of 𝑟 added it; that transition
is induced by a transaction of 𝑅𝐶 determined by a schema 𝜏 ′ at a binding 𝛽 ′ with 𝛽 (𝜑) ∈ 𝛽 ′ (+( 𝑗))
for a role whose extent holds 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ); by clause 2 of Definition 10 it is no listed role, so it is an agent
role sent to 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ). By that clause 𝛽 ′ sends a role of 𝜏 ′ to the other of 𝑝 and 𝑞, and 𝛽 ′ (+( 𝑗)) names
that agent. Both are then participants in no reading role, so by Lemma 80 the state of each changes,
and the state of 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) afterwards records the other, so it lies in no 𝑆 (𝑃) omitting them and the
transition restricted to 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) is no transition of that agent’s own transition system: the transition is
an interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞, which 𝑟 does not contain. So no forbidden atom is present, and 𝜏,
having no name term, is machine-enabled at 𝛽 at the last configuration of the extension, and every
transaction it determines there leaves 𝑝 holding an atom naming 𝑞 and 𝑞 one naming 𝑝, changing
both states, and is guarded by both; by Lemma 27 each records the other, so it is a mutual recording
of 𝑝 and 𝑞 in 𝑅𝐶 (𝑄), 𝛽 mapping into {𝑝, 𝑞} ⊆ 𝑄, and by Lemma 81 the transitions it induces are
interactions between 𝑝 and 𝑞.                                                                            □

   Closure requires that the participants of an unguarded act already lie in one instance. Where an
atom can be added only by an act of the two agents it relates, that follows directly. An edge may
also come from a transfer: a coin passes from hand to hand, and the agent it points at is reached
through the agents that carried it rather than by a single act. The lemma below covers both: no act
ever leaves an edge whose ends lie in different instances.
   Let 𝐸 be the set of predicates occurring in 𝐶 other than its environment predicates, which has
traceable provenance, 𝑄 ⊂ Π, and 𝑟 a finite safe run of F (𝑄).
  Lemma 83 (No edge crosses instances). Every edge of the 𝐸-recording graph of 𝑟 joins two agents
that lie in one instance in 𝑟 .
   Proof. The 𝐸-recording graph of 𝑟 is the union of those of its configurations, and every config-
uration of 𝑟 ends a prefix of 𝑟 ; the interaction graph of a prefix of 𝑟 is a subgraph of that of 𝑟 , so
two agents in one instance in a prefix are in one instance in 𝑟 . It suffices to treat an edge 𝑝 → 𝑞
of the 𝐸-recording graph of the last configuration. The claim is trivial for 𝑞 = 𝑝, and we argue
by induction on the length of 𝑟 . The initial state is empty, so a run of one configuration holds it
vacuously. Let 𝑟 end with the transition 𝑒 → 𝑐, and let the state of 𝑝 at 𝑐 contain an atom with
predicate in 𝐸 naming 𝑞 ≠ 𝑝. If the state of 𝑝 at 𝑒 contains such an atom, the induction hypothesis
applied to the prefix of 𝑟 ending at 𝑒 gives the claim. Otherwise 𝑒 → 𝑐 added it, so it is a volitional
machine transaction induced by a transaction determined by a schema 𝜏 at a binding 𝛽, and the
atom is 𝛽 (𝜑) for an atom 𝜑 added at a role 𝜋𝑖 , with predicate in 𝐸, where 𝑝 is in the extent of 𝜋𝑖 ,
and 𝑞 = 𝛽 (𝑠) for 𝑠 the base of an argument of 𝜑 that is an agent role, an agent variable or a name
term, or 𝑞 is the signer of 𝛽 (𝑦) for a speech-act variable 𝑦 that is an argument of 𝜑 or occurs in a
name term among its arguments.
   By Lemma 80 the state of every participant in no reading role changes, and by Lemma 27 the
state of 𝑝 at 𝑐 records 𝑞, so it lies in no 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) with 𝑞 ∉ 𝑃 and the restriction of the transition to
𝑝 is no transition of F ({𝑝}). By Definition 67 the transition is therefore an interaction between
𝑝 and every other participant in no reading role. A participant 𝑝 ′′ in a reading role 𝜋 𝑗 lies in one
instance with 𝑝 in 𝑟 too. If 𝜏 has a guarding role, by Definition 13 a guarding role 𝜋𝑔 requires an
atom naming the base of 𝜋 𝑗 , or 𝜋 𝑗 is a listed role whose base 𝜋𝑔 is a guarding role, which holds
its anchor at the instance sending 𝜋 𝑗 to 𝑝 ′′ ; either way 𝛽 (𝜋𝑔 ) holds at 𝑒 an atom of 𝐸 naming 𝑝 ′′ ,
and 𝛽 (𝜋𝑔 ) and 𝑝 ′′ lie in one instance in the prefix ending at 𝑒 by the induction hypothesis, while
𝛽 (𝜋𝑔 ), in no reading role, is joined to 𝑝 by an edge of the interaction graph. If 𝜏 has none, its role
graph is connected (Definition 13), and the proof of Proposition 29, which uses this lemma only
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                        65


for the prefix ending at 𝑒, where the induction hypothesis gives it, puts every participant of the
transaction, 𝑝 ′′ and 𝑝 among them, in one instance in that prefix, hence in 𝑟 .
    By Definition 11 one of two cases holds, in the first case of 𝑠 and in the second of 𝑦. If 𝑠 is the base
of a role of 𝜏, an agent role whose agent is in its own extent, or 𝑦 occurs in no atom required at a
role and so by Definition 5 has 𝛽 (𝑦) signed by the agent in a guarding role, then 𝑞 is a participant, so
𝑝 and 𝑞 are joined by an edge of the interaction graph of 𝑟 . Otherwise 𝑠 is the base of an argument
of an atom 𝜓 required at an agent role 𝜋 𝑗 , with predicate in 𝐸, or 𝑦 is an argument of such an atom
or occurs in a name term among its arguments, or a safe formula of 𝐵 names 𝑠 or 𝑦 through 𝐸, and
then, the formula holding at 𝑒 under 𝛽 (Definition 7), by induction on it as in the proof of Lemma 18
some atom 𝜓 of Φ𝐸 in it, held by an agent role 𝜋 𝑗 , has 𝛽 (𝜓 ) in the state of 𝛽 (𝜋 𝑗 ) at 𝑒 naming 𝑞; put
𝑝 ′ := 𝛽 (𝜋 𝑗 ). By Definition 7 𝛽 (𝜓 ) is an atom of the state of 𝑝 ′ at 𝑒, its predicate is in 𝐸 and it names
𝑞, so by the induction hypothesis 𝑝 ′ and 𝑞 lie in one instance in the prefix ending at 𝑒, hence in 𝑟 . If
𝑝 ′ ≠ 𝑝 then 𝑝 ′ is a participant, so 𝑝 and 𝑝 ′ lie in one instance in 𝑟 , by the edge of the interaction
graph or, 𝑝 ′ in a reading role, as shown above. Either way 𝑝 and 𝑞 lie in one instance in 𝑟 .               □

  Proposition 29 (Closure). 𝑅𝐶 is closed.
    Proof. Let (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) of 𝑅𝐶 be enabled at the last configuration 𝑐 of a finite safe run 𝑟 of the protocol,
determined
Ð               by a schema 𝜏 at a binding 𝛽, and let 𝑄𝑖 := ext𝑐 (𝜋𝑖 ), so that the participants of 𝑡 are
   𝑖 𝑄 𝑖 (Definition 7). Let 𝐸 be the set of predicates occurring in 𝐶 other than its environment
predicates, which has traceable provenance.
    First, let 𝜋𝑖 be a listed role with base 𝜋𝑘 and 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄𝑖 (Definition 6). 𝛽 (𝜋𝑘 ) holds (𝛽 ∪ 𝛼) (𝐴) at
𝑐, 𝐴 the anchor of a quantifier binding 𝜋𝑖 and 𝛼 an instance with 𝛼 (𝜋𝑖 ) = 𝑝, an atom naming 𝑝,
so 𝑝 = 𝛽 (𝜋𝑘 ) or the 𝐸-recording graph of 𝑟 has the edge 𝛽 (𝜋𝑘 ) → 𝑝, and 𝑝 and 𝛽 (𝜋𝑘 ) lie in one
instance in 𝑟 by Lemma 83. So the participants in the roles of one base lie in one instance in 𝑟 .
    Second, let 𝜋𝑖 require an atom 𝜓 of traceable provenance an argument of which has the base
𝜋𝑙 of a role 𝜋 𝑗 . That argument is 𝜋𝑙 or a name term with base 𝜋𝑙 , so 𝛽 (𝜓 ) names 𝛽 (𝜋𝑙 ), and every

the participants in 𝜋𝑖 and in 𝜋 𝑗 lie in one instance in 𝑟 .
    Instances are the connected components of the interaction graph of 𝑟 , so lying in one instance
is an equivalence, and by Definition 13 the participants in the roles of one connected component
of the role graph of 𝜏 lie in one instance in 𝑟 . If the role graph is connected, all participants of
𝑡 lie in one instance. Otherwise each connected component of it has a guarding agent role, by
Definition 13, whose agent is a guard of (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) and lies in the instance of the participants of that
component; every instance holding a participant holds the participants of a component, and so
holds a guard.                                                                                            □

B.5   Contracts with Wards
  Lemma 30 (Reflexive predicates). Lemmas 18 and 83 and Propositions 28 and 29 hold for a
contract declaring reflexive predicates, with Definition 10 read as above.
  Proof. Each proof goes through with one case added, where an atom a role requires is, under
the binding, the atom of a reflexive predicate naming the agent in that role, which 𝑑ˆ holds at every
agent. Containment: an argument of an atom added, by traceable provenance an argument of an
atom 𝜓 required at a role 𝜋 𝑗 , is 𝛽 (𝜋 𝑗 ) itself when 𝛽 (𝜓 ) is that atom, an agent of 𝑄. No edge crosses
instances: for an argument 𝑠, or a speech-act variable 𝑦, of the added atom that is an argument of
an atom 𝜓 required at a role 𝑗, with 𝑝 ′ := 𝛽 ( 𝑗), 𝛽 (𝜓 ) is the atom of a reflexive predicate naming 𝑝 ′ ,
and then 𝑞 = 𝑝 ′ , or an atom of the state of 𝑝 ′ , as there. Closure: the image of the atom of traceable
provenance that 𝜋𝑖 requires naming 𝜋 𝑗 lies in the state of 𝑝 := 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ) or is the atom of a reflexive
66                                             James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


predicate naming 𝑝, and then 𝑞 = 𝑝 and the two lie in one instance. Openness: the extension of
the run adds, at each role, every atom required there other than an atom of a reflexive predicate
naming its holder, which 𝑑ˆ holds; no such atom is forbidden; and no schema adds or deletes at a
role the atom of a reflexive predicate naming it, so the rest of the proof is unchanged.       □

  Lemma 84 (Wards and guardians). Let 𝐶 have kinds (𝜄, 𝑓 , 𝑀) and guardianships, and let (𝑚, 𝑣, 𝑢)
be the guardianship of 𝑚 ∈ 𝑀. In every configuration of a run of the protocol realising 𝐶:
(1) an agent holds 𝜄 if and only if they hold 𝑓 or a mark in 𝑀, and then they hold one of these, once;
(2) an agent holding an atom of 𝑣 holds 𝑓 ;
(3) an agent holding an atom of 𝑢 holds 𝜄; and an agent 𝑥 holds 𝑢 (𝑏, 𝑧)
                                                                      ¯ if and only if 𝑏 holds 𝑣 (𝑥, 𝑧),
                                                                                                     ¯
    each at most once;
(4) where every schema of 𝐶 adds the atoms of 𝑢 and of 𝑣 at agent roles, no agent holds an atom of 𝑢
    or of 𝑣 naming themselves.

   Proof. By induction on the run. The initial state is empty. A transition is induced by a transaction
determined by a schema at a binding, and changes a state only by the atoms the schema adds,
deletes and clears there. No schema deletes 𝜄 or 𝑓 (Definition 31, clause 1), so an agent holding
either holds it from then on.
   (1) A schema adds 𝜄 at a role only where it forbids it, with 𝑓 or one mark in 𝑀 and no other of
them, and adds a mark in 𝑀 only so (clause 2); so an agent gains 𝜄 once, and with it one of them,
once. A schema adds 𝑓 otherwise only at a role at which it deletes a mark in 𝑀, and deletes a mark
in 𝑀 only where it adds 𝑓 (clauses 2 and 3); the agent there held that mark and no other of them,
and holds 𝑓 and no other of them after it.
   (2) An atom of 𝑣 is added at a role that requires 𝑓 (Definition 32, clause 3), which is never deleted.
   (3) An atom of 𝑢 is added at a role that gains 𝑚, and so 𝜄, or that requires 𝑚 (clause 3), whose
holder holds 𝜄 by (1). By clause 3 a transition adds 𝑢 (𝑏, 𝑧) ¯ at 𝑥 exactly when it adds 𝑣 (𝑥, 𝑧) ¯ at 𝑏.
Before it 𝑥 does not hold 𝑢 (𝑏, 𝑧):
                                 ¯ either the schema forbids it at 𝑥, or it adds 𝑚 at 𝑥, where it adds 𝜄
and so forbids it, and an agent holding an atom of 𝑢 holds 𝜄; so by induction 𝑏 does not hold 𝑣 (𝑥, 𝑧), ¯
and after it each holds its atom once. By clause 4 a transition deletes 𝑢 (𝑏, 𝑧) ¯ at 𝑥 exactly when it
deletes 𝑣 (𝑥, 𝑧)
              ¯ at 𝑏, each held once; and it clears 𝑢 at 𝑥 exactly when it deletes 𝑣 (𝑥, 𝑧)
                                                                                         ¯ at every agent
in the extent of the listed role, which is every 𝑏 with 𝑢 (𝑏, 𝑧)
                                                              ¯ in the state of 𝑥, the conjuncts having
no condition (Definition 6): after it 𝑥 holds no atom of 𝑢 and no agent holds an atom of 𝑣 naming 𝑥.
No schema clears an atom of 𝑣 (clause 1).
   (4) A transition adds 𝑢 (𝑏, 𝑧)
                               ¯ at 𝑥 only where it adds 𝑣 (𝑥, 𝑧)¯ at 𝑏 (Definition 32, clause 3), at two
agent roles each adding an atom naming the other; Definition 5 sends two such roles to distinct
agents, so 𝑥 and 𝑏 are distinct.                                                                         □

  Lemma 85 (Leaving). Let 𝐶 have kinds (𝜄, 𝑓 , 𝑀) and guardianships. In every configuration of a
run of the protocol realising 𝐶:
(1) a ward of a passive kind has left;
(2) a ward of a kind 𝑚 that is not passive, holding no atom of the 𝑢 of its guardianship, has left.

   Proof. Let 𝑥 hold 𝑚 ∈ 𝑀. By Lemma 84, 𝑥 holds 𝜄 and neither 𝑓 nor another mark in 𝑀. A
guarding role forbids 𝜄, requires 𝑓 , requires a mark in 𝑀 and an atom of the 𝑢 of its guardianship,
or has in the body of its schema that it can act (Definition 32); so a guarding role sent to 𝑥 in a
transaction that is machine-enabled requires 𝑚 and an atom of 𝑢, the 𝑢 of the guardianship of 𝑚, or
can act by the disjunct of 𝑚, which 𝑚 has only if it is not passive and which holds only if 𝑥 holds
an atom of 𝑢.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                    67


   (1) If 𝑚 is passive, no guarding role requires it, no formula that a role can act has a disjunct of it
and no schema deletes it, so in every later configuration 𝑥 holds 𝑚 and no transaction 𝑥 guards is
machine-enabled.
   (2) If 𝑥 holds no atom of 𝑢, no transaction 𝑥 guards is machine-enabled. A transition deletes 𝑚
at 𝑥 only by a schema requiring an atom of 𝑢 there (Definition 32, clause 2), and adds an atom of
𝑢 at 𝑥 only by a schema that adds 𝑚 there, and so forbids 𝜄 there, or that requires an atom of 𝑢
there, 𝑚 not being passive (clause 3); so no transition does either, and the same holds in every later
configuration.                                                                                         □

  Throughout the rest of this subsection 𝐶 is a contract with an introduction with wards, traceable
provenance and volition, 𝑅𝐶 its realisation and F the protocol realising it.
   Definition 86 (Open among the agents that have not left). A set 𝑅 of volition-guarded transactions
is open among the agents that have not left if for any two agents 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞 in any 𝑄 ⊂ Π, every finite
safe run of F (𝑄) with no interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞, in whose last configuration neither 𝑝 nor 𝑞
has left, has a finite safe extension, by volition changes and transitions that are not interactions
between 𝑝 and 𝑞, at whose end some transaction of 𝑅(𝑄) whose induced transitions are interactions
between 𝑝 and 𝑞 is machine-enabled.
  An open set is open among the agents that have not left.
  Proposition 87 (Openness with wards). 𝑅𝐶 is open among the agents that have not left.
    Proof. Let 𝑝 ≠ 𝑞 in 𝑄, let 𝑟 be a finite safe run of F (𝑄) with no interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞,

state holds a mark in 𝑀, and free otherwise; a free agent holds 𝑓 and 𝜄, or neither (Lemma 84). By
Lemma 85, each of 𝑝 and 𝑞 that is a ward is of a kind 𝑚 that is not passive and holds an atom 𝑢 (𝑏, 𝑧)   ¯
of the guardianship of 𝑚; we take one such 𝑏 as its guardian, who by Lemma 84 holds 𝑣 (𝑥, 𝑧),   ¯ and so
𝑓 , and is free. Two facts. First, the transition that added 𝑢 (𝑏, 𝑧)
                                                                   ¯ at a ward 𝑥 added 𝑣 (𝑥, 𝑧)
                                                                                             ¯ at 𝑏, a role
of its schema (Definition 32, clause 3), so it changed both states (Lemma 80, neither role reading),
left 𝑥 recording 𝑏, and was an interaction between 𝑥 and 𝑏; so the guardian of 𝑝, if 𝑝 is a ward, is
not 𝑞, and that of 𝑞 is not 𝑝. Second, for free 𝑥 ≠ 𝑦 in 𝑄, the introductory act 𝜏 at the introduction
of 𝑥 and 𝑦 can be made machine-enabled by unary transitions at 𝑥 and at 𝑦: where one of them
holds no 𝜄, the establishing act, which forbids only 𝜄 and requires nothing, is machine-enabled there,
and leaves them holding 𝜄 and 𝑓 (Definition 31, clause 4); an atom 𝜏 requires that is then absent is
the one atom of a schema of arity one requiring nothing but 𝜄 and 𝑓 and forbidding nothing but
that atom, which is machine-enabled; and 𝜏 forbids nothing (Definition 33). Taking 𝜏 there, after
volition changes of 𝑥 and 𝑦, leaves 𝑥 holding 𝑒 (𝑦) and 𝑦 holding 𝑒 (𝑥), and is an interaction between
𝑥 and 𝑦 and no others.
    If 𝑝 and 𝑞 are free, the second fact at 𝑥 := 𝑝 and 𝑦 := 𝑞 gives an extension by unary transitions
after which 𝜏 at their introduction is machine-enabled, and every transaction it determines is a
mutual recording of 𝑝 and 𝑞, so its transitions are interactions between 𝑝 and 𝑞 by Lemma 81.
    Otherwise let 𝜒 be the relay act for the statuses of 𝑝 and 𝑞, which 𝐶 has, their marks not being
passive, bound with its ward roles to those of 𝑝 and 𝑞 that are wards, its free role to the free one,
and the guardian role of each ward role, where it has one, to that ward’s guardian 𝑏, an agent
variable of the ward role’s atom of 𝑢, where it has none, to 𝑏 too, and the value variables of that
atom to 𝑧; ¯ and the speech-act variables of each atom of a predicate ℎ that 𝜒 requires at a ward
role, as clause 2 of Definition 34 admits, to the arguments of an atom of ℎ held by the ward bound
there, which holds one: the transition that added its mark added one, and no transition deletes
one; every transition that added an atom of 𝑣 naming the ward at an agent added its record there,

with the arguments of the ward’s ℎ-atom, and no transition deletes one, so the guardian holds it
under the binding. The related roles are bound to distinct agents, and two guardian roles are bound
to one person only where the guardians taken for 𝑝 and 𝑞 are one, which the schema marks as
possibly one, as GnW:4 does Alice and Bob, and Definition 5 permits as the guardian roles add

with 𝜅 the other ward role, bound to the ward 𝑦, whose guardian is 𝑏, where 𝑥 does not hold 𝑒 (𝑦):
first 𝑒 (𝑏) at 𝑥, as above, and then the relay act with ward role 𝑦, free role 𝑥 and guardian role 𝑏,

holding 𝑒 (𝑦) and the record of 𝑦, and is no interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞, 𝑥 being neither; where

a reflexive atom naming its holder is in 𝑑;ˆ the unary atoms, 𝜄 and 𝑓 are present at the free agents; a
ward role bound to 𝑝 has 𝜄, 𝑚, its atom of 𝑢 naming 𝑏 and the atom of ℎ its variables are bound to at
