ward’s ℎ-atom at 𝑏; 𝑒 (𝜅) at a role bound to 𝑥 with 𝜅 bound to 𝑦 holds by the extension where 𝑦 ≠ 𝑥
and by reflexivity where 𝑦 = 𝑥, and the record of the other ward at its guardian role likewise; and
𝜒 forbids nothing. Every transaction 𝜒 determines there leaves 𝑝 holding an atom naming 𝑞 and 𝑞
one naming 𝑝, changing both states, so each records the other after it, the transition restricted to 𝑝
is no transition of F ({𝑝}), and it is an interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞.                                □

   Theorem 88 (Polite among the agents that have not left). The protocol over a contained
set of volition-guarded transactions that is closed and open among the agents that have not left is
volitionally grassroots.

   Proof. Let 𝑅 be such a set and F the protocol over it. By the parts of the proof of Theorem 26
headed Oblivious and Volitionally grassroots, which use containment and closure and not openness,
F is oblivious, and the first interaction of two disjoint sets of agents in a safe run is induced by a
transaction guarded by a member of each. Let 𝑄 ⊂ Π, let 𝑃, 𝑃 ′ ⊆ 𝑄 be disjoint and nonempty, and
let 𝑟ˆ be a prefix of a correct run of F (𝑄) containing no interaction between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , in whose
last configuration 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 and 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 ′ have not left. By Definition 86 some finite safe extension of 𝑟ˆ,
by volition changes and transitions that are not interactions between 𝑝 and 𝑞, ends where some
(𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅(𝑄) whose induced transitions are interactions between 𝑝 and 𝑞 is machine-enabled.
If a transition of the extension is an interaction between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , the extension contains one.
Otherwise extend it by a volition change of each agent of 𝑄 ′ , where needed, so that [𝑡] lies in the
volitional state of each, and by the volitional machine transaction that (𝑡, 𝑄 ′ ) then induces, an
interaction between 𝑝 and 𝑞 and hence between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ . Either way the result is a finite safe run
of F (𝑄) having 𝑟ˆ as a prefix and containing an interaction between 𝑃 and 𝑃 ′ , and by Lemma 60
it is a prefix of a correct run of F (𝑄). So F is interactive among the agents that have not left
(Definition 78), and volitionally grassroots (Definition 79).                                       □

   Theorem 36 (Grassroots realisation with wards). The protocol realising a contract with an
introduction with wards, traceable provenance and volition is volitionally grassroots.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                     69


   Proof. By Proposition 87 𝑅𝐶 is open among the agents that have not left. The proof of Proposi-
tion 29 uses traceable provenance and volition and nothing of the introductory act, so 𝑅𝐶 is closed,
and by Lemma 18, whose proof uses traceable provenance only, it is contained; both hold with
reflexive predicates by Lemma 30. By Theorem 88 the protocol over 𝑅𝐶 is volitionally grassroots. □
  Proposition 37 (GnW is grassroots). The protocol realising GnW is volitionally grassroots.
  Proof. By Section 8.1 GnW has an introduction with wards, traceable provenance and volition,
and by Theorem 36 the protocol realising it is volitionally grassroots.                     □

B.6   Relations among Contracts
   Proposition 42 (Restriction is undecidable). Whether one syntactically grassroots contract
restricts another is undecidable.
   Proof. Let 𝑀 be a two-counter machine, 𝐶𝑀 the contract of the proof of Proposition 4, and 𝐶𝑀       ′

that contract without its schema halt (Alice) ← at𝑞ℎ (Alice), ¬halt (Alice), which is syntactically
grassroots by the argument given there. We show that 𝐶𝑀        ′ restricts 𝐶 if and only if 𝑀 does not
                                                                            𝑀
halt from empty counters, which is undecidable [59]. As in that proof, in every safe run of either
protocol the states at𝑞 and the multiplicities of ctr 1 and ctr 2 an agent holds follow the computation
of 𝑀 from empty counters, so an agent holds at𝑞ℎ only if 𝑀 halts.
   Suppose 𝑀 does not halt. Then no transaction of halt is machine-enabled at a configuration of a
safe run. Only halt adds the atom halt, so no other schema determines a transaction it determines,
and the classes of 𝐶𝑀 are those of 𝐶𝑀     ′ and those of halt. So the agent configurations and the
                                        ′
transitions of the protocol realising 𝐶𝑀 over 𝑃 are among those of the protocol realising 𝐶𝑀 over 𝑃,
and a class of the latter enabled at every configuration of a suffix of a correct run 𝑟 ′ of the former
is a class of the former, a member of which 𝑟 ′ takes there. So 𝑟 ′ is a correct run of the protocol
realising 𝐶𝑀 over 𝑃, and, the predicates of 𝐶𝑀 ′ being those of 𝐶 but halt, 𝐶 ′ restricts 𝐶 .
                                                                      𝑀            𝑀           𝑀
                                             ′
   Suppose 𝑀 halts, and let 𝑃 = {𝑝}. Let 𝑟 be the run of the protocol realising 𝐶𝑀    ′ over 𝑃 in which

𝑝 wills establishing an identity and takes it, wills start and takes it, and then takes the schemas of
𝑀, one at each step, until 𝑝 holds at𝑞ℎ . At its last configuration 𝑝 wills nothing, establishing and
start forbid atoms 𝑝 holds, no schema of 𝑀 is machine-enabled, and every other schema of GKK
has two agent roles and so no binding into 𝑃; so no class is enabled there, and 𝑟 ′ is correct. A run
of the protocol realising 𝐶𝑀 over 𝑃 whose machine configurations, repetitions removed, include
those of 𝑟 ′ in order and end with its last one stays at that machine configuration from some point
on. There halt, which has no guard, is machine-enabled at the binding sending Alice to 𝑝, so its
class is enabled at every later configuration and never taken, and the run is not live. So 𝐶𝑀    ′ does

not restrict 𝐶𝑀 .                                                                                     □
   Lemma 44 (Stronger body). Let the body of 𝜏 ′ be stronger than that of 𝜏, every role of 𝜏 ′ that is
not one of 𝜏 an agent role, and every binding admissible for 𝜏 ′ admissible for 𝜏. If 𝜏 ′ determines 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′
at an admissible binding 𝛽, then 𝛽 is admissible for 𝜏, 𝜏 determines at 𝛽 the restriction of 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ to
the agents in the extents in 𝑑 of the roles of 𝜏, and every other agent 𝑝 of 𝑑 has 𝑑𝑝′ = 𝑑𝑝 .
   Proof. Write 𝑄 and 𝑄 ′ for the agents in the extents in 𝑑 of the roles of 𝜏 and of 𝜏 ′ . A listed
role of 𝜏 is one of 𝜏 ′ , written 𝜋 [𝐴] with the same anchor, so it has the same extent in 𝑑 for both
(Definition 6), and 𝑄 ⊆ 𝑄 ′ . Restricted to the variables of 𝜏, 𝛽 is a binding for 𝜏, the agent roles of
𝜏 being agent roles of 𝜏 ′ and the variables of 𝜏 roles or variables of 𝜏 ′ . It is admissible for 𝜏, by
hypothesis. The name terms occurring in no atom required at a role are the same for both, so the
condition of Definition 7 on fresh names is the same; and the body of 𝜏 ′ holds at 𝑑 and 𝛽, so that of
𝜏 does (Definition 43). A role of 𝜏 ′ that is not one of 𝜏 holds no atom of the head, which is that of 𝜏,
70                                               James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


since an agent variable holding an atom of 𝜏 is a role of 𝜏; so it adds, deletes and clears nothing. The
atoms added, deleted and cleared, the conditional literals among them, are read off the common
head at instances fixed by 𝑑 and 𝛽, so for 𝑝 ∈ 𝑄 the joins +𝑝 and −𝑝 and the set 𝐾𝑝 are the same for
both, the condition +𝑝 ∩ (−𝑝 ⊎ 𝐾𝑝 ) = ∅ holds for 𝜏 as for 𝜏 ′ , and 𝑑𝑝′ is the same; and an agent 𝑝 of
𝑄 ′ \ 𝑄 is in the extents of such roles only, so 𝑑𝑝′ = 𝑑𝑝 . So 𝑑 and 𝑑 ′ differ on 𝑄, and 𝜏 determines
their restriction to 𝑄 at 𝛽.                                                                           □
   Proposition 45 (Syntactic restrictions). Let 𝐶 and 𝐶 ′ have traceable provenance and satisfy
volition, with Σ′ ⊆ Σ. 𝐶 ′ restricts 𝐶 if it is obtained from 𝐶 by one of the following, and, restriction
being transitive, by a sequence of them:
(a) replacing a schema 𝜏 with a guarding agent role by a schema 𝜏 ′ with its name and guards and a
    stronger body, every role of 𝜏 ′ that is not one of 𝜏 an agent role that is a reading role, or deleting
    such a schema, 𝐶 and 𝐶 ′ having simple unguarded classes;
(b) adding a schema with no guarding role, with the head and the roles of a schema 𝜏 of 𝐶 and a body
    stronger than that of 𝜏;
(c) replacing a schema 𝜏 with no guarding agent role by a schema 𝜏 ′ with its name, roles and guards
    and a stronger body, where at every configuration of every correct run of the protocol realising
    𝐶 ′ and at every admissible binding the body of 𝜏 ′ holds wherever that of 𝜏 does, 𝐶 and 𝐶 ′ having
    simple unguarded classes;
(d) replacing a schema 𝜏 with a guarding agent role by a schema 𝜏 ′ with its name and a stronger body,
    whose guarding roles are those of 𝜏 and agent roles that are no roles of 𝜏, every other role of 𝜏 ′ that
    is not one of 𝜏 an agent role that is a reading role, and every binding admissible for 𝜏 ′ admissible
    for 𝜏, 𝐶 and 𝐶 ′ having simple unguarded classes.
   Proof. Restriction is transitive, composing the runs that two restrictions give, since including a
sequence in order is transitive; so it suffices to treat one step. Write F and F ′ for the protocols
realising 𝐶 and 𝐶 ′ , let 𝑃 ⊂ Π, and let 𝑟 ′ be a correct run of F ′ (𝑃).
The run. Let (𝑡 ′, 𝐺 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 ′ be determined by a schema 𝜌 ′ of 𝐶 ′ at an admissible binding 𝛽. If 𝜌 ′ is
a schema of 𝐶, put 𝑡ˆ′ := 𝑡 ′ . Otherwise 𝜌 ′ is the 𝜏 ′ of (a), (c) or (d) or the schema (b) adds, which
meet the hypotheses of Lemma 44, a binding admissible for 𝜏 ′ being admissible for 𝜏 in (a), (b) and
(c) as every guarding role of 𝜏 ′ is one of 𝜏 and the two have the same head and the same speech-act
variables in the atoms they require, and 𝑡ˆ′ is the restriction of 𝑡 ′ to the agents in the extents of
the roles of 𝜏, which 𝜏 determines at 𝛽, admissible for it. Let 𝐺ˆ ′ be the agents in the extents of the
guarding roles of the schema of 𝐶 determining 𝑡ˆ′ , so that (𝑡ˆ′, 𝐺ˆ ′ ) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 , and 𝐺ˆ ′ = 𝐺 ′ but in (b) and
in (d), where 𝐺ˆ ′ ⊆ 𝐺 ′ . The agents of 𝑡 ′ that 𝑡ˆ′ lacks are stationary in 𝑡 ′ , so the 𝑃-closure of 𝑡 ′ is
contained in that of 𝑡ˆ′ (Definition 62). Let 𝑟 start at the initial configuration of F (𝑃), and, for each
volitional machine transaction of 𝑟 ′ , with one (𝑡 ′, 𝐺 ′ ) inducing it chosen, take a volition change of
each agent of 𝐺ˆ ′ adding [𝑡ˆ′ ], its class in 𝐶, and then the volitional machine transaction of F (𝑃)
induced by (𝑡ˆ′, 𝐺ˆ ′ ) between the machine configurations of that step of 𝑟 ′ , which removes [𝑡ˆ′ ] from
every person (Definition 58); it is one, as (𝑡ˆ′, 𝐺ˆ ′ ) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 (𝑃), 𝑅𝐶 being contained (Lemma 18) and
𝑆𝐶 ′ (𝑃) ⊆ 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) as Σ′ ⊆ Σ. Where 𝑟 ′ has finitely many volitional machine transactions, 𝑟 ends after
the last. So 𝑟 is a safe run of F (𝑃) whose machine configurations, repetitions removed, are those
of 𝑟 ′ , and in which a person wills a class only from the volition change before its transaction until
that transaction, which removes it.
Liveness. Suppose a class 𝑋 of 𝐶 is enabled at every configuration of a suffix of 𝑟 and no member
of it occurs there. The suffix holds a configuration at which no person wills anything; from the
first such, after the 𝑘th volitional machine transaction of 𝑟 , each of its machine configurations
is that of such a configuration, at which 𝑋 is enabled only by an unguarded transaction. So at
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                        71


each of these machine configurations 𝑑 some (𝑢𝑑 , ∅) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 with 𝑢𝑑 ∈ 𝑋 is machine-enabled, and
every configuration of the suffix of 𝑟 ′ after its 𝑘th volitional machine transaction has one of them.
Below we find a class 𝑌 of 𝐶 ′ such that (i) at each such 𝑑 some (𝑢𝑑′ , ∅) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 ′ with 𝑢𝑑′ ∈ 𝑌 is
machine-enabled, and (ii) 𝑡ˆ′ ∈ 𝑋 for every (𝑡 ′, 𝐺 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 ′ with 𝑡 ′ ∈ 𝑌 . By (i) 𝑌 is enabled at every
configuration of the suffix of 𝑟 ′ , and 𝑟 ′ is live, so a transition of that suffix is induced by some
 (𝑦, 𝐺) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 ′ with 𝑦 ∈ 𝑌 . The (𝑡 ′, 𝐺 ′ ) chosen for that transition has 𝑡 ′ ∈ 𝑌 : the transition removes
 [𝑦] and [𝑡 ′ ] from every person and changes no other volitional state (Definition 58), so if the
two classes differ neither is held by anyone before it, 𝐺 and 𝐺 ′ are empty, and 𝑦 and 𝑡 ′ underlie
unguarded transactions whose 𝑃-closures share the transition, so are of one class by Lemma 17 for
𝐶 ′ . So the corresponding transition of 𝑟 , after its 𝑘th volitional machine transaction, is induced by
 (𝑡ˆ′, 𝐺ˆ ′ ) with 𝑡ˆ′ ∈ 𝑋 by (ii), a member of 𝑋 , which contradicts the supposition.
     It remains to find 𝑌 . In (b) take 𝑌 := 𝑋 . The machine transactions the added schema determines
at a binding are among those 𝜏 determines there (Lemma 44), and 𝐶 ′ keeps every schema of 𝐶, so
the relation generating ∼ has the same classes in 𝐶 ′ as in 𝐶, 𝑋 is a class of 𝐶 ′ , and each (𝑢𝑑 , ∅) is in
𝑅𝐶 ′ , which gives (i); and 𝑡ˆ′ = 𝑡 ′ for every 𝑡 ′ , the added schema having the roles of 𝜏, which gives (ii).
     In (a), (c) and (d), 𝐶 has simple unguarded classes, so 𝑋 is the set 𝑇 of machine transactions that
one schema 𝜎 of 𝐶 with no guarding agent role determines at one admissible binding 𝛽, and no
other schema or binding determines a member of it. A schema that (a) or (d) replaces or deletes has
a guarding agent role, so 𝜎 is a schema of 𝐶 ′ or the 𝜏 that (c) replaces; let 𝜎 ′ be 𝜎 in the first case
and 𝜏 ′ in the second, and 𝑇 ′ the set of machine transactions 𝜎 ′ determines at 𝛽, which is admissible
for it, the two having the same head, guards and speech-act variables in the atoms they require.
Each 𝑢𝑑 is in 𝑇 ′ : in the first case 𝑇 ′ = 𝑇 ; in the second 𝜏 determines 𝑢𝑑 at 𝛽, so its body holds at 𝑑
and 𝛽, 𝑑 is a machine configuration of the correct run 𝑟 ′ of F ′ (𝑃), so the body of 𝜏 ′ holds there
by (c), and 𝜏 ′ , with the head and the roles of 𝜏, determines 𝑢𝑑 at 𝛽, as in the proof of Lemma 44.
The guards of 𝜎 ′ at 𝛽 are those of 𝜎, so (𝑢𝑑 , ∅) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 ′ ; let 𝑌 be the class of 𝐶 ′ holding 𝑇 ′ , which
gives (i). 𝐶 ′ has simple unguarded classes and 𝑌 holds an unguarded transaction, so 𝑌 = 𝑇 ′ and
no other schema or binding of 𝐶 ′ determines a member of it; so every (𝑡 ′, 𝐺 ′ ) ∈ 𝑅𝐶 ′ with 𝑡 ′ ∈ 𝑌 is
determined by 𝜎 ′ at 𝛽, 𝑡ˆ′ = 𝑡 ′ , 𝜎 ′ having the roles of 𝜎, and 𝜎 determines it at 𝛽 (Lemma 44), so
𝑡ˆ′ ∈ 𝑇 = 𝑋 , which gives (ii).                                                                             □
   Theorem 46 (What the check preserves). Let 𝐶 be syntactically grassroots.
(1) If 𝐶 ′ amends 𝐶, then 𝐶 ′ is syntactically grassroots if and only if 𝐶 ′ has an unobstructed introductory
    act, every atom an added schema adds meets Definition 11 in 𝐶 ′ with 𝐸 the predicates of Σ′ other
    than environment predicates, every added schema meets Definition 13 in 𝐶 ′ , and every kept schema
    with no guarding role and a reading role has signature atoms in 𝐶 ′ .
(2) If 𝐶 ′ is obtained from 𝐶 by deleting schemas and by replacing schemas 𝜏 by schemas 𝜏 ′ with the
    name and the head of 𝜏 and a stronger body, the guarding roles of 𝜏 ′ those of 𝜏 and agent roles
    that are no roles of 𝜏, every other role of 𝜏 ′ that is not one of 𝜏 an agent role that is a reading role
    and 𝜏 then having a guarding agent role, then 𝐶 ′ is syntactically grassroots if and only if 𝐶 ′ has
    an unobstructed introductory act and every such added reading role has an edge to a guarding role
    from an atom that guarding role requires.
(3) If 𝐶 ′ instantiates 𝐶, then 𝐶 ′ is syntactically grassroots.
(4) The lift of 𝐶 at any set of its predicates is syntactically grassroots; so 𝐶 ′ extending 𝐶 at it is
    syntactically grassroots if and only if it meets the conditions of clause 1 as an amendment of that
    lift deleting nothing.
(5) Clauses 1 to 3 hold with ‘has an introduction with wards, traceable provenance and volition’
    (Section 8) for ‘is syntactically grassroots’, of 𝐶 and of 𝐶 ′ , and ‘an introduction with wards’ for ‘an
    unobstructed introductory act’.
72                                             James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


    Proof. Write 𝐸 and 𝐸 ′ for the predicates of Σ and of Σ′ other than environment predicates, 𝐷
for the schemas an amendment deletes, 𝐴 for those it adds and 𝐾 := 𝐶 \ 𝐷 for those it keeps. Two
facts are used. First, Definition 11 for an atom 𝜑 a schema 𝜏 adds asks of each argument of 𝜑 that
its base be that of a role of 𝜏 or of an argument of an atom 𝜓 ∈ =( 𝑗) ∩ Φ𝐸 required at an agent role
of 𝜏, or be named by a safe formula of its body through 𝐸, and the like of its speech-act variables;
it concerns 𝜏 alone and the set 𝐸, and holds of 𝜏 with a larger set or a larger body if it holds with
a smaller. Second, Definition 13 for a schema 𝜏 asks of its role graph, whose edges come from
atoms 𝜏 requires of traceable provenance, of its reading roles, and, where 𝜏 has no guarding role
and a reading role, of signature atoms, whose last clause alone mentions the other schemas of the
contract.
    (1) Let 𝐶 ′ = 𝐾 ∪ 𝐴 amend 𝐶. If 𝐶 ′ is syntactically grassroots the four conditions hold by
Definition 14, the second and third being instances of Definitions 11 and 13 for 𝐶 ′ and the fourth
part of Definition 13. Conversely, let the four hold. Every predicate of 𝐸 ′ occurs in a schema of
𝐶 ′ . 𝐸 ′ has traceable provenance in 𝐶 ′ : an atom added by a schema of 𝐴 meets the condition by
hypothesis; one added by a schema 𝜏 of 𝐾 meets it in 𝐶 with 𝐸, through some 𝜓 required at an
agent role of 𝜏, whose predicate occurs in 𝜏 and so is in 𝐸 ′ , hence it meets it in 𝐶 ′ with 𝐸 ′ , by the
first fact. Every schema of 𝐶 ′ satisfies volition in 𝐶 ′ : one of 𝐴 by hypothesis; one of 𝐾 has in 𝐶 ′
every edge it has in 𝐶, its required atoms being of traceable provenance in 𝐶 ′ as all of 𝐸 ′ is, so its
role graph is connected or has a guarding role in each component as in 𝐶, its reading roles have
their edges as in 𝐶, and where it has no guarding role and a reading role it has signature atoms in
𝐶 ′ by hypothesis. With an unobstructed introductory act, 𝐶 ′ is syntactically grassroots.
    (2) Let 𝐶 ′ be so obtained, with 𝐴 the schemas 𝜏 ′ and 𝐷 the schemas they replace and those deleted.
If 𝐶 ′ is syntactically grassroots it has an unobstructed introductory act, and an added reading role
has the edge Definition 13 asks of a reading role of a schema with a guarding role. Conversely, let
both hold; by (1) it remains to show the other three conditions. An atom 𝜏 ′ adds is one 𝜏 adds, and
in 𝐶 the condition of Definition 11 holds of it through roles of 𝜏, which are roles of 𝜏 ′ , and through
atoms 𝜓 required at agent roles of 𝜏, which 𝜏 ′ requires there (Definition 43) and whose predicates
are in 𝐸 ′ , and through safe formulas of the body of 𝜏, which the body of 𝜏 ′ holds (Definition 43);
a speech-act variable occurring in no atom required at a role of 𝜏 occurs in none of 𝜏 ′ , the two
having the same such variables; so the condition holds in 𝐶 ′ with 𝐸 ′ . The role graph of 𝜏 ′ has every
edge of that of 𝜏, since 𝜏 ′ requires every atom 𝜏 requires and all of 𝐸 ′ has traceable provenance in
𝐶 ′ , and each added reading role has an edge to a guarding role. So every connected component of it
holds a guarding agent role: one holding a role of 𝜏 holds a connected component of the role graph
of 𝜏, which has a guarding agent role where that graph is not connected and is all of it where it is,
𝜏 having a guarding agent role; and one holding added roles only holds an added guarding role, or
an added reading role with its edge to a guarding role. The guarding roles of 𝜏 ′ are those of 𝜏 and
the added ones, and its reading roles are those of 𝜏, the heads being the same, with their edges, and
the added ones. A schema of 𝐶 ′ with no guarding role is one of 𝐶 or replaces one with its head and
roles, so the schemas of 𝐶 ′ with no guarding role add, delete and clear what a subset of those of 𝐶
do, and signature atoms of such a schema in 𝐶 are signature atoms of it, or of its replacement, in 𝐶 ′ .
    (3) An instantiation writes, for each parameter instantiated, a predicate, a value or an utterance
not occurring in 𝐶, a different one for each, so it is a bijection between the atoms of 𝐶 and those of
𝐶 ′ and between 𝐸 and 𝐸 ′ that keeps every argument and its kind, and adds to an atom of a predicate
written with further value arguments a value variable for each, shared by the atoms over the same
agent variables (Definition 38), a wildcard among them being a variable of its own, or universally
quantified where the atom is negated (Definition 3). Definitions 9, 10, 11, 12 and 13 mention a
predicate only to compare the predicates of two atoms and to form the sets Φ𝐸 , and an argument
only by its kind and its base, a value and an utterance having none, so each holds of 𝐶 ′ exactly
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                   73


when it holds of 𝐶, an added argument being read as the proof of clause 4 reads the argument a lift
adds: an introduction of 𝐶 ′ is one of 𝐶 with any values for the added variables of its act; an atom it
requires at a role is added by the instance of the schema of arity one that adds the atom of 𝐶, at the
binding sending that schema’s added variables, which no atom it requires binds, to those values;
and a schema of 𝐶 ′ adding at some binding an atom the introductory act forbids is the instance of
a schema of 𝐶 adding at that binding, without the values, the atom of 𝐶 it forbids.
   (4) Let 𝐿 be the lift of 𝐶 at 𝐹 . An added argument is a value variable, which has no base, so
Definitions 11, 12 and 13 read each schema of 𝐿 as they read the schema of 𝐶 it lifts, and a schema of
𝐿 adds, deletes or clears an atom of a predicate exactly where its schema of 𝐶 does. An introductory
act of 𝐶 lifts to one of 𝐿, with no constraint, its introduction choosing for the fresh variable any
value (Appendix C). Clause 1 of Definition 10 holds of the lift: an atom it requires at a role, with
the chosen value 𝑣, is added by the lift of the schema of arity one that adds the atom of 𝐶, at the
binding sending its fresh variable to 𝑣; clause 2 holds, a schema of 𝐿 adding at some binding an
atom the introductory act forbids being the lift of a schema of 𝐶 adding at the binding without
the value the atom of 𝐶 it forbids. So 𝐿 is syntactically grassroots, and the rest is clause 1 with 𝐶
replaced by 𝐿.
   (5) Clauses 1 and 2 use of the introductory act only that 𝐶 ′ has one, and their proofs of traceable
provenance and volition stand, both being conditions on each schema given the predicates of the
contract. For clause 3, Definitions 31, 32, 33, 34 and 35 mention a predicate only to compare the
predicates of two atoms and to say where the schemas add, delete, clear, require and forbid its
atoms, and an argument only by its kind, which the bijection keeps; an added argument is a value
of a predicate that is not a mark, so Definition 31 reads the marks of 𝐶 ′ as those of 𝐶, and the two
records of one guardianship, over the same two agents, share it, as clauses 3 and 4 of Definition 32
and clause 2 of Definition 34, which admit value arguments, ask; so each holds of 𝐶 ′ exactly when
it holds of 𝐶.                                                                                       □

B.7   Clauses and Their Modalities
  Proposition 51 (Witnessing). A schema is witnessed against every agent role it names at another
agent role.

   Proof. Let 𝜏 name 𝜋 𝑗 at 𝜋𝑖 by an atom 𝜑 added at 𝜋𝑖 , let 𝛽 be a binding for 𝜏, and put 𝑎 := 𝛽 (𝜋 𝑗 )
and 𝑏 := 𝛽 (𝜋𝑖 ), which differ by Definition 5. Let 𝑑 → 𝑑 ′ be determined by 𝜏 at 𝛽. By Definition 7 𝑑𝑏′
is 𝑑𝑏 less the atoms deleted or cleared at the roles sent to 𝑏 and plus those added there, no atom
added being deleted or cleared, so 𝑑𝑏′ \ 𝑑𝑏 is the join of the atoms added at those roles and holds
𝛽 (𝜑), an atom naming 𝑎.                                                                              □



  Proposition 52 (Enforcement by guarding). If 𝑒 is guarded in 𝐶, the consent clause for 𝑒 is
enforced by 𝐶.

   Proof. Let 𝑟 be a finite safe run of the protocol realising 𝐶 and let an 𝑒-atom occur in the state
of an agent at some configuration of 𝑟 . The initial state is empty, so some transition of 𝑟 added it,
and by Definitions 16 and 7 that atom is 𝛽 (𝜑) for a binding 𝛽 of a schema 𝜏 of 𝐶 and some 𝜑 ∈ +(𝑖)
with predicate 𝑒. By hypothesis every role 𝜑 names is a guarding role of 𝜏, so every agent the atom
names is 𝛽 of a guarding role and hence in the guard of the transaction taken; by Definition 64
each held its class in its volitional state at that point, which is to say willed the act.         □
74                                             James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


B.8    Signing the Contract
  Proposition 54 (Every participant has presented its undertaking). In a presenting run,
every agent of an instance of two or more agents has presented their undertaking to another agent of
that instance.
   Proof. An instance is a connected component of the interaction graph of the run (Definition 69),
so an agent of an instance of two or more agents has an edge to another agent 𝑞 of it, put there by
an interaction between the two; the run is presenting, so the agent has presented their undertaking
to 𝑞.                                                                                             □

C     VALUES IN SPEECH-ACT SCHEMAS
Speech-act schemas allow values of any type (Section 3.2). This appendix defines them and extends
states and bindings to them; the compilation and the conditions then apply as stated.
  Definition 89 (Schema with values). Fix a countable set 𝑉 of values, disjoint from Π, 𝐷 and N ,
and a set 𝑍 of value variables, disjoint from 𝑅, 𝑋 and 𝑌 . A schema with values is written
                                   𝜏:    𝐻 ← 𝐵, 𝛾      guarded by 𝐺
as in Definition 3, except that its variables may be value variables in 𝑍 ; an argument of an atom
may be a value or a value variable, or the item item(𝑦) of a speech-act variable 𝑦 of the schema
that an agent role requires, the utterance under the signatures of 𝑦, which names no agent; 𝛾,
its constraint, is a decidable condition on the values assigned to its value variables, written in
the relations and functions the types of the values provide, as the order and the arithmetic of
numbers and the distance of points of the plane, combined by the connectives, together with signing
conditions signed(𝑢, 𝑦) and unsigned(𝑢, 𝑦), 𝑢 an agent role or agent variable and 𝑦 a speech-act
variable of the schema, the author author(𝑦), the first signer of 𝑦, in an equality with an agent role
or agent variable, the item item(𝑦), the utterance under the signatures of 𝑦, in an equality with
another, and a decidable relation among utterances and values that their types provide, as that an
utterance is a grassroots contract with its schemas and pragmas, or that bylaws admit an act given
the votes cast, and equalities and inequalities 𝑢 = 𝑣 and 𝑢 ≠ 𝑣 of two agent variables, two speech-
act variables or two name terms, and, as a whole number, the cardinality cardinality(𝑥,          ¯ 𝐴 ∧ 𝜑)
of an anchored safe formula, the number of instances of its anchor 𝐴 at which 𝜑 holds, each
counted as many times as its multiplicity, binding 𝑥¯ as the anchored quantifiers of Definition 2
do, as cardinality(𝑥, points(Alice, 𝑥)) < 𝑑, Alice pointing to fewer than 𝑑 persons, which makes
the body a formula of first-order logic with counting [52], and, as a value of a numeric type, the
sum sum(𝑥,       ¯ 𝐴 ∧ 𝜑, 𝑢) of a numeric term 𝑢 over the instances of its anchor 𝐴 at which 𝜑 holds, each
taken as many times as its multiplicity, binding 𝑥¯ as cardinality does; a quantified conjunct carries
constraints of the same kind on its variables and those of the schema; an introductory act and a
schema of arity one admitted by clause 1 of Definition 10 carry no constraint, since whether some
binding satisfies a constraint is undecidable in general; and an atom of 𝐻 or of 𝐵 with no wildcard
may carry a multiplicity 𝜅, a positive whole number or a value variable, written 𝜅 · 𝜑, 𝜅 copies of 𝜑,
which in 𝐵 are required, or forbidden when negated, the machine then holding fewer than 𝜅, and in
𝐻 added or deleted; where an atom is required with multiplicity 𝜅 and deleted with multiplicities
𝜅 1, . . . , 𝜅𝑛 , the schema’s constraint implies 𝜅 1 + · · · + 𝜅𝑛 ≤ 𝜅, so that −(𝑖) ⊆ =(𝑖) holds at every
binding; the checker decides the implication where the constraint is linear over whole numbers,
and refuses the schema otherwise, the condition being sufficient.
  A speech-act variable that takes a text a person enters may carry a type too, naming the kind of
the text, as an email address or a person’s name, written 𝑦:𝑇 at one of its occurrences. The type
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                75


constrains nothing: a binding, the three conditions and the compilation read 𝑦:𝑇 as 𝑦, and the app
built from the contract reads the type for the field that takes the text.

  Definition 90 (Binding with values). A binding 𝛽 for a schema with values 𝜏 is a binding for 𝜏
(Definition 5) that also maps each value variable of 𝜏 to a value, under which 𝛾 holds, signed(𝑢, 𝑦)
holding when 𝛽 (𝑢) is the signer of 𝛽 (𝑦) or of a speech act inside it and unsigned(𝑢, 𝑦) otherwise,
author(𝑦) = 𝑢 when 𝛽 (𝑢) is the innermost signer of 𝛽 (𝑦), item(𝑦) = item(𝑦 ′ ) when 𝛽 (𝑦) and 𝛽 (𝑦 ′ )
carry one utterance, and 𝑢 = 𝑣 when 𝛽 (𝑢) = 𝛽 (𝑣), and every multiplicity of 𝜏 is a positive whole
number; it maps a value to itself, and 𝜅 · 𝜑 to the multiset holding 𝛽 (𝜑) with multiplicity 𝛽 (𝜅).

   A ground atom may have values and utterances among its arguments: 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) is the set of finite
multisets over Σ[𝑃 ∪ 𝐷 𝑃 ∪ N𝑃 ∪ 𝑉 ∪ Δ], and a permutation of Π is the identity on values and
on utterances; a binding sends item(𝑦) to the utterance 𝛽 (𝑦) carries, so an item holds the same
program, or the same post, whoever signed it over. Which types the values have — numbers, strings,
dates — is the realisation’s; the contract is parametric in them, and a value variable of a schema has
a type, which the schema’s term form writes, so that the same clauses are realised over whole or
over fractional amounts by the schemas’ choice. A multiplicity counts copies of an atom, so it is a
positive whole number in every contract: paying zero coins is no act. In the examples of this paper
coins are whole, an amount a multiplicity: GC’s payment pays 𝑘 coins, −𝑘 · ¢(Bob) at Alice and
+𝑘 · ¢(Bob) at Bob; the bonds of [81] number dates by days, the successor of a date being the next
day. A binding chooses its values, so an introduction (Definition 9) chooses its own.
   Definition 7 applies to a schema with values as written, the multisets of a role at a binding
carrying each atom as many times as its multiplicity says. Definition 3 requires that some role add
or delete an atom, and a multiplicity is at least one, so this holds at every binding. A value, and an
utterance, is no agent role, no agent variable and no name term, so it has no base, Definitions 11
and 13 do not concern it, and no state records an agent by it (Definition 8). The conditions of
Section 3.8 therefore read a schema with values as they read one without, they stay decidable,
no condition reading a constraint, which the realisation evaluates when it binds the schema, and
Theorem 15 and its proof stand; a signing condition and an equality name no agent a binding does
not, so Lemma 18 and Proposition 20 stand, a permutation carrying signers with it and being a
bijection on agents, speech acts and names.
Environment predicates. A contract may name a set of environment predicates in Σ, whose atoms
are over 𝑉 and over agents, as nearby(Bob) in the state of Alice, which the platform keeps current
from the persons its networking layer reports nearby: the machine holds them from startup, in place
of the empty initial state, and the platform keeps them current, as it keeps today(𝑡) at the calendar’s
date; no schema adds or deletes one, and a schema may require or forbid one. The platform’s update
of an environment atom is a unary transition of the machine holding it, in no class, so it carries
no liveness obligation and is no interaction; in a run over 𝑃 an environment atom names only
agents of 𝑃, and any update naming them may occur, so the states stay in 𝑆𝐶 (𝑃) and Proposition 20
stands. An environment predicate has no traceable provenance (Definition 11): Provenance traces
through none of its atoms, Volition takes no edge of a role graph from one, and Lemma 83 and
Proposition 29 read the set 𝐸 of the other predicates, so an environment atom naming an agent
in another instance puts an edge in the recording graph and none in the 𝐸-recording graph, and
they stand, as do Lemmas 27 and 18, the latter reading only atoms a schema adds; an unobstructed
introductory act requires no environment atom, clause 1 of Definition 10 admitting only atoms a
schema adds, and forbids none, and the proof of Proposition 28 stands. The bonds of [81] name
today, which their first act requires.
76                                                   James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon

D      RELATED WORK, EXPANDED
The paragraphs of Section 12, expanded.
Contracts as formal objects. The formal study of contracts in this field is dominated by the
contract-as-automaton family. Contract automata [5] give the deontic modalities an operational
semantics over the acts of named interactive parties; Flood and Goodenough [24] render a loan
agreement as a deterministic finite automaton whose states are performing, delinquent and default
and whose transitions are drawn from an alphabet of events; the L4 domain-specific language [93]
has an executable semantics in which deontic clauses with deadlines become states and transitions;
and timed contract automata [13] add quantitative time. Rule-based encodings represent contract
clauses as defeasible deontic rules with violation and reparation chains [36], and imperative and
declarative smart contracts running on a distributed ledger have been compared [35].
   Three commitments are common to all of these, and this paper makes none of them. The parties
are fixed when the contract is formed, so the object of study is one instance among a known set
of parties, where our conditions quantify over the family of systems a contract induces on every
finite set of people. There is one contract state, the automaton’s state, the rule base’s facts or the
ledger, where the definition of records, and therefore of what it is for two agents to be connected,
needs a local state per agent and a local-states function monotone in the set of people. And each
requires something closure forbids: an alphabet of events every party is assumed to observe alike,
a clock, or a ledger.
   Two languages outside this venue are the nearest to ours. Symboleo [63, 85] specifies a legal
contract as a set of obligations and powers over an ontology of roles, assets and events, with a
semantics of logical axioms on statecharts, and properties verified in temporal logic; its purpose
is monitoring, and roles are assigned to parties during each contract execution. Stipula [16] is a
domain-specific language with legal constructs, agreement, permissions, obligations and violations,
whose contracts are state machines over parties, fields and linear assets; its agreement operator is the
contract’s constructor, fixing the full list of parties, and thereafter each function is invoked by one
named party. Catala [56] does the corresponding work for statute rather than contract, encoding the
base-case and exception structure of legislation in default logic. Both contract languages make the
three commitments above: a Stipula configuration carries a global clock, and Symboleo presupposes
a monitor, which is a participant that is not a party.
   A concrete case is befriending, which cannot be written in Stipula: it is an act that two agents
must both will, available repeatedly between any two of them, and Stipula allows joint consent
Grassroots Contracts                                                                                     77


only in the constructor, so each befriending would be the formation of a new contract rather than
a clause of a standing one.
   We claim no new language. The conditions of Section 7 are on the four things a schema carries.
Stipula’s function declarations name an invoking party and a precondition, and Symboleo attributes
obligations to a debtor and a creditor, so the four could be extracted from either given a semantics
in which each party holds its own state.
   BSPL [87] is the protocol language of multiagent systems nearest to ours. A protocol is a set of
message schemas between roles, each parameter adorned in or out, and a role may send a message
once it knows the bindings of the message’s in parameters and does not know those of its out
parameters. There is no global state: the local state of a role is the history of the messages it sent
and received, and the enactments of a protocol are its tuples of parameter bindings, a key keeping
them unique. The meaning of a message, the commitments it creates among them, is a layer above.
A speech-act schema is the counterpart of a message schema in which an act may take any number
of roles, each consenting by its guard, and changes the local state of each. BSPL’s well-formedness
conditions make a protocol enactable. The conditions of Section 7 concern the family of systems a
contract induces on every finite set of people, and the coalescence of two of them.
Dynamic and temporal logic. In first-order dynamic logic [42] a state is a valuation of the
program’s variables over a fixed first-order structure, among them array variables, an 𝑛-ary array
𝑓 assigned by 𝑓 (𝑡 1, . . . , 𝑡𝑛 ) := 𝑡; a program is built from assignments and tests by sequence,
nondeterministic choice and iteration, with the wildcard assignment 𝑥 :=? choosing any element
of the domain; a program denotes a relation on states, its input/output pairs; and a formula is a
first-order formula closed under [𝛼]𝜑, that every halting run of 𝛼 ends in a state satisfying 𝜑. A
speech-act schema 𝐻 ← 𝐵 guarded by 𝐺 is the program 𝑥 1 :=?; · · · ; 𝑥𝑚 :=?; 𝐵 ◦ ?; 𝐺 ◦ ?; 𝐻 ◦ ; 𝑊 ◦ :
each predicate of arity 𝑛 is an array of arity 𝑛 + 1 with values in the natural numbers, 𝑒 (𝑝, 𝑢)   ¯ =𝑚
saying that the machine of 𝑝 holds 𝑒 (𝑢)  ¯ with multiplicity 𝑚, and a person’s will an array wills(𝑝, [𝑡]);
𝐵 ◦ tests the body, 𝑒 (𝑢)
                       ¯ ≥ 𝜅 for an atom and 𝑒 (𝑢)¯ = 0 for a negated one; 𝐺 ◦ tests wills(𝑎, [𝑡]) = 1 for
                  ◦
each guard 𝑎; 𝐻 adds and subtracts multiplicities as the head says, a cleared atom and a quantified
conjunct by a while loop with a wildcard assignment; and 𝑊 ◦ sets wills(𝑎, [𝑡]) := 0 at every agent.
Its input/output relation is the denotation of Definition 7, and the Kripke frame of these programs
is the transition system of Section 3.3. A volition change is the person’s own program. What
dynamic logic does not state is a property of runs: liveness, that a class enabled from some point
on is eventually taken, is Manna and Pnueli’s justice [55], a formula of linear temporal logic [64];
interactivity, that from every reachable state some continuation interacts, is a formula of branching
time, CTL∗ [19], over the transition system of a set of agents; and process logic [41] interprets
programs and formulas alike over traces, joining the two. Obliviousness relates the systems of two
disjoint sets of agents to that of their union, which no formula over one system states, and persons
with wills are in none of the three; both are [51]’s, and the paper takes its semantics from there.
Inheritance, refinement and generics. Section 9’s relations each have a counterpart. Extension
is subclassing, a class adding methods to its parent’s, and an amendment that rewrites a clause
is overriding. Behavioural subtyping [54] requires of a subtype that every property proved of the
supertype’s objects hold of its own, and design by contract [57] lets a redefined method weaken its
precondition and strengthen its postcondition, so that a client of the parent is served by the child;
the refinement calculus [6] orders programs the same way, a refinement terminating wherever
its original does and choosing among fewer outcomes, and the B method [2] develops a program
from a specification by such refinements. The syntactic approximations of a restriction strengthen
preconditions, which all of these forbid, since a client who could act under the original may be
unable to under the restriction; restriction is CSP’s refinement [44], where a process refines another
78                                            James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


when its traces are among the other’s, every correct run of the restriction being, on the machines,
one of the original (Definition 41). Instantiation is parametric polymorphism [58], a contract generic
in a predicate as a program is in a type, checked once for all its instances; the types of Grassroots
Logic Programs [84] are the realisation’s. Z’s schema calculus [88] builds a specification by including
schemas in schemas, which is extension, and conjoining them, which is composition. In algebraic
specification [31] a theory extends another by adding symbols and axioms, the extension being
conservative when it proves nothing new of the old symbols, and a theory morphism carries one
theory’s symbols to another’s with their signatures, which is instantiation; Theorem 46 shows
the check of an amendment confined to what it adds, a conservativeness of the check. Contract
law amends a contract by modifying its terms, supplements it by adding terms, and novates it by
substituting a new contract, with a new party or between the same ones [3, §§89, 280]. None of
these says what a syntactic check preserves, and Theorem 46 does.

Norms and normative systems. The formal study of what a contract obliges uses deontic
and defeasible logic: contracts are represented as defeasible rules with deontic operators and
compliance is checked against them [34]. Normative multi-agent systems concern how norms are
operationalised, detected, and brought into conflict [9]. A volition guard [51] is a condition of
enactment rather than a deontic operator: an act does not occur unless the people it obliges are
willing. Where a clause of the contract describes no act the protocol carries out, Section 10 says
what the realisation does with it: record its breach, or record the undertaking and the party who
gave it.
   Social commitments [86] give the normative concepts one form, a commitment 𝐶 (𝑥, 𝑦, 𝐺, 𝑝) of
a debtor 𝑥 to a creditor 𝑦 in a context group 𝐺 to bring about 𝑝, the context group providing the
norms by which disputes between the two are settled. A commitment is created, satisfied, cancelled,
released, delegated and assigned, and obligations, taboos, conventions and the Hohfeldian relations
are kinds of commitment. The three modalities of Section 10 classify what a realisation does with a
clause, and are not kinds of obligation. An undertaken clause is a commitment in this sense of its
obligor to every other participant, and another agent holds its record; a grassroots contract has no
context group above the agents.

Juridical acts and declarative power. What we call an act is Sartor’s result-declaration, one of
“acts intended to produce legal determinations” [68], and the fullest model of it is Hage’s [40], which
analyses juridical acts as intentional changes in a world of law furnished with entities, facts and
rules. A multi-party act willed by its parties is not new either. Declarative power is “the capacity of
the power-holder of creating normative positions, involving other agents, simply by ‘proclaiming’
such positions” [29], and its authors observe that a declarative power exercised jointly by several
parties, with the consent of each, is a contract [28].
   We differ from that tradition on where the effect of an act comes from. There a proclamation has
effect only if an institution provides for it: “when an agent 𝑗 proclaims 𝐴, 𝑗 brings it about that
𝐴 only if the concerned institution 𝑠 provides for this result” [28]; in norm-governed institutions
“designated agents are empowered to create particular kinds of states of affairs” [45]; counts-as
statements “hold only with respect to a context” [38]; and an artificial institution “presupposes an
agreement on an unambiguous definition of a set of concepts” [25]. A grassroots contract has no
such institution, being subject to no external authority. The effect of an act is therefore the change
it makes in the agents’ own states, and the condition of its effectiveness is their will rather than a
conferred power. A speech-act schema is a result-declaration with the institution taken out: its
precondition is on each participant’s own local state rather than on an institutionally conferred
power, and its guard takes the place of authorisation.
Grassroots Contracts                                                                               79


   An electronic institution [20] is specified by a performative structure, a network of scenes con-
nected by transitions, each scene a protocol over roles whose arcs are labelled with illocution
schemes; by institutional roles, which external agents cannot play; by a role hierarchy and a sep-
aration of duties among roles; and by normative rules that infer an agent’s obligations from its
illocutions. Every agent adopts a role, every interaction takes place in a scene, an institutional
agent at each transition brokers agents between scenes, and the institution keeps for each agent
the obligations its acts incurred. A grassroots contract has no institutional role, and the records of
its acts are kept in the agents’ own states.
Standard-form contracting. A grassroots contract has no drafting party, so there is no party
whose terms are policed and no party from whom disclosure is required; the defects the literature
finds in the arrangement between a person and a proprietor are absent because the proprietor is
absent.
   The End-User Licence Agreement is a contract of adhesion. Radin [66] treats mass-market boil-
erplate as the deletion of rights that the legal system otherwise confers, and as a matter for the
rule of law rather than for consent only. Kim [48] treats the digital instances of the form — the
terms accepted by clicking, browsing, or installing — and what assent means when the act of assent
is a condition of access. Ben-Shahar and Schneider [7] address the remedy most often proposed,
mandated disclosure, and find that it fails: the terms are not read, and requiring more of them is
not read either.
   This literature takes the bilateral form as given; its questions are what may be done within the
form: whether assent was real, which terms a court should refuse, what a drafter must disclose.
Terms a machine can evaluate. Surden [89] separates the terms of a contract a machine can
evaluate from those it cannot; work on smart legal contracts separates the operational parts of an
agreement, which code executes, from the rest, which stays in prose [14]; and in security, Schnei-
der [69] characterises the policies an execution monitor can enforce. Each divides a contract’s terms
in two, by what a realisation can do with them. Section 10 divides clauses in three, distinguishing
a clause no act of the contract can breach from a clause an agent may breach while the contract
records the breach against them. A two-way division has no room for the second, and it is the class
the law needs: a remedy requires a provable breach and an identifiable obligor.
   In the monitoring literature, compliance is computed by monitors that “receive inputs from
trusted observers” [60], and where the timestamps cannot be trusted the monitor is made robust
rather than removed: compliance “is typically computed with respect to timed event traces with
event timestamps assumed to be perfect”, and the remedy offered is a semantics for compliance
when they are not [11]. A monitor is a participant without a role in the act, and closure forbids one.
Here the record is held by the parties themselves, and what makes it evidence is Definition 48: it
names the party answerable and it lies in someone else’s hands.
Code as a regulator. Lessig [49] argued that architecture regulates conduct as law, markets and
norms do, and that in the digital realm the architecture is code. A grassroots contract is not code:
the conditions of Section 7 concern who may take part in an act and whose will is required for it,
rather than what the code makes an agent do, and they are checked on the text rather than on the
code. The digital social contract [12] identifies the contract with code: the contract is a program,
and a party can behave only according to it; the smart contract literature [17] does the same on a
blockchain.
Self-governance without an external authority. Ostrom [62] documented communities that
devise, adopt and enforce their own rules over shared resources, without an owner and without a
state administering them, and identified the conditions under which such arrangements endure.
A grassroots contract makes the same claim in the digital realm, that a community constitutes
80                                            James Golike, Andy Lewis-Pye, Ehud Shapiro, and Nimrod Talmon


itself, and openness is its formal counterpart: any two people can come to record each other by
acts of their own, so any person may start an instance and instances coexist. Commons scholarship
concerns how a resource is governed in common, for physical resources [62] and for knowledge and
networked production [8, 43]; a grassroots contract governs a relationship rather than a resource,
and applies where nothing is held in common.
   Platform regulation binds the operator: the General Data Protection Regulation [22] imposes
duties on the party that processes personal data, and so presupposes it. Where a community’s
platform is constituted from its members’ own devices [74, 76, 77], there is no operator to bind.
Grassroots systems. The notion of a grassroots protocol is due to [74] and was recast in terms
of interleaving and interaction in [51], which also introduced volition-guarded transactions and
volitionally grassroots protocols. Grassroots platforms have been specified for social network-
ing [75] and for personal cryptocurrencies [50, 73, 81]. Secure Scuttlebutt [90] is a peer-to-peer
social network, started in 2014. Each person is identified by a key pair they generate themselves, so
no authority introduces people to the system; each signs every message of their own append-only
log; and a person’s device replicates the logs of the people they follow, and of those these follow, up
to a set distance, so data flows along the social graph, with no consensus and no central directory.
Following is a message in the follower’s log and takes no consent of the one followed, where
befriending under GSG:1 takes the will of both. Its messages are signed and cannot be repudiated,
as GSN’s messages, signed by their senders, cannot. PGP’s Web of Trust [94, 95] connects people
who have never met: every person is an introducer, signing the keys of people they know to vouch
that each key belongs to its owner, and each person chooses whom they trust as an introducer,
with no authority above them; its 1994 guide calls this a grass-roots approach, in which allowing
all users to act as trusted introducers for their friends works better than a centralized key server,
and its authors call it an early peer-to-peer design and arguably one of the first social networks. Its
trust is in a key, and the guide distinguishes trusting a key from trusting the key’s owner. GKK:3’s
introduction, by which Alice makes Carol known to Bob with Bob’s consent, is PGP’s introducer; a
friendship under GSG is trust in the owner, and a breach of an attested clause, of which the contract
holds the evidence, ends it (Section 2.2). Three things are added here. The first is politeness, a
condition on a protocol’s transactions, which Theorem 26 proves sufficient for the protocol to be
grassroots, so no proof about runs is needed. The second is the step before the specification: the
legal text, and decidable conditions on it under which politeness is established from the acts rather
than from the protocol. The third is what a realisation does with a clause of the contract, whether
or not the contract provides an act for it, which depends on the same schemas.
Neighbouring instruments. Four instruments resemble a grassroots contract, and each differs
from it in what it does rather than in how it is drafted. A licence states what one party may do with
something another party is entitled to: the General Public License [27] presupposes the copyright it
licenses, grants freedoms on conditions, and binds a taker because they would otherwise infringe. A
grassroots contract licenses nothing, and its obligations are owed by each agent to every other and
derive from no agent’s prior entitlement, so a person who has never held any right that the others
could infringe is bound as the rest are. Bylaws bind the members of an association symmetrically
and are adopted on joining, which makes them the closest of the four, but they presuppose an
association: an entity with legal personality, organs that act for it, and a membership register. A code
of conduct states standards of behaviour, and its sanction is exclusion administered by whoever runs
the forum, which is the party a grassroots contract does not have; terms of membership presuppose
the same party, in its capacity as the one who admits.
   A Ricardian contract [37] is a single document by which an issuer of a financial instrument offers
a contract to its holders: readable by people and parsable by programs, signed by the issuer with
Grassroots Contracts                                                                             81


the keys that verify the signature included, and identified by its hash, which every transaction
record of the instrument carries, so neither party can change the contract unnoticed. It binds an
issuer to the holders of one instrument. Under GC (Section 2.6) every person issues their own coins,
and each participant signs an undertaking to keep the contract towards all the others (Section 11).
